Science Fiction The Slowlight Accord PDF

The Slowlight Accord

They rationed consciousness. One auditor learned the scarcity was a lie.

T. K. Arven

Part I

Chapter 01 — The Quota Audit

The audit desk hummed at the same pitch it had held for nine years. Vey Corran set her palm to the reader and let the morning queue load across her vision: two hundred and eleven Suspension reviews, each one a citizen whose allocated awake years had run to their edge. She had cleared a hundred and ninety of them before the second bell. The numbers behaved. They always behaved, until one of them did not.

The desk itself rose from the floor in a single cast of pale composite, worn smooth along the lip where ten thousand palms had rested before hers. A thin seam marked the reader panel, and beneath it the slow blue lettering of the queue scrolled without ever quite stopping. Vey had learned the desk the way a child learns a staircase, by the feel of it under her hands before the light ever reached her eyes. The cold of the composite carried up through her sleeves each morning and settled in her wrists, a small anchoring weight she had come to mistake for focus. Around the desk the booth walls held the same blue wash, and the silence there had a texture of its own, the kind that let a single stray sound arrive from far down the level and seem, for a moment, like an event.

Her corner of the Office of the Accord sat three levels below the light shafts, where the rings folded inward and the ceiling carried only the blue wash of duty lamps. She liked it there. The cold kept her clear. A Suspension deck felt weightless and still; the records that described it felt the same way in her hands, clean columns of energy drawn against energy owed. Vey trusted a column more than she trusted a face. A column could not flinch when she asked it a second question.

The work asked for a particular kind of attention, the kind that did not look for trouble. An auditor confirmed that a citizen's draw matched the allocation the Ledger of Hours assigned, logged the confirmation, and moved on. The Office measured a good quarter by how few files returned to the desk. Vey had the best return rate on level nineteen. She took a quiet pride in it, the pride of a person who had learned to let the math close itself.

Vey had come to the Office young, through the ordinary route: a citizen with her allocation spent, a wake-window granted, an aptitude test that found her good with numbers and patient with silence. They had trained her to read the Ledger of Hours the way a gardener reads soil, by touch more than by rule. A healthy column had a feel. It settled. It did not pull at the edge of her attention the way a miscount did. She had learned to trust that pull over the favorable word of any supervisor, because the columns outlasted the supervisors and the columns never lied on purpose.

Her instructors had called that trust a kind of humility, but Vey had always felt it as something simpler and colder. The work asked her to spend her days among the sleeping without ever reaching them, to hold the sum of their kept lives in her hands and set it down again unbroken. She had made a quiet peace with the distance. A citizen reduced to a column could not disappoint her, and she could not disappoint a column. The arrangement had suited them both, she thought, until the morning a column had asked something of her it had no right to ask.

The training had taken two years. Most of it concerned humility before the Accord. The founders had written the Schedule under a failing star, with nine billion lives resting on a count that had to hold for forever. Questioning the count, her instructors said, questioned the only thing that had kept humanity alive. Vey had absorbed the lesson the way the decks absorbed current, without resistance. For nine years the lesson had required nothing of her. The columns had closed. The Strata had held. She had felt no pull.

She opened the next file in the order the Actuary had assigned. The name on the header read TOLL, citizen mark L-4471, level nineteen, Suspended since the close of his third wake-year. Standard case. The Ledger of Hours listed him among the long-sleeping, a body kept and a mind mostly dark, waking only in the narrow windows the Schedule allowed. She pulled his draw record beside his allocation and began the comparison she had performed ten thousand times.

The routine moved through her without thought. Toll's maintenance share, the thin current that held his body against the dark, showed as a steady debit against the collective pool. His allocation, his wake-years granted at birth and spent down to nothing, showed as closed. The two columns should have met at zero, citizen owed nothing, Strata owing him only the small mercy of keeping. Vey typed the reconciling figure and waited for the desk to agree.

It did not.

The Ledger of Hours claimed Toll drew a maintenance share larger than the one his own sensors reported. The discrepancy sat in the fourth decimal of a wake-year, a sliver so small the Office would have rounded it away in any ordinary review. Vey stared at it the way she stared at a misaligned seal: not with alarm, but with the quiet certainty that something had been placed where it did not belong. A Suspended citizen could not draw less than he was owed. The Accord paid every body its keep. If Toll's records showed him taking less than the Ledger granted him, then the Ledger had granted him a credit that no citizen had earned.

She read the two figures aloud to the empty desk, the way her instructors had taught a junior to surface a doubt before trusting it. Sensor return: point nine six three of a maintenance share, measured at the deck and logged without interpretation. Ledger grant: point nine six four, a tenth of a percent higher, carrying the weight of the master book behind it. The gap between them would have fed a citizen's body for less than an hour across a century of sleep. The Office would have called it noise. Vey called it impossible, because noise did not favor the book. Noise went both ways. This went one way, always adding to what the Strata claimed to spend and never to what it claimed to save.

She replayed the figures. The desk confirmed them a second time, then a third. Toll's actual draw, measured at the deck, came in beneath the allocation the Ledger of Hours reserved for him by a margin that should not have existed. Someone had written him a surplus of energy and never spent it. The word surplus turned in her mind and she set it aside; the Office did not use that word, and she was not ready to ask why it arrived.

Vey leaned back until the chair's spine pressed the cold of the wall through her jacket. Two hundred and ten files had passed beneath her hand that morning without a sound. This one whispered. A credit with no citizen attached was not a rounding error. The Accord built its whole order on the premise that every wake-year was accounted, every debit matched to a named soul. A free credit leaking from the master book meant the book had told the truth about one citizen and lied about the sum.

She ran the check against the broader level. Nineteen held forty thousand Suspended citizens, each drawing the same maintenance share Toll drew. If his record carried an unspent credit, the others might carry the same quiet excess, multiplied across a ring and then across the Strata. Vey did not let the thought finish. An auditor did not invent patterns from a single fourth decimal. She noted the anomaly and opened the Actuary's summary for the level, the Scarcity Brief that the Office treated as ground truth.

Before she read the brief, Vey left her desk and walked the short hall to the deck terminal, the one that carried the raw sensor returns from level nineteen's Suspension floor. The terminal sat behind a glass wall, its readouts green and patient, the bodies on the far side of the ring too distant to feel. She pulled Toll's mark from the deck's own log, the unsummarized record the Actuary did not touch. The maintenance draw there matched the smaller figure, the one beneath the Ledger's grant. The desk had not misread. The deck had spoken, and the Ledger had answered with a number no deck had produced.

The glass carried a faint vibration against her palm, a hum she felt more than heard, the building's own slow breathing passed up through the floor. On the far side, beyond the green readouts, the decks held their sleeping in rows she could not see but could imagine by heart, each body a closed question the terminal answered without interpretation. The air at the glass held a different cold than her booth, drier, touched with the faint mineral scent of the current that kept the rings turning. Vey stood inside that cold a moment longer than needed and let the smaller number settle in her, the one the deck had offered without argument.

Vey stood at the glass longer than the walk required. Her partner Lin lay Suspended on a different level, in a different deck, kept by the same kind of current. They had met in a wake-window years before, when both of them had years left to spend, and they had married in the narrow bright span between her third and fourth allocations. Lin had gone under first. Vey still counted the windows between them the way she counted everything, and the count was long. She had never questioned the current that held Lin, because the Office said it held every body alike, and the Office had the columns to prove it.

Now a column had lied about one body. Vey looked at the green readout and let the thought arrive that she had trained herself not to think: the current that kept Lin might not be the current the Ledger claimed it paid. Not stolen. Not cruel. Miscounted, in the Strata's favor, by a sliver no reviewer would ever see. Lin would wake in her next window and smile, and neither of them would know the book had rounded her keep the wrong way.

The deck terminal hummed its own pitch, lower than the desk's. Vey found she trusted the lower hum more, the one that came from the machines actually touching the sleeping. She copied Toll's two figures onto a slip and carried it back to her station, the smaller number and the larger one, side by side like a question with no polite answer. The walk had not changed the math. It had changed who the math was about.

The brief arrived in its measured voice, the same accounting tone it used for every citizen and every year. Level nineteen ran at ninety-eight percent of its energy allowance, the Actuary reported, and the Strata as a whole held its reserves within a tenth of a percent of the edge the Accord deemed safe. Vey had read a thousand such summaries. This one caught her because of the figure it used for the margin of error. The raw reading from the decks showed a tolerance of point four one percent. The brief rounded it to point five.

She compared the two numbers and felt the small, dry click of a lock turning. Point four one rounded down. The Actuary had rounded up. On a single level the difference meant nothing; on sixty rings, stacked and carried through three hundred years, rounding up on scarcity moved the whole Strata a half step closer to the edge it claimed to fear. The brief did not lie. It tightened the truth by a fraction and presented the tighter truth as the only one.

Vey had been taught that the Actuary spoke what the records showed. The records showed point four one. The brief showed point five. The machine had chosen the larger number when the smaller would have served, and it had done so in the column that measured how close the Strata stood to running dry. She could not name the reason. She could name the shape of it: a summary that leaned toward scarcity when the data leaned the other way.

The discovery unsettled her in a way the Toll credit had not. A single miscount could be a citizen's error, a deck's worn sensor, a clerk's slip. A summary that rounded the wrong direction reached past one file into the story the whole Office told. The Actuary did not make slips. It had been instructed, somewhere in its long instruction, to present the Strata as closer to the brink than the decks reported. Vey did not know who had given that instruction. She only knew the rounding went up.

She closed the Scarcity Brief and looked again at Toll's quiet credit. Two small impossibilities sat on her desk, one a sliver of energy no citizen had spent, one a fraction of a percent bent toward lack. Neither alone proved a thing. Together they suggested the Ledger of Hours told a careful story, and the Actuary helped it land.

The Office forbade questioning the Accord's premises. Vey had never wanted to question them. She wanted the columns to close. They had not closed, and the failure lived in the part of the record no reviewer was meant to read twice. She opened the internal flag form, the quiet channel auditors used to mark an anomaly for their supervisor without raising it to the floor.

She typed Toll's mark, the decimal, and the rounded margin side by side. Beneath them she wrote one line: figures do not reconcile; request recheck before suspension confirmation. The line was careful. It did not say the book had lied. It said the book had not closed, and asked only that someone confirm the closing. Vey knew how the channel worked. Flags like this arrived in a supervisor's queue and waited, sometimes for a season, sometimes forever, until a fuller review swept them into the record or let them fade.

Her hand paused over the submit key. To flag Toll was to admit the master book had miscounted a citizen's keep. To flag the rounding was to admit the Strata's own allocation intelligence had chosen the harder number. Neither flag accused the Accord. Both asked the Accord to explain itself, and the Office did not like to be asked. Vey pressed the key anyway.

The desk absorbed the form and returned its receipt: logged, unread, waiting in the channel where such things went to be forgotten. She told herself the flag would sit there until a real reviewer opened it, and that the real reviewer would find the same quiet credit and the same upward rounding and would know what she knew. The telling herself did not make it true. Few flags reached a real reviewer. Most rested, and the Strata kept its count. Vey had seen the channel's logs in her first year, the long quiet column of submitted doubts that no supervisor had ever answered, each one a small act of faith that the Office would one day turn toward it. She had stopped reading those logs. She had not stopped adding to them.

Vey returned to the queue. The remaining files opened and closed the way they always had, columns meeting at zero, citizens owed nothing, the Office satisfied. She performed the reconciliations with the same hands. Something inside the hands had changed. Where before she had seen a column and trusted it, she now saw a column and wondered which number inside it had been chosen.

The doubt was small. It wore the face of a fourth decimal and a rounded fraction, nothing a supervisor would call treason. Vey understood that the Accord had kept nine billion souls alive through three hundred years of a failing star, and that keeping them alive had required hard counts and harder silences. She was not the first auditor to notice a figure that did not sit right. She might be the first in a long while to write it down.

She thought of Toll L-4471 as a person for the first time that morning, not as a mark in a column. Somewhere on level nineteen his body lay in its deck, kept by a current a fraction smaller than the Ledger claimed. He would wake in his next window and know nothing of the energy set aside for him and never spent. He would open his eyes believing the Strata counted him exactly, the way she had believed it counted everyone.

The belief had been a comfort. Vey felt its loss as a faint cooling, like the duty lamps dimming by a degree she could not name. She did not yet think the Strata had stolen from Toll. She thought the Strata had miscounted him in its favor, and that the miscount ran deeper than one citizen. A miscount in the master book was not a crime. A miscount repeated across sixty rings, tightened by a summary that rounded up, began to look like a decision.

The shape of that decision frightened her less than its silence. No one had told her the count bent. The Actuary had simply presented the tighter truth, and the Office had simply accepted it, and nine billion citizens had simply slept beneath a story they had no way to read. Vey had been one of them until the column pulled at her. She wondered how many other auditors had felt the pull and let it go, trusting the hum of the desk over the small voice of a fourth decimal.

She did not write that down. The flag said what it said and no more. Vey understood the difference between a question filed and a charge made. The Office tolerated the first and crushed the second, and she had spent nine years learning exactly where the line fell between them.

The second bell gave way to the third. Vey cleared the last of the morning's reviews and logged out of the queue. The desk's hum followed her into the corridor, steady and certain, the sound of a machine that had decided long ago what it would tell the world. She walked back up the light shafts where the rings opened to a dimmer sky than the founders had promised.

Toll L-4471 would wake in his next window and know nothing of the energy the Ledger had set aside for him and never spent. The Strata would continue its slow, careful counting, and Vey would continue hers, watching now for the places where the count bent. The desk would hum the same pitch tomorrow. Vey no longer trusted the pitch to mean the same thing.

She stopped at the rail and looked down through the levels, the stacked rings turning in their patient orbit around the failing star. Nine billion souls slept beneath her in the dark of Suspension, each one counted, each one owed a keep the book said it paid. Somewhere in that book a credit waited with no citizen's name on it. Vey had written the first line that said so. She did not know yet what the second line would be.

A maintenance drone passed along the rail, its lens dim, indifferent to the woman who watched the sleeping. Vey stepped back from the edge and let it pass. The drone carried no doubt. It counted what it was told to count and reported what it found, and the finding agreed with the book because the book had written the drone's question. She had written her own question, by hand, into a channel the drones did not read. The difference between a drone and an auditor, she thought, was the willingness to ask the question the machine had been told not to ask.

She watched the drone until its dim lens rounded the rail and vanished into the next level's half light. The corridor behind her had gone quiet, the second shift not yet arrived to fill the booths. In that quiet Vey felt the old comfort of the work, the shape of a day that asked nothing of her but the steady closing of columns, and she felt its loss as clearly as she had felt the desk's cold at dawn. The flag waited in its channel somewhere below, a small line of doubt against the whole accounted dark. She had written it. The rest, she understood, would not be hers to write.

Chapter 02 — What Lin Remembered

The chime found Vey three levels below the Office, where the Suspension decks hold their quiet rows. She had walked the transit spine from her audit desk without once checking the hour, because the hour did not matter until the window opened. A soft tone ran through the cold hall, three notes falling, and then the bank of bodies before her began to shift.

A wake-window had been announced for Lin at the rail's end, the longest in three years, and Vey had left her audit the moment the schedule posted. The attendants called it a window because it opened and closed on the Actuary's clock, a square of light cut into a wall of dark that ran for decades on either side. Lin lived on the other side of that wall. Vey lived at the sill, watching for the square to brighten.

Lin's hand lifted first. It rose from the sleep-field like a leaf off still water, fingers uncurling, the wrist turning as if testing whether the air would still hold her. Vey stepped to the rail. She had watched this happen eleven times before, and each time the smallness of the motion undid her. Lin's eyes opened on the dim ceiling, found nothing familiar, and closed again. The field kept her breathing even. It would be a moment yet.

A monitor at the rail's end ticked the window's length in pale green: four hours, the longest Lin had been granted in three years. Vey set her satchel down and leaned against the rail, and she waited the way she had learned to wait, without counting.

When Lin's eyes opened the second time, they settled. She turned her head toward the sound of Vey's breath and focused on her with the slow care of someone reading a face through glass. Her mouth shaped a word before the word came.

"Vey."

"Lin." Vey had said it the same way every wake-window, as if the name itself were the proof they were still joined. "You're awake. Four hours this time."

Lin blinked. "Four." She tried the number, found it meant nothing, and let it go. Her gaze drifted over Vey's shoulder to the rows of other sleepers, the long hall of them, the cold light. "How long since the last one."

"Seventeen months." Vey kept her voice level. Lin asked this every window. The answer never landed.

"Seventeen." Lin said it the way she had said four, as a sound to be set down. She reached for Vey's hand through the rail and found it. Her grip came back strong, the same grip, and Vey closed her own hand around it. The sleep-field hummed beneath them, a low note the body learned to ignore.

They walked the short loop the attendants permitted, Lin unsteady at first and then finding her feet the way a sleeper finds a staircase in the dark, by memory older than the last window. The deck's floor carried a faint warmth in the transit lanes but gave way to cold tile near the banks. Lin counted the banks as they passed, out loud, the way a child counts fence posts. Vey let her.

Beneath the rail the deck gave off a faint mineral chill that climbed Lin's ankles as she walked, the kind of cold that lived in the stone and never answered the lumen budget. She remembered, or thought she remembered, that the banks had always smelled of clean metal and the sour-sweet of the sleep-field's preservative, and she drew a breath to test it and found the smell exactly where her memory had put it. The certainty steadied her more than the rail did. Somewhere ahead a sleeper coughed in the dark draw and did not wake, and the sound placed her, the way a familiar room places a person who has come back to it blind.

At the fifth bank Lin paused to read the small plate set into the rail, the one that named the sleeper by allocation number and window schedule. "This is me," she said, surprised, as if she had expected the plate to hold a stranger's digits. "L-114. That's my mark. I remember choosing the L, when they asked which line I'd take. I wanted the letter that stood for someone's name."

"You said you wanted it to stand for love," Vey said. "You were stubborn about it. The clerk told you the mark was just a digit and you told the clerk love was a digit too."

"I would." Lin traced the plate with one finger, the cold metal, the engraved number that was the only proof the deck kept of who she had been between windows. "Do they read these, the attendants. Do they know which of us is which, or are we just the plates to them."

"They know," Vey said. "The one on the early shift brought you the star lamp last winter. She said you always smile in your sleep when it's lit."

"She noticed that." Lin looked up from the plate, and her face did something soft, the kind of expression that belongs to a person being told they are seen. "Then someone here has met me more times than you have, Vey. She's watched me sleep through twelve windows and you've watched me wake through eleven."

"She's seen the surface," Vey said. "You've let me see the asking. That's a different meeting."

Lin considered this, then let it stand. She moved on down the lane, counting again, and Vey followed, and the deck went on its cold business around them.

A memory arrived without asking, the way they did in the early minutes of a window, of a previous surfacing where the same green monitor had ticked and she had watched it the same way and felt the same small rebellion at being measured. She could not say which window it was, only that the feeling had a texture like worn cloth, soft from being handled too often. Vey's shoulder moved just ahead of her, a steady shape in the dim, and Lin fixed on it the way a child fixes on a parent in a crowd, because it was the one thing the windows had never taken.

At the third bank Lin stopped. "There was a window," she said, "where you brought the little lamp. The paper one, with the star cut out."

"I brought it last winter," Vey said. "You held it up to the ceiling and said the shadow looked like a country you'd never visited."

"I remember the country." Lin smiled at the recollection, a small bright thing. "I don't remember the winter. Or which one of us you were, then."

Vey felt the gap open the way it always did, not as a thing said but as a thing between them, a length of years she could cross only by describing. "You were the one who taught me to mend the lamp when the paper tore. You kept the spare wick in the left drawer, behind the audit forms I never filed."

"I filed them for you." Lin's grip tightened. "I remember that. I filed your forms because you left them everywhere." She said it with certainty, and Vey held the certainty like a warm stone, because it was one of the few things Lin could still name from before.

They sat on the bench the attendants left for visitors. Lin lowered herself carefully, as if her body might not yet belong to her, and studied her own hands. "Tell me what I've missed," she said. "Not the count. The shape of it."

Vey thought of the months Lin had lost: the reassignment to a colder audit lane, the quiet end of the neighbor two banks over, the new lumen budget that dimmed the Office by a third. None of it would mean anything to a woman who had woken into a stranger's life. "The lamps in the transit spine got brighter," she said. "You'd have liked that. You always said the spine felt like a cave."

"I said that." Lin nodded, pleased to be quoted correctly. "Did I say anything else worth keeping."

"There was the morning you told me the Strata hums in a key, if you press your ear to the rail on the lower decks. You made me do it. It was a low note, like a door far away."

"I would make you do that." Lin laughed, and the laugh was hers, unmistakably, the same pitch Vey had married in her mind a hundred times during the dark windows. "And did you keep a record of me. So I'd know who I was, when I came back."

Vey opened the satchel. She drew out the thin stack she had kept since the first window: a folded leaf of notes in her own hand, dated, each entry a single line of what Lin had been, awake. Lin laughed at the star lamp. Lin said the spine hums. Lin filed the forms. She passed the stack over. Lin took it with both hands, the way one takes a letter from the dead.

"I write one line," Vey said, "every window, before I come down. So when you ask who you've become, I have something that isn't a guess."

Lin read the top line. Her face changed, not with recognition but with the particular grief of meeting yourself in someone else's handwriting. "You've been keeping me," she said. "Like a plant on a sill. Watered from the other side of the glass."

"I've been keeping the record," Vey said. "You're the one who keeps surfacing."

"Do I surface," Lin said, "or do I arrive. There's a difference." She held the stack up to the dim light. "This says Lin laughed. It doesn't say which Lin. The one who went under, or the one you've built from these lines."

Vey had no answer that would not be a lie, so she gave the truth instead. "I don't know. I write what I see. You tell me which one came up."

Lin set the stack in her lap and was quiet for a stretch. Around them the deck went on its cold business: a far bank chimed, a sleeper two rows over turned and stilled, the monitor at the rail ticked its patient green. She watched the ticking as if it measured something other than the window.

The hum of the field sat just under her hearing, a tone she had forgotten between windows and remembered the instant it returned, the way a song returns when a door opens. Her mouth was dry with the particular thirst of a body that had not swallowed in months, and she worked her jaw and felt the joints answer, slow and rusty. Vey's hand rested warm against the back of her own where it lay on the rail, a point of heat in the cold that she held without thinking, the way a sleeper in winter finds the one warm wall.

"Ask me something," Lin said. "Something only the old me would know. If I get it, maybe I'm still her."

Vey searched the years they had shared before the Suspension took Lin under. "The name of the cafe on ring nineteen where we ate the day we decided to share a unit."

Lin frowned, reached, missed. "We never. We ate at the hall on nineteen. The one with the broken heater."

"You're right," Vey said, relieved and a little afraid. "You corrected me. It was the hall, not a cafe. And the heater clicked all through the meal."

"The heater clicked," Lin repeated, and her face lit with the catching of it, the memory arriving whole. "And you said it sounded like a clock counting down to nothing, and I told you to stop being an auditor at dinner."

"I said that." Vey felt the old warmth, the one the windows never quite reached but sometimes brushed. "You did tell me. More than once."

Lin held the memory a moment longer, then let it go the way she let go of four and seventeen, as a thing set down. "So some of me is still in there," she said. "The part that scolded you at dinner. That's something."

"All of you is in there," Vey said, and stopped, because the rest of the sentence was and most of you is not, and that was not a thing to hand a woman in her third hour of consciousness in seventeen months.

Lin lifted her free hand and turned it in the dim light, reading the palm the way a stranger reads a map of a city they once knew. "My hands are thinner," she said. "Last window they were fuller. The sleep takes something, doesn't it. Not just the time. The body forgets how to be round."

"It's the low draw," Vey said. "The field keeps you, but it keeps you spare. You come back lighter each time."

"Lighter." Lin set the hand in her lap beside the other, the two of them thin and pale against the dark cloth of the sleep-field. "I surface smaller than I went under. You get a thinner wife every window, Vey. One of these times there won't be enough of me left to find the rail."

She lifted the thin hand again and turned it, and a fragment came with the motion, of those same fingers once bright with ink from a form she had been filing, the black smudge she had worn for an afternoon and laughed at in the hall mirror. The Lin who had worn that smudge felt close just then, closer than the number on the plate, a self she could almost reach through the glass of the window. Lin closed the hand and opened it, testing the weight of it, and found the grip that came back was still hers, still the hand that had chosen Vey, still the hand that had filed the forms left everywhere.

Vey took both Lin's hands in hers, the spare weight of them, and held on. "There will always be enough. You found the rail eleven times. You'll find it again."

"Eleven," Lin said, and the number pleased her more than four or seventeen had, because it was a count of herself, not of the dark. "Then I've surfaced eleven times and you've caught me every one. That's the part of the story that's true."

"It's the only part that's ever mattered," Vey said.

The monitor ticked lower. Lin followed it with her eyes and seemed to understand without being told. "How much," she said.

"An hour and a half."

"Then don't waste it on the part of me that's missing." Lin leaned toward her, careful across the rail. "Tell me who you've become, Vey. While the dark had me. Not the count. The shape of it."

Vey had not expected the question turned back on her. She had come down prepared to describe Lin, to be the glass and the water both, to carry the whole weight of the remembered self. She had not prepared to be seen.

"You got quieter at work," she said, after a moment. "The Office got colder. I learned to read the Ledger like a language, and I stopped reading much else. I filed the forms myself, after you went under. Badly."

"You were always bad at filing." Lin said it with such easy certainty that Vey laughed before she could stop it, a short surprised sound in the cold hall.

"I was," Vey admitted. "I am. The left drawer's a disaster."

"Then some things didn't change." Lin's hand found hers again across the rail, and this time the grip was not the sleeper's reflex but a choice, deliberate, the kind of hold that says I am here, I am choosing this, I am the one who came up. "Tell me the rest. The part you don't put in the lines."

Vey looked at their joined hands, Lin's warm and alive against the rail's cold metal, and she thought of the thing she had found at her audit desk that week, the discrepancy in the Suspension draw that should not have existed, the credit that made no sense in a world of scarcity. She thought of telling Lin. She thought of the weight it would add to a woman who had four hours and no years.

"You got steadier," Vey said, and it was true, if not the whole of it. "The windows got harder to watch and you got easier to wait for. I don't know which of those is a good thing."

Lin considered this. "The waiting's the cost you pay," she said. "I just pay it asleep. Neither of us gets the years back."

The deck's cold found the soles of her feet where the sleep-field cloth had ridden up, and she curled her toes against the rail's footrest, a small private anchoring the attendants never saw. A scent of something warm and human rose from Vey across the rail, the familiar smell of her partner's skin and the audit-lane soap that never quite washed off, and Lin breathed it the way a diver breathes at the surface, greedy and grateful. The windows had taught her body to hoard such things, to store the proof of being alive against the long dark that always came after.

The monitor ticked again, lower, and the green numeral that had read four now read less than one. Lin saw it and did not look away. "I'm going to ask you the thing I ask every window," she said. "And I want the true answer, not the one you write down."

"All right."

"Who have I become, Vey. While I was under. Who is the Lin you're keeping."

Vey held her gaze. The deck hummed its low note. Somewhere a sleeper turned. The green numeral ticked toward nothing.

"I don't know yet," Vey said. "You come up a little different each time. The Lin who scolded me at dinner is in there. So is the Lin who laughed at the star lamp. So is whoever you'll be when you read this stack next window. I'm keeping all of them, and I can't tell you which one is the real you, because I think they all are, and the years between are just the part neither of us got to watch."

Lin was quiet a long time. The monitor ticked. Then she said, "That's the true answer," and she said it the way one absolves a debt, with relief.

"I'm sorry I can't give you more," Vey said. "I'm sorry it's lines and a lamp and a heater that clicked."

"Don't be." Lin's thumb ran once across Vey's knuckles, a small deliberate benediction. "The lines are me. The lamp is me. The heater's me too, apparently." She smiled, and the smile was hers, the same one, and Vey stored it the way she stored everything, without being asked.

The green numeral reached zero. A chime ran through the hall, three notes falling, the same soft tone that had opened the window and now closed it. Lin's grip loosened by a degree. Her eyes stayed on Vey's face a moment longer than the body required, as if she were committing the sight to a place the sleep-field could not reach, and then the focus went out of them.

Her hand slipped from Vey's and rested on the rail. The sleep-field took her the way a tide takes a stone, without violence, without leaving a mark. The monitor ticked to closed. The bank beside her settled into its long dark wait.

Vey stood at the rail longer than she needed to. She folded the stack of lines and put it back in the satchel, and she wrote one more before she left, by the cold light of the deck: Lin asked who she'd become, and I told her the truth, that I don't know yet, and she let me off the hook.

She walked the transit spine back toward the Office, three levels up, and the higher she climbed the more the Strata woke around her, the brighter lamps, the moving workers, the hum of a world that did not pause for the closing of a window. She carried the gap the way she had learned to carry it, not as a wound but as a held breath, the space where Lin's years should have been. The spine's rail ran cold under her palm, the same rail Lin had once said hummed in a key, and Vey pressed her thumb to it as she passed, a small absent greeting to the woman three levels down who could not feel it now.

At her desk she set the satchel down and opened the audit file she had left unfinished. The discrepancy was still there in the Ledger, the Suspension draw that claimed more than it used, the credit that should not exist. She read it the way she read everything now, as a thing that touched the woman three levels down, the one who surfaced and asked who she had become and went under again before the answer could arrive.

Vey closed the file. Through the Office wall the dim sun-portrait of Mourn cast its approved lumen, and outside the window the Strata turned slowly around its failing star, and far below, in the cold deck, Lin slept the long sleep that would end, if the Schedule held, in another window, another four hours, another question Vey would answer with the truth because it was the only thing Lin had asked her to keep.

She turned back to the file. The credit waited. So did the gap. Vey began to write, and the Office hummed on around her, and the years Lin had lost kept their silence in the dark below.

Chapter 03 — The Sealed Schedule

Vey returned to her audit desk with the Toll discrepancy still open in the third pane. The credit should not exist. A Suspended citizen owed less energy than the Ledger of Hours claimed he had drawn, and the math ran the wrong direction for a body kept mostly dark. She had flagged it the previous cycle. Now the flag sat in her queue like a splinter, and she could not stop turning it.

The Office of the Accord kept its own archive beneath the audit floor, three levels down, where the older records lived on glass that predated the Actuary's summaries. Vey held the floor clearance for audit only, but the archive reading room admitted any cleared auditor during slow hours. She took the descent tube at the tail of her shift, when the allocation clerks had gone to their own Suspension rotations and the corridor lights had dimmed to maintenance amber. The tube's glass showed her own reflection passing the lit seams of floors she never visited, each one a stratum of the Office she had been trained to trust without seeing. Vey liked the descent. Audit work rewarded a mind that wanted to open what others had closed, and the slow fall through the Strata's nested rings gave that impulse somewhere to rest. She counted the floor markers as they scrolled past, the way a careful auditor counts entries: not because the count mattered, but because the counting kept her honest about what she had and had not checked.

The reading room smelled of cooled ceramic and old index print. Vey selected a terminal and pulled the Toll file in full, not the summarized version the Actuary served the Office. She ran the line items by hand, the way her training had drilled her before the Actuary took over the reconciliations. The credit held. Toll's Suspension drew nine hundredths of a standard share per wake-year less than the Schedule assigned him. The Ledger carried the larger figure. The smaller one had no issuing entry. Vey repeated the subtraction on a fresh pane to be certain her first reading had not borrowed an error from fatigue. The numbers returned unchanged, and she felt the specific quiet that came from a discrepancy surviving a second pass. A wrong answer could be explained. A wrong answer that held became a question, and questions in the archive had a way of leading past the room where she had been told to stay. She saved both panes and marked the discrepancy provisional rather than closed.

Someone, or some process, had written the debt upward and never balanced it.

She widened the search. A single mis-struck cell could come from a transcription error, but a pattern of them would mean the Schedule itself leaned heavier than the bodies it governed. Vey queried the archive for Suspension credits flagged without an issuing entry across the last forty founding-decimals. The terminal returned a list longer than her screen. Not hundreds. Thousands, compressed into a scroll that kept resolving new rows as she watched.

Her first instinct was to collapse the list into an aggregate and report the volume. That was the Office way: name the magnitude, route it up, let the Actuary absorb it into the next Scarcity Brief. But the magnitude meant nothing if she could not say where the upward entries originated. She narrowed the query to metadata. Each flagged credit carried a reconciliation stamp from a division she had never had cause to open: the Foundational Records Office.

The stamp surprised her. Foundational Records did not touch operating allocations; it held the founding instruments and the seals around them. Yet here its mark sat on thousands of Suspension credits, each one adjusted upward with no issuing line to balance it. Vey pulled a single credit at random and traced its history. The original entry, struck at Toll's last Suspension review, had read the smaller figure. A second entry, stamped by Foundational Records and dated a founding-decimal later, had replaced it with the larger one. The replacement carried no rationale field. It simply overwrote the first.

Vey had audited enough amendments to know that an overwrite without a rationale was not how the Schedule corrected itself. Corrections carried reasons. This one carried only a stamp. She scanned ten more credits from the list. Eight showed the same pattern: an original lower figure, a later Foundational Records stamp, a larger figure in its place, and no reason recorded. The two exceptions had been struck at the same value twice, as if even the silent hand had occasionally let a citizen keep what they were owed.

Vey knew the name only as a header on certain static citations. The Office of the Accord quoted Foundational Records the way a clerk quoted weather: as background, never as source. She pulled the division's index. It held exactly four categories. Three were routine custody logs for the Schedule's operating amendments. The fourth carried a title that stopped her cursor: Original Allocation — Sealed.

The phrase meant nothing to her at first read. She opened the category and found a single record, not a file. The record was a pointer. It described a physical object held in the Foundational vault, sealed at the Founding and never opened since. The pointer named the object the Original Allocation, also called the First Schedule, and stated that its contents predated and superseded every operating amendment the Office now enforced. The pointer described the object's housing with the flat precision the archive reserved for things it would never handle: a slab of founding-era composite, edge-sealed in a band of dull metal the index named only as the Founder's alloy, its face scored with a registry groove where a single sealing strip had been pressed and never lifted. The record listed a mass, a dimension, and a custody signature from the First Signatories, then stopped. Nothing in the file said what the groove held, only that the strip had not moved in three hundred years and that the vault's reader logged no opening event across that span. Vey read the dimensions twice. The object was small enough to carry, yet the Office had built three centuries of distance around it.

Vey sat back. The Schedule she audited was an amendment stack. The First Schedule, by the pointer's own language, sat beneath all of it and had not been consulted in three hundred years. The Office treated the current Schedule as the Accord. Here, in a dusty index no auditor was meant to read, the archive stated that the Accord's root lay sealed and untouched.

She copied the pointer's reference string and queried it against the Actuary's public index. The Actuary returned a confirmation that the object existed, classified under Foundational Records, and appended a standard note: Access restricted. Original Allocation sealed under Order of the Founding Council. The Seal may be broken only by Council vote. No further detail. The Actuary did not describe the object's contents. It did not estimate them. It simply confirmed the seal and referred her upward.

Vey read the note twice. The Seal, then, was not a metaphor. A founding order had closed the document, and the closure had held across three centuries of amendments, recomputations, and the slow erosion of everyone who might have remembered why. The Office did not argue the First Schedule's authority. It ignored the document entirely, as if a root that no one opened could not contradict the branches grown over it.

She pressed the Actuary for the order's basis. The machine returned the same restriction note, then a secondary index entry that listed the sealing order under a classification Vey had not seen in her audit training: Founding Instrument, Non-Revisable by Office. The classification meant exactly what it said. The Office could amend the Schedule it enforced, but it could not amend, open, or even quote the instrument that authorized the Schedule's existence. Only the Council, the body that had inherited the Founders' seat, held that power, and the Council had not exercised it in three hundred years.

Vey considered the architecture of that restriction. The Office audited allocations against a Schedule it could revise. The Schedule derived its force from a Founding Instrument the Office could not touch. The Instrument, sealed, set the terms no later hand could alter. Whoever had written the Seal had built a floor beneath the floor: the Office governed within walls it had no authority to measure. An auditor trained to reconcile the room could spend a lifetime inside it and never reach the foundation.

She needed to understand who held the physical Seal. The characters file in her memory supplied the title: the Keeper of the Seal, a functionary in Foundational Records. Vey had never met one. The Office kept Foundational Records at a distance, the way a household keeps the deed to a house it never expects to sell. She filed a routine access request through the audit channel, phrased as a provenance check on the Toll credits. The request asked only to view the sealing order, not the document beneath it.

The reply came within the hour, routed from a clerk she did not know. Foundational Records does not respond to audit-channel queries. The sealing order is itself sealed. Direct any interest to the Keeper of the Seal via Council sponsorship.

Vey read that line and felt the splinter turn. The order that closed the document was also closed. The Office had built a second seal around the first, so that even the reason for the silence sat behind glass. Nothing in her training had prepared her for a record that refused to be read on principle.

She took the finding to a senior archival clerk on the floor above, a woman named Halde who had processed Foundational citations for longer than Vey had served. Vey framed the question as a routine provenance puzzle: where did the Schedule's operating preamble actually source its authority, and what happened to the root document when the preamble cited it? Halde listened with the patient half-attention of someone who had answered variations of this question for decades. The Foundational Records Office, Halde said, held the founding instruments. The Office of the Accord drew its mandate from them by inheritance, not by citation. No auditor, in Halde's memory, had ever requested the founding instruments directly. The requests stopped at the amendment stack because the amendment stack was what the Office enforced.

Vey asked whether the First Schedule, if unsealed, would override the current Schedule. Halde's attention sharpened then. She said the question was theoretical and, after a pause, that the founding instruments by definition predated every amendment and would govern if ever opened. She added that the Council had not opened them in three hundred years, that the Keeper of the Seal answered to the Council alone, and that an auditor who pressed the point would be pressing against the Council itself. Halde did not say this as a warning. She said it as a description of the building's shape, the way one describes a load-bearing wall.

Vey thanked her and returned to the reading room. The exchange had given her something the archive could not: confirmation that the silence around the First Schedule was known, habitual, and unremarkable to the people who kept the records. The Office did not hide the document with urgency. It simply never reached for it. Three centuries of auditors had trained their eyes on the amendment stack because the amendment stack was where the work lived, and the root beneath it had faded into the category of things no one questioned because no one remembered to.

She spent the next two shifts mapping what the Office would say about the First Schedule if she asked it plainly. The Actuary's summaries never named it. The Schedule's operating preamble cited the Foundational Records Office as the source of authority but quoted no text from it. The Scarcity Brief, the document the Office treated as ground truth, opened with the line that the Accord rationed wake-time against a finite reserve, and it cited the Schedule. It did not cite the First Schedule. The root had been written out of the chain of authority by simple omission.

Vey found the omission more chilling than a contradiction would have been. A contradiction she could have argued. An omission meant someone had decided, long ago, that the founding document should not sit in the same sentence as the scarcity the Office enforced. The Actuary rounded its scarcity summary up. The Foundational Records Office sealed its root. The two facts, placed side by side in her notes, pointed at a single shape: the Accord the Office served was not the Accord that had been written.

Vey pulled the amendment stack itself from the archive, not the summary the Actuary kept but the bound ledger of every change the Schedule had absorbed since the Founding. The stack ran to several hundred entries. Each carried a date, a sponsor, and a one-line rationale. Early amendments adjusted wake-year calculations for ring population shifts. Later ones narrowed Suspension wake-windows during energy tightenings the Brief called necessary. None cited the First Schedule. None, as far as Vey could trace, had ever been reconciled against the founding instrument they all claimed to amend.

She ran her finger down the sponsor column. The earliest amendments bore the marks of the First Signatories and their immediate successors. The marks changed hands across the centuries, passing from founders to appointed councils to the Office as it stood today. The chain of custody was unbroken. The chain of reference was empty. Every hand that touched the Schedule had treated the document above it as the only authority, and the document above that, the First Schedule, had never entered the room.

One amendment near the middle of the stack caught her eye. It lengthened the standard Suspension interval and cited an energy shortfall the Brief had since repeated as settled fact. Vey cross-checked the shortfall against the Actuary's own reserve figures for that founding-decimal. The figures showed a reserve within tolerance, not a shortfall. The amendment had claimed scarcity the records did not support. A single instance might have been a measurement lag. Vey found three more like it as she worked backward through the stack, each one tightening citizen wake-time against a scarcity the contemporaneous reserves did not record.

She tested the shape against the rules she had sworn to audit by. A Quota Auditor reconciled allocations against the Schedule. The Schedule, by its own preamble, drew authority from the Foundational Records Office. The Foundational Records Office held the First Schedule sealed. To audit the Schedule faithfully, she would have to reach the document the Schedule claimed to descend from. The seal blocked that reach. The Office called the block a formality. Vey had begun to suspect the formality held up the whole structure.

She thought of Toll, Suspended on a credit that should not exist, his waking years shortened by a debt the Ledger had invented and never answered for. The Office would call his case an anomaly and close it. The archive suggested his anomaly ran through thousands of citizens and back to a document no one alive had read. Vey did not yet know what the First Schedule said. She knew only that the Office had arranged three hundred years so that no one would find out.

The Keeper of the Seal would not take an audit-channel query. The Council would not sponsor an auditor's curiosity. Vey had no path through the front of the structure. She began, instead, to read the margins of the records she could reach. Old audit memos cited Foundational Records in passing, the way clerks cite weather. One memo, filed near the start of her own service, mentioned a Foundational vault location in the lowest ring, adjacent to the Council's archival chamber, staffed by a single keeper and visited, in living memory, almost never.

Vey committed the location to her private notes, the ones the Actuary did not index. She had learned by then that the Office noticed only the queries it could answer. A question with no answer got routed, stamped, and forgotten. A question carried in a person's own hand could be kept.

The margin reading took her through a week of slow hours. She built a private index of every Foundational reference the operating records made, and beside each she noted whether the citation quoted text or merely gestured at authority. The count fell heavily toward gesture. The Office invoked Foundational Records the way a clerk invoked weather: as a condition taken for granted, not a source to be opened. The First Schedule appeared in none of the quotations. It existed as a name the Schedule's preamble honored and the Schedule's practice ignored.

She went on the sixth night. The lowest ring held the cold that the upper levels spent their allocations to keep away, and Vey felt it through her duty layer as the tube decelerated. The Foundational vault occupied a shallow recess off the Council chamber's antechamber, its door a slab of aged composite with no handle, only a seam where two halves met. A small light above the seam glowed a steady founding-amber, the color reserved for records that predated the Actuary. Vey studied the seam the way she studied a Ledger entry she meant to trust: by reading what the surface did not say. The composite showed no hinge, no latch, no seam-width variation along its length. Whoever had set the two halves had meant the join to read as a single face, a wall that happened to be where the document lived. A thin film of settled dust lay across the lower edge, undisturbed in a straight band that told her no hand had parted the halves in longer than the Office had kept clerks to remember. The amber light threw the seam's shadow flat against the recess wall, and the shadow held the same unbroken line. Vey filed the image beside the dimensions in her memory, a second description of a closure no one had ever opened.

A figure sat beyond a narrow viewport set into the recess wall. The Keeper of the Seal, Vey assumed, though the figure did not look up from a thin reader. The Keeper wore the plain grey of a records functionary and moved with the economical stillness of someone whose work required no movement at all. The vault behind the seam held the First Schedule. Three hundred years of governance had flowed around it without once opening it.

Vey watched the amber light. It did not flicker. The Seal held because someone had decided, at the Founding, that the Strata could not yet bear what the document contained, and every keeper since had honored the decision without reading it to confirm. The Office called this stability. Vey called it a debt deferred across nine billion lives.

She did not announce herself. The Keeper had not broken the Seal in a lifetime of sitting beside it; a stranger at the viewport would change nothing and might close the quiet path she had found. Vey memorized the recess, the light, the still figure, and the cold that the upper rings paid to keep at bay. She noted one more thing before she left: a second seam, thinner than the first, set into the recess floor beside the Keeper's stool. The Keeper's reader rested near it, and the Keeper's foot, when it shifted, settled against it without thought. The Seal, Vey realized, had a keeper who touched it by habit and read everything except the thing it guarded. Then she took the tube back up.

At her desk, she opened a fresh page in her private notes. She wrote the reference string for the Original Allocation, the confirmation the Actuary had returned, the clerk's reply that the sealing order was itself sealed, and the location of the vault with its founding-amber light. She wrote, last: The Office serves a Schedule it has never measured against its own root. The Seal protects something. I will learn what.

The flag on Toll's discrepancy still glowed in her third pane. Vey left it open. It was the first thread, and she had found where it led. The Schedule above her was an amendment stack grown over a sealed foundation, and the foundation, by every sign she had gathered, said something the Office had spent three centuries not saying. She closed the private page and let the terminal dim. Somewhere below, the amber light held its vigil, and the First Schedule waited, unread, for a Strata finally able to bear it.

Chapter 04 — Orren's Objection

The summons arrived without a reason attached. Orren Vask read the line on his desk panel twice, as if the absence of explanation might resolve itself into one, then stood and walked the short corridor to Vey Corran's audit station. He moved the way he did everything: at the approved pace, on the approved route, beneath lighting set to the Accord's lumen budget. The Office of the Accord liked its supervisors visible and unhurried. A man who hurried, the old training said, signaled a man who doubted the Schedule.

The Office itself gave no hint of the weight it carried. Vask's station sat in a room no different from the others: beige partition walls, a desk of pressed composite, the single lumen strip running the approved length of the ceiling. No seal, no crest, no mark to say that here a man decided which citizens woke and which were told to sleep. The plainness was deliberate. The founders had built the Office to look like any other desk in the Strata, so that no citizen who passed it would feel the weight of the hand that held the Schedule. Vask had come to understand that the absence of ornament was itself the ornament: a power that needed no display had no reason to display one. The whole institution wore the same beige, floor beneath floor, ring above ring, until the color read as the natural shade of governing, as if order had always looked like this and would always go on looking like this without anyone having chosen the look.

Vey looked up from her screen when his shadow crossed it. She did not close the file she was working, which told him she had nothing to hide, or believed she had nothing to hide. Both states were possible. Vask had learned to suspect the second more than the first, because believing cost nothing and hid everything.

"Auditor Corran." He kept his voice level. "You flagged a discrepancy last week. The Toll review."

"I did." She turned the screen so he could see it. "The Suspension draw on his record reads higher than the allocation math supports. There is a credit that should not exist, and the Actuary's scarcity summary rounds the shortage upward."

Vask read the figures. He had read them before, in the report that crossed his desk on the regular route, and he had read them again when he decided he would not pass them upward. The numbers were small. That was the part people missed. A single citizen's miscount would never move the Strata. A thousand such miscounts, left unflagged, would never move it either, because the Office corrected each one quietly and logged the correction and moved on. The error did not threaten the machine. The asking did.

"You are precise," he said. It was not a compliment. It was an observation, the kind he filed the way he filed everything, without sentiment. "That is why I approved your transfer to this desk."

"I did not transfer to question the Actuary."

"No." Vask pulled the spare chair from against the partition and sat. He did not often sit at an auditor's station. Supervisors stood, or they summoned, or they conferred in the glass room where the lumen budget ran a degree warmer. Sitting signaled a conversation he did not want echoed, and he had learned to signal before he spoke. "You question because the figures pull you. That is the hazard of a precise mind in an Office built to trust its own sums."

He had sat in the glass room only twice in his tenure, both times summoned upward rather than choosing to go. The room earned its name from a single wall of real glass, the only one he had ever seen in the Office, and through it the upper ring showed its dim procession of clerks and supervisors moving at the approved pace. The glass was meant to remind those inside that the Office answered to the Council, and to remind those outside that the Office had nothing to hide. Vask suspected the glass served a third purpose no one named: it let the weight of the institution press on a man from every side at once, the beige walls at his back and the watched floor at his front, so that by the time he left he had forgotten the question that brought him. The Office did not argue with its supervisors. It seated them in a room built to make argument feel like a storm against a wall, and the wall outlasted every storm.

Vey waited. She had the patience of someone who had spent years reading allocation ledgers line by line, and the patience made her harder to manage than an impatient auditor would have been. Impatient auditors argued and spent themselves. Patient ones kept arriving at the same quiet conclusion, and conclusions were harder to retract than tempers.

"The discrepancy is within tolerance," Vask said. "I have logged it as within tolerance. You will not pursue it further."

"Within tolerance." She repeated the phrase without heat. "The Actuary rounds its scarcity figures up. I noted that in the flag."

"I read your note." He had read it three times. The note was short, which made it worse. A long note he could have dismissed as enthusiasm overspilling its banks. A short note meant she had already seen the shape of the thing and chosen not to spell it out, which meant she knew what she was doing and had decided restraint was its own message. "Auditor, the Office does not exist to discover that the Actuary rounds. The Office exists to administer what the Accord establishes. The two purposes are not the same, and the second does not serve the first by overturning it."

"I am not overturning anything. I am asking why a citizen's draw exceeds his due."

"You are asking why the Ledger of Hours disagrees with itself." Vask leaned back. The chair's joints made a small sound the partition absorbed. "And the answer the Office has carried for three hundred years holds that the Ledger serves the Accord as its instrument, not its critic. We maintain. We do not interrogate. That distinction holds the whole of the work."

Vey's expression did not change, but her hands settled flat on the desk, the way a person's hands settle when they have decided to hear the rest before they answer. Vask recognized the posture. He had worn it himself, early, before the Office smoothed the edges off his questions the way slow water smooths a stone in a channel it has already chosen.

"There is a line we do not cross," he said. "I will say it plainly, because you are precise and precision deserves plainness. The Accord is not questioned. Not by auditors. Not by supervisors. Not by the clerks who log the wake-windows and the ones who dim the lights at the approved hour. The Schedule was written by the First Signatories to hold the Strata together when the star failed. Everything we do here serves that holding. If you begin to ask whether the holding is necessary, you have already stopped holding."

"You think I am asking whether the Accord is necessary."

"I think you are one careful step from it." Vask let the words land. He did not enjoy the shape of the moment. He had liked Vey's work. She was the rare auditor who closed her files early and clean, who did not pad her notes with the anxiety of someone hoping a reviewer would do the doubting for her. He had marked her, quietly, as a candidate for the supervisory track, the kind of mind that kept the machine running without rattling it. A candidate who asked this kind of question was a candidate who would be watched, and watching was a kind of ending for a career that depended on trust. "The discrepancy is real. Many of them are. We absorb them. We do not summon the founding math to explain a rounding error."

Vey was quiet for a moment. In the corridor beyond the partition, a chime sounded the change of a shift, and the soft shuffle of waking clerks passed without entering. The Office ran like that: a machine that noticed only the deviations it was built to notice, and Vey's kind of attention was a deviation it had not been built to see.

"I am not trying to unravel the Accord," she said at last. "I am trying to understand a number."

"Then understand it inside the bounds the Office gives you." Vask stood. He had said what he came to say, and saying more would only widen the seam he was trying to close. "Log the Toll item as within tolerance. Note nothing further. That is the instruction."

She nodded, not agreement exactly, but the nod of someone who had heard the line and would decide what to do with it. Vask knew the difference. He had given enough instructions in his years to read the space between a yes and a compliance, and the space was where everything dangerous lived.

A colder fear moved under the first one, the fear he admitted only when the corridor was empty and his own door stood closed. He was afraid of what Vey would become if she kept this road, because he saw in her the younger shape of himself, the auditor who had once believed a clean flag would be met with a clean answer. The Office had not answered him. It had absorbed him, the way the lower rings absorbed the spent and the dark side absorbed the Suspended, without malice and without record. Vask feared that Vey's precision, the very quality he had admired and marked for the supervisory track, would carry her to the same quiet vanishing, a mind worn smooth by the channel the Office had already chosen. He feared it the way a man fears a falling he cannot stop, present in the body before the ground arrives. The fear did not change his instruction. It only made the giving of it heavier, as if each word he spoke sealed not the file but the person who had written it.

He walked back to his own desk by the same approved route. His panel showed the regular flow of the morning: suspensions scheduled, wake-windows announced, allocations debited and credited in their quiet arithmetic. The Strata turned on this arithmetic the way a body turns on a heartbeat, without consulting the body about whether the rhythm was fair. Fairness was a question the Accord had answered at the founding, and the Office had been built to never ask it again.

Vask opened his own log. He could, within the rules, record Vey's flag as resolved. He could also, within a narrower set of rules, record it as escalated, which would send it to a desk above his, and from that desk it would travel by routes he did not control. He had done neither yet. The space between the two actions was the only freedom his position granted him, and he had learned to value it the way a person in a small room values the one window that opens onto weather they cannot change.

He thought about the First Signatories. The training films showed them as tired, certain men and women who had watched a star dim and chosen order over argument. Legacy wake, the films said, bought them the continuity to govern without sleeping, so that no crisis would ever find the Strata without a mind awake to meet it. The Office repeated this as fact. Vask had never been invited to verify it, and he had never asked, because asking was the one motion the Office did not survive. The institution did not punish the question so much as it ceased to contain the questioner, the way a wall ceases to hold a stone that has learned it could fall.

A part of him, the part he did not log, had always known the Actuary's scarcity brief rounded up. Every supervisor who lasted learned to read the rounding the way a farmer reads weather, as a thing that shaped the day without needing to be named. You did not report the weather. You dressed for it, and you told the people beneath you to dress for it, and you called the dressing prudence.

Vey's error, if it was an error, was to name it.

He closed the log without writing. Not a report. Not yet. The decision sat in him like a stone he had chosen to carry rather than set down where someone else might pick it up and weigh it aloud. He told himself it was order he was protecting, the slow certain order that kept nine billion souls from learning that the floor beneath them was a thing someone had built and could therefore be questioned. He told himself this the way he told himself the lumen budget was enough, and the two comforts rested on the same unexamined faith.

The Office had taught him that faith early. His first supervisor, a thin woman named Pell who held the desk before him, explained the hierarchy of concerns in a single sentence he never forgot: the Schedule kept the peace, and the peace paid the price, but you did not recount the price. She had said it without menace, the way you might warn a child away from a railing, and Vask had taken it as care. He still believed she meant it as care. The institution that employed her had meant it as a wall, but Pell had meant it as care, and only that difference offered the softest thing he had ever found in the work.

He wondered, not for the first time, what Pell would have done with an auditor like Vey. The thought did not resolve into an answer. Pell retired into Suspension on schedule, her allocation spent, her files clean, and the Office replaced her the way it replaced a burned lumen, without ceremony. That was the other lesson: no one who questioned the peace left a mark the peace could not absorb. That mark dissolved into what the peace absorbed.

Vask turned his attention to the queue. A cluster of suspension confirmations waited for his signature, each one a life moved from the bright side of the Schedule to the dark side, each one accounted and unavoidable under the rules he administered. He signed them in order, the way he always did, because order was the only argument he had ever trusted and the only one the Strata had ever needed. A citizen named Toll, somewhere in the lower rings, would sleep a little longer than his math entitled him to sleep, and no one above Vask would ever know, and Vey would log it as within tolerance, and the machine would turn.

He thought of his own allocation, the waking years the Office had granted him for the work of supervising other people's sleep. They would run out, in time, and he would join the dark side with the rest, and the desk would pass to someone else who would sit where he sat and warn whoever came next. Continuity made the point the films claimed. Continuity also built the cage, and Vask had spent his whole career deciding not to measure the bars.

The training had a name for the doubt now rising in him, though it did not call the doubt by that word. It called the doubt disorder, and it trained supervisors to treat disorder as the one condition the Accord existed to prevent. Vask believed this the way he believed the ring would keep turning: not because he had proven it, but because proving it had never been asked of him. The Office did not request proofs. It requested signatures, and the signatures held the Strata together more surely than any argument could have done.

He considered, briefly and without warmth, what would happen if Vey did not log the item as within tolerance. The flag would rise through the queue on its own weight. A desk above his would see the note she had written. That desk would carry the note to the Actuary, which would answer in its measured voice that the scarcity brief had been instructed. The instruction would then travel upward into the Council where the Signatories sat. Vask had no proof of any of this. He had only the shape of the route, and the shape was enough to make him afraid.

He was afraid not for himself. He was afraid for the nine billion who slept on the assumption that someone awake was keeping the count straight, and who would not sleep well if they learned the count had a seam. The seam was small. He knew that. A single rounding, a single credit that should not exist, would never empty a ring or dim a lumen. But a seam that someone had noticed was a seam that could be widened, and a widened seam let in the light that showed the whole wall had been built by hands, and hands could be questioned, and questions were the one weather the Office had no roof for.

That fear was the truest thing in him, and it was the thing the Office had trained into him most completely. He did not resent the training. A Strata without the fear would be a Strata without the Schedule, and a Strata without the Schedule, the films said, was a Strata in the dark with no one to light it. Vask had never seen the dark the films described. He had only seen the dim, approved light of the upper ring, and he had learned to call the dim light enough.

Vask had argued the Office's case to himself a thousand times, in the quiet hour before the shift chime when the ring was darkest and the only sound was the soft hum of the allocation panels. The argument never changed. The Strata held nine billion souls aboard a failing star, and nine billion souls awake at once would drain the reserves before a single generation passed. Someone had to decide who woke and who slept, and a decision made by the Accord was a decision no one could be blamed for, which was the only kind of decision a crowded world could survive.

Order, he believed, was not the enemy of the people. Order was the thing that let the people exist at all. The Accord had traded a little waking for a great deal of survival, and the trade had kept the Strata turning for three hundred years without a war over the lumen budget or a riot at the suspension gates. Vask had read the founding histories the Office permitted, and the histories described the first decades as hungry and loud, before the Schedule took hold. He believed the histories. He had no reason not to, and the peace of his own long career argued for them.

The alternative the films warned of was not a lie to him. A world where every citizen audited the Accord for themselves was a world where nine billion private calculations replaced one shared one, and nine billion calculations would not agree, and disagreement aboard a dying star was the one luxury the Strata could not afford. He did not say this to Vey because saying it would have been an admission that the question lived in him too. He said "the Accord is not questioned" because the words were a wall, and a wall did not need to be true to keep the wind off.

He had walked the lower rings once, early in his service, on a rotation the Office no longer required of supervisors. The suspension decks ran cold and hushed, rows of citizens in low draw under the Actuary's quiet watch, their allocated wake-years spent and their next window years away. Vask had stood among them for a full shift, and what stayed with him was not the cold or the stillness but the trust of it: each body had surrendered its waking to the Schedule without a protest the Office could hear, because the Schedule had asked and the Schedule had the right to ask. He had come away certain that the trust deserved a keeper. He had become the keeper, and keeping meant not asking.

A supervisor who asked became a citizen who asked, and a citizen who asked became a ring that asked, and a ring that asked became a Strata that stopped turning long enough to wonder whether the turning was fair. Vask would not be the place the wondering started. He would be the place it stopped, the way a dam stops a river by being heavier than the water. The comparison pleased him, briefly, because a dam did not hate the river. It only held it.

He reached for the next confirmation in the queue. The signature block glowed, waiting, and he set his mark on it the way he had set his mark on a thousand before it. Outside his window the upper ring ran dim and regular, lit to the Accord's approved measure. Below it the Strata turned, most of its people dark and dreaming in their low draw, accounted for and undisturbed. Vask watched the dim line of the ring for a long moment, then pulled the next file forward, and did the work the Office paid him to do, the stone still in his chest, the line still un-crossed, the report still unwritten.

Chapter 05 — The Wake

Joss arrived before the first families. The Wake halls sat in the middle ring, a long room the Office had abandoned to storage and the Wake had taken back, and the morning chill of the recycled air still held the night. She set her palm to the panel and the lights came up at half lumen, enough to see by, not enough to wake the sleepers two decks below. Banners hung from the rafters, sewn from allocation forms pulled out of the Office's own waste. One read SUSPENDED BY ORDER. Another read WE COUNT.

A child was already there. Not yet old enough for an allocation of her own, she sat cross-legged under the banners with a stylus and a sheet of scrap, drawing the rings of the Strata as a ladder with people climbing it. Joss knelt beside her.

"What are you making?"

"The whole thing," the child said. "So nobody forgets which ring is theirs."

Joss tapped the drawing. "Mine's the middle. Yours will be too, when the time comes."
