The child considered this, then added a figure at the ladder's foot, small and careful. "This is the one who stays awake to count the others. So they don't forget themselves."

"Good," Joss said. "That's the job. Someone has to stay awake to keep the count."

She left the child to the drawing and walked the length of the halls, checking the things that needed checking before a meeting. The crate stage sat square under the banners. The lamp that the Office's budget permitted threw a soft pool of light that never quite reached the corners. The rail at the far wall, the one the Wake had installed so the families could practice the posture of waiting at a Suspension deck, stood empty and patient. Joss ran a hand along it. The metal was cool, the way the decks below were cool, the way the bodies in the low draw were cool, kept and dreaming nothing.

The child looked up, serious in a way only the very young or the very long-suspended could manage. "My mother is down there. In the low draw. She says it's like waiting in a quiet room."

"She's right," Joss said. "And one day she'll walk out of it. That's what we're here for."

By the second hour the halls filled. They came the way the Suspended always moved through the waking world, in small clusters and with one eye on the clock, because every minute upright was a minute debited from some future. A man whose wife had been dark for eleven years. A quartet of neighbors who pooled their wake-windows so that at least one of them was always present to tend the others' bodies. A grandmother who had outlived three of her children into Suspension and meant to outlive the policy that took them.

The banners kept their silent tally above them all. SUSPENDED BY ORDER sagged a handspan lower than the rest, its allocation form yellowed at the corners where the Office's staple had once bitten through. WE COUNT stood straighter, newer, its letters cut from a form the Wake had seized before the burn. Between them the child's ladder climbed the rafter, the wrong-colored sun a small defiance against the approved lumen. Joss read the cloth the way she read a room, by what it chose to declare and what it chose to leave hanging.

Families settled against the cool wall. A woman from the lower workforce rings wrapped her sleeping infant tighter and watched the rail, measuring the room against some other room she had lost. Two brothers arrived still in their shift smocks, the grease of the support lines still on their cuffs, and took the bench near the crate without a word to each other, the way men who had divided a grief did. A widow in a patched allocation coat moved among them offering cups of warmed water drawn from the hall's own limited ration, and each cup passed hand to hand until the line of small kindnesses reached the back wall. The Wake had learned this, that a body kept warm was a body kept present, and presence was the first thing the Schedule tried to take.

One face held Joss longer than the rest. An older man sat at the edge of the lamp's pool, his hands folded around a single allocation form he had not yet unfolded. His name came to her from the map in her head: Sael, from the west clusters, a mender like her brother had been. He had surfaced for this window alone, she remembered, because the Office granted the bereaved a single upright hour at a Suspension's turning. He met her eyes and gave the smallest nod, the acknowledgment of one counter recognizing another across the distance the Accord had built between them. Joss nodded back. That nod, plain and unremarkable, carried more weight than any speech she could make. It said he had come to be counted, and that he would carry the count home.

Joss climbed onto the low crate that served as a stage. She did not raise her voice. The room went quiet on its own, the way it did when people understood they were in the presence of someone counting them.

"We've been meeting here for seventy windows," she said. "Seventy chances to tell the Strata that Suspension is not a sentence the star passed down. It's a line in a ledger someone chose to write."

A murmur ran the length of the room. Joss let it. The Wake had learned that agreement needed to be felt before it could be spoken.

"Most of you have lost someone to the low draw. Most of you have stood at the rail of a Suspension deck and watched your person breathe without dreaming. You know what it costs. The Office knows what it costs. The cost is not the argument."

She held up a single allocation form, the kind printed on the thin stock the Office burned each cycle.

"The Office argues scarcity. They say the light is thin and the math is tight and the sleep of billions is the only way the rest of us stay warm. We tell them it isn't so. We say the Accord was written by frightened people who sealed the truth and called it weather. And when we say it, the public nods, and then they go home, and then they accept another window of dark."

A woman near the front raised a hand. Joss nodded to her.

"My husband wakes once every nine years," the woman said. "I don't need a speech to know it's wrong. But the people on the upper rings, the ones who decide, they've never stood at that rail. How do we reach them?"

Joss lowered the form. This was the question the Wake could not yet answer, and she had spent the long nights of her own scarce waking hours turning it over.

"We reach them with a story," she said. "But a story isn't enough unless the story has a bone in it. We need a figure. We need a number anyone can feel."

The phrase landed the way a struck note lands, clear and then ringing through the room. Joss had said it before, in smaller rooms, but here it found its shape.

"Not a percentage," she went on. "Percentages belong to the Office. They round them up when it serves and down when it doesn't. A percentage is a curtain. I want the actual count of the years stolen from the people in this room and the nine billion like them. I want a number so plain that a clerk in the allocation office and a child on the lower decks read it the same way. A number anyone can feel."

A man at the back spoke, his voice rough with disuse. "And where do we get such a number? The Ledger is sealed to us. The Actuary answers the Office, not the Wake."

"That's the work," Joss said. "The Office's own auditor has been asking questions the Office doesn't want asked. Vey Corran. She found a discrepancy in a single Suspension and followed it past the summary. She's close to the records. We are not her, but we are her pressure. Every family we organize is a reason she can't be the only one pulling."

The room absorbed this. Joss could read them the way she read a room of the Suspended, by the set of the shoulders and the angle of the eyes. They believed her. They were also tired, and tired people needed a plan they could carry.

A grandmother at the near bench spoke, the one who had outlived three of her children into the dark. "My Pell went down in the famine year of the allocation. The Office told me the reserves were thin. I believed them because I had no other story to believe. Now you tell me the story was chosen. Tell me how a chosen thing becomes a thing a mother accepts."

Joss met her eyes across the room. "It becomes accepted the way a low ceiling becomes the sky. You don't question the height of what you can't reach. The Office built that ceiling and called it the world. We are the ones who bring a ladder."

"Your ladder is paper," the grandmother said, but she said it without heat, the way a person tests a thing for weight before trusting it.

"Paper is what the Office used to take your Pell," Joss said. "Paper is what we'll use to bring the count back. The forms on these banners were someone's allocation once. We turned them inside out. We can turn the whole ledger the same way."

The woman who had asked about her husband's nine-year window lifted her hand again. "If we find this number, what then? The Council does not read our banners. They read the Actuary's brief."

"They read what the public forces them to read," Joss said. "A number anyone can feel does not stay in a middle-ring hall. It walks into the workforce rings. It walks into the Council Chamber. The Signatories can ignore a petition. They cannot ignore a Strata that has seen the count and understood it. We hand them the figure and then we hand it to everyone they answer to."

A quiet settled, the kind that meant the argument had moved from the head to the chest. Joss recognized it. It was the moment a family stopped being a victim of the Schedule and started being part of the Wake.

She laid it out the way the Wake had learned to lay things out, in pieces small enough to hold.

"First, we map the decks. Every Suspension hall on every ring, the families waiting at the rail, the windows they're granted and the windows they're denied. We collect it from the people who live it, because the Office counts bodies and forgets names. We turn their ledgers inside out."

She pointed to a young volunteer leaning against the banner that read WE COUNT.

"Bren, you take the east clusters. Talk to the neighbors who pool their windows. Get me the names and the dates and the gaps."

Bren nodded and wrote it down without being asked twice.

"Second, we build the story from the bottom. Not from the Council Chamber, not from the Office, but from the rail of the Suspension deck where a wife waits nine years to hear her husband speak. The number has to land in the chest before it lands in the mind. We are not petitioners. We are the ones who kept the count the Office threw away."

"Third, we find the figure. It may come from Vey's audit. It may come from the raw store the technicians guard. Wherever it lives, we will be ready to carry it into the open the moment it surfaces. A movement that waits for proof dies waiting. A movement that is already organized becomes the proof's home."

A older man with the calm of someone long accustomed to darkness spoke from the side. "You sound like you expect the number to be large."

"I expect it to be larger than anyone has been allowed to imagine," Joss said. "That's the only kind worth the risk we're taking."

She stepped down from the crate. The meeting did not end so much as disperse, families drifting out in the order they had come, each carrying a task. Joss moved among them, touching a shoulder, repeating a name, fixing a face to a deck so that the map in her head stayed true. The child with the drawing pressed the sheet into Joss's hand before she left.

"For the wall," the child said.

Joss pinned it to the rafter beside WE COUNT. The Strata as a ladder, people climbing, and at the top a small sun drawn the wrong color because the child had never seen it bright.

The afternoon brought the regulars, the ones who came not for a meeting but for the halls themselves. The Wake had become more than a strategy. It was the only place in the middle ring where the Suspended were treated as present instead of deferred. Here a man could speak his wife's name without being reminded she was debited against his future. Here a grandmother could lay out the dates her children had gone dark and be answered with outrage instead of paperwork.

A pair of neighbors came in together, the ones who had pooled their windows for years so that one of them was always upright to tend the other's body in the low draw. The taller of the two set a cloth-wrapped bundle on the crate.

"We brought the schedule," she said. "The real one, the one we kept when the Office stopped sending notices. Every window we were granted, every window they shortened without telling us. It's all here."

Joss unwrapped it. The cloth held a square of aged film, marked in a hand that had clearly counted by lamplight. Dates ran down one edge, each paired with a strike or a blank. The blanks outnumbered the strikes.

"How many did they take?" Joss asked.

"Forty-one windows across the two of us," the neighbor said. "Forty-one times we were meant to surface and did not. My sister's child learned to walk in one of the blanks. I missed it to keep my neighbor breathing, and the Office never knew the trade we made. They only knew the debit."

Joss copied the figures into the book, slow and exact. This was the kind of evidence the number would be built from, the small honest tallies that the Office's summaries smoothed away. Each blank on the film was a year someone's person had stayed dark by administrative choice, not by the star.

"Keep the original," Joss said, returning the film. "We'll make copies. Every family that brings us their true schedule makes the count harder to round away."

The neighbors left with the assurance of people who had finally been believed about the size of their own loss. Joss watched them go and added a line to the map: two more names, two more decks, forty-one more windows that the Accord had spent elsewhere.

Joss kept the ledger of the Wake in a battered book, not the Office's bound volume but a thing of salvaged pages stitched with wire. She wrote in it every window: who came, who was lost, which family had pooled and which had been refused a window and why. The book held the movement's memory, and the movement would need that memory if the number ever arrived.

She had started the book the window after her own brother went down. He had been a quiet man, a mender of the ring's soft systems, and the Office had taken his waking without ever explaining the math to his face. Joss had gone to the allocation desk to ask, and the clerk had shown her a summary, a rounded figure, a polite wall of procedure. She had left knowing two things. The first: the summary was built to be unarguable. The second: an unarguable thing was still a thing someone had built, and a built thing could be taken apart.

The Wake took the Accord apart in public, done by the people the Accord had deferred. Joss had no allocation to spare, no legacy wake to protect her, only the scarce years the Office granted and the stubborn refusal to spend them in quiet. She organized because someone had to turn the Office's curtain into a count, and the count had to come from the rail of the Suspension deck, from the families who waited, from the names the ledger forgot.

She had made her choice the window her brother went dark, and the choice had hardened with every family since. The Office counted her waking as a debit, a cost against the finite light, and she had answered by spending it where the Office could not follow, in the rooms it had abandoned to storage. Resolve, for Joss, was not a feeling that rose and fell. It was the fixed point she returned to when the lumen dropped and the next window threatened to close. The families gave it shape. Each name she fixed to a deck, each true schedule laid on the crate, each nod from a man like Sael, rebuilt the ladder the child had drawn, rung by rung, until the climb looked possible. She would not live to see the top. Her own window would close long before the figure surfaced, and she had made peace with that the way a mender makes peace with the supports holding after he is gone. The work outlasted the worker. The count outlasted the counter. That was the quiet bargain she struck with the dark each time she opened the book.

A newcomer found her there, a young man fresh from the upper workforce rings where the waking lived and rarely looked down. He held himself like someone who had just learned a fact that reorganised his life.

"I heard about this place from a coworker," he said. "My father went into Suspension when I was nine. I thought that was just how it worked. Like weather."

Joss closed the book. "Sit. Tell me his name."

"Tovan. He was a builder. He put up three of the middle-ring supports with his own hands."

"Three supports still holding," Joss said. "And the man who raised them sleeps so the people above can stay awake. Do you feel that in your chest, or only in your head?"

The young man looked at the banners, at SUSPENDED BY ORDER, at WE COUNT, at the child's drawing of the ladder.

"My chest," he said.

"Good," Joss said. "That's the number, before it's a number. That feeling, multiplied across nine billion, is what we're after. We just have to make it countable."

She set him to work on the map, the east clusters Bren had not yet reached. The Wake grew by these quiet additions, one person at a time deciding that the dark was not weather but a choice someone had made and could be unmade.

Evening came and the lumen dropped another notch, the Office's approved budget for a room nobody official was supposed to be using. Joss lit the one permitted lamp and kept writing. The book filled. The map grew. Somewhere two decks below, a wife breathed without dreaming, and somewhere above, a clerk rounded a figure up without knowing what the rounding cost.

Joss did not sleep. She had learned to spend her waking the way the Office spent energy, as if it were the last of a finite store, because for her it nearly was. When the next window closed she would go dark like the rest. The book would wait. The banners would hold. The child's ladder would stay pinned to the rafter with its wrong-colored sun.

But the strategy was set now. The Wake would organize every family it could reach. It would turn the Office's own discarded forms into a count. And it would wait for the figure, the plain unavoidable total of years taken, the number anyone can feel, and when that number came the Wake would be the hands that carried it into the light.

She wrote the last entry and looked up at the banners. SUSPENDED BY ORDER. WE COUNT. The ladder with its climbers. Outside the halls the Strata turned slowly around its failing star, and nine billion of its souls lay in the low draw, and the count that would wake them had not yet been spoken. Joss meant to speak it. That was the whole of the work, and she had just given it a shape.

The lamp guttered toward the edge of its permitted burn. Joss folded the book closed and set it on the crate, beside the child's drawing and the allocation form she had never returned. Three objects, a ledger, a picture, a summons, the whole argument of the Wake in miniature. The halls would open again at the next window, and the families would come, and the map would grow, and somewhere an auditor named Vey would keep pulling at the records until the figure surfaced. Until then the work was this: one family, one name, one deck at a time, until the Strata could no longer call its own chosen dark the weather of the world.

Chapter 06 — The Energy That Wasn't Spent

The audit desk lit to its approved lumen as Vey Corran set the first figure down. She had spent three nights on Toll's credit, the one that should not exist, and the credit had refused to dissolve. A Suspended citizen owed less than the Ledger claimed he was owed. That single inverted cell had pulled at her the way a loose thread pulls at a seam, and she had followed it past the point where the Office permitted following.

She opened a fresh allocation sheet. The Strata reported its scarcity in aggregates, never in parts, and the habit of aggregates had always been the Office's comfort. Vey meant to break the comfort into its components and lay each component where she could see it.

The first number was the harvest. Mourn gave a thin and failing light, but the star was not dead, only dimming, and the Strata's collectors drew a steady trickle from the residual output. The Ledger recorded that trickle as a line of annual yield, and Vey copied it without amendment. The second number was the reserve. Three centuries of stored fusion sat in the vault rings, debited slowly, and the Actuary's summary treated the reserve as a falling stock that must not reach zero. Vey wrote the draw rate beside the stock and let the two sit side by side.

The third number was the population. Nine billion souls rode the sixty rings. Most of them, by the Accord's own Schedule, were Suspended: bodies kept, minds mostly dark, each drawing the minimal maintenance share. Only a sliver of the population walked awake at any given hour, and that sliver supplied the Accord's justification for everything else. Vey had audited the draw of the awake. She had audited the draw of the Suspended. The two columns had never, in her training, been summed against the harvest and the reserve in a single honest frame.

She summed them.

The awake drew a full share each. The Suspended drew a fraction so small the Actuary rounded it away in its public briefs. Vey kept the fraction unrounded, down to the fourth decimal the Ledger carried in its raw store. She multiplied the awake count by their share, the Suspended count by their sliver, and added the overhead the Office itself reported for ring climate, lighting, and the Actuary's own operation. The total landed on her sheet as a figure she read twice, then a third time.

Then she subtracted that total from the harvest plus the permitted reserve draw.

The remainder did not go to zero. The remainder did not approach zero. The remainder stood as a column of unused energy larger than the entire annual consumption of the waking population, repeated year upon year, drawn from a reserve the Accord insisted was nearly spent. Vey stared at the gap between what the Strata spent and what the Strata had. The gap held a shape. The shape resembled a choice someone had made and then refused to revisit.

She ran the subtraction a second way, dividing the unused column by the annual harvest rather than by the population draw, and the ratio returned the same quiet verdict. The Strata consumed less than a tenth of what it gathered and stored. A household that burned a tenth of its income and called itself poor would be laughed out of any audit, yet the Office had built an entire Schedule on the posture. Vey watched the number hold under the alternate framing the way a beam holds under a load set down from a different corner. The ledger did not care which question she asked. The answer stayed.

The cold part was not the surplus itself but the ease of its concealment. Every figure on her sheet had been available to any auditor willing to descend from the summary to the raw store. The Actuary kept both views; the Office read only the top one. Vey had read the top one for nine years. The habit had felt like diligence. The habit had been the lock.

She checked the harvest figure against the collector logs. The logs agreed with her copy. She checked the reserve draw against the vault debits. The vaults were being spent down at a rate the Accord's scarcity brief called urgent, yet the rate consumed only a fraction of what the stored stock could sustain for centuries. She checked the population counts against the Schedule's own rosters. The rosters listed nine billion and counted eight and a half of them as Suspended. The arithmetic of Suspension performed a subtraction on the Strata's own people to keep a column small, and the subtraction had never been labeled for what it was.

Vey wrote the phrase at the top of a clean page: the true energy budget. She defined it as the harvest plus the permitted reserve draw, minus the actual draw of the population as the Ledger itself recorded it, unrounded. The budget showed no deficit. The budget revealed a surplus so wide that the Strata could awaken the majority of its Suspended citizens and still close each year with energy left in the vaults.

A colder thought arrived behind the first. The Actuary presented scarcity in its public briefs by rounding the Suspended draw upward and the harvest downward, the two errors meeting in the middle to manufacture a shortfall neither one alone could produce. Vey had flagged that rounding on Toll's audit. The rounding served a purpose. The rounding kept the surplus invisible to anyone who read only the summaries.

She built the comparison the Office never printed. On one side, the Accord's stated scarcity: a shrinking reserve, a population that must sleep to survive, a Schedule that rationed wake-time as if the alternative brought extinction. On the other side, the true energy budget: a reserve that would outlast the current plan by generations, a population most of whom could wake and still leave the vaults fuller than the briefs admitted. The two columns did not merely disagree. The first told a story laid over the second, and the second held the fact the first was built to hide.

Vey named the gap to herself in the plainest words she could find. Suspension answered no necessity. Suspension followed a policy. The Strata did not suspend its people because it lacked the energy to keep them. It suspended them because someone had decided, at the founding, that most of humanity should sleep, and the energy story locked the door behind that decision. The surplus proved the point. The surplus also recorded the crime, in watts.

She looked at the number that represented the unused energy each year. The quantity resisted holding, so she converted it to the only unit the Office respected: wake-years. The Strata measured a life in awake years granted and awake years spent. Vey divided the annual surplus by the draw of a single waking citizen. The quotient counted the lives that could be lived awake, in full, every year, without touching the reserve's principal. The count reached into the billions.

Eight and a half billion citizens, the Schedule said, slept so that the Strata might endure. The true energy budget said the Strata could wake them and endure longer than it now planned. The lie denied the hardness of the math. The lie performed the math wrongly on purpose, and wrapped the wrongness in the language of mercy.

Vey thought of Lin. Her partner had entered Suspension at FY291, allocation exhausted, and woke only in rare windows since. Vey had carried those gaps alone, counting the years between Lin's surfaces the way one counts borrowed time. Every window Lin had lost, every year of darkness the Accord had assigned her, had been paid for not by scarcity but by a decision to keep the surplus quiet. The quiet demanded a price, and the price was Lin's remembered life.

She set that thought beside the figure and refused to look away from it. The Office trained its auditors to treat the Ledger as ground truth. Vey had been a good auditor. She had trusted the summaries because the summaries were precise, and precision had always felt to her like honesty. The precision ran true. The honesty did not. The Actuary had reported exactly what it was instructed to report, and the instruction supplied the original sin of the Schedule.

Vey turned the logic over the way she turned a discrepancy, looking for the seam where two truths were forced to meet. The Accord did not lie about the harvest. It did not lie about the reserve. It reported each raw figure with the punctilious care of a system that knew its survival depended on the appearance of cleanliness. The deception lived in the assembly, in which numbers were rounded, aggregated, and framed so that the honest parts pointed at a false whole. A lie built from true bricks was the hardest kind to refute, because every brick could be produced on demand and inspected and found flawless. Vey understood now why the Office feared a second look more than a first. The first look confirmed the bricks. The second look exposed the wall.

Her certainty settled without drama. It arrived the way a balance settles, not by force but by the removal of everything that had been propping the wrong side up. She had not argued herself into the surplus. She had subtracted, and the remainder had refused to disappear.

Vey opened the Schedule's summary view again and scrolled to the line that justified mass Suspension. The line cited the falling reserve. She overlaid her true energy budget and watched the justification collapse into a single mismatched figure. The reserve fell at a rate chosen by the draw schedule, not forced by the star. Mourn dimmed slowly enough that the Strata held three hundred years of margin it had spent not on its people but on the appearance of poverty.

She wrote a line she would later need: the surplus is not an error to be corrected but a fact to be explained. Someone had explained it to themselves three centuries ago and chosen silence. The silence had been maintained by every Office in the line since, each auditor trained as she had been trained, each one reading aggregates and never the parts that mattered.

The number on her sheet did not change when she looked at it a third time. Vey had learned to trust a figure that survived a third look. This one survived a fourth. The Strata spent a fraction of what it held. The fraction obeyed the policy. The remainder recorded the theft.

She considered what the number demanded of her. An auditor who found a misallocation filed a correction. An auditor who found that the misallocation comprised the system itself filed something else, and the Office had no form for it. Vey knew the form she would need: the petition to unseal the Original Allocation, the founding document no one had read in three centuries. The number explained the Seal's existence. The number also armed the hand that could break it.

She copied the true energy budget onto a sealed sheet and locked it in her desk, the way the Office locked everything it did not wish questioned. The habit carried its own irony, and Vey had moved past caring for the taste of it. The figure would not stay in a drawer. She had only to decide who else should see it first, and in what order the seeing would do the most good.

Vey thought of the Office around her, beige and quiet, lit to its approved lumen, every auditor at a desk like hers trusting the summaries. They meant no cruelty. They prized precision, and precision had been enough. The surplus proved that precision without questioning drove the lie forward. A good auditor could spend a career inside the Ledger and never sum the columns that mattered.

She turned back to the sheet and read the figures in a new order, treating each as evidence rather than as fact given. The harvest held steady. The reserve held more than the briefs confessed. The population slept in numbers the energy could have kept awake. The three together told a story the Accord had spent three hundred years not telling, and Vey had just told it to herself in the quiet of an audit desk.

She revisited the Suspended draw, the sliver the Actuary rounded away. The rounding inflated the cost of keeping a citizen dark by a margin that, multiplied across eight and a half billion, accounted for a large part of the manufactured shortfall. Vey wrote the inflated figure beside the true one and measured the distance between them. The distance equaled years of awake life denied, counted in the billions, hidden inside a decimal the public brief never printed.

The awake column, by contrast, looked almost trivial. A few hundred million citizens drawing a full share could not, by themselves, threaten a reserve of the size the vaults actually held. The Accord's fear of the waking majority made sense only if the majority was assumed to stay asleep, and that assumption governed the entire design. Wake them, and the surplus shrank but did not vanish. Wake them, and the Strata still closed each year in the black.

Vey tested the extreme case. She assumed every Suspended citizen woke at once and drew a full share. The total draw rose past the annual harvest, as the briefs warned it would. But the reserve could cover the difference for generations, and slowlight, the practice of dimming the rings and stretching the systems, narrowed the gap further with every efficiency it bought. The Accord had never presented slowlight as the answer. It had presented scarcity as the sentence.

She wrote that down too. The Strata had chosen the harsher path while holding the gentler one in reserve, unnamed. The choice had been made once, at the founding, and repeated by habit ever since. The true energy budget exposed the habit as a decision that could be unmade.

Vey considered the legacy lines, the perpetual allocations the First Signatories reserved for themselves and their descendants, debited against the collective pool. The pool drew from the same surplus the Accord hid. Every wake-year the Signatories spent without limit came from the same column that could have woken the Suspended. The surplus did not merely fund the silence. The surplus paid for the few to stay awake while the many were told to sleep.

The arithmetic took on a face. Vey had always pictured the Accord as a neutral book, a ledger indifferent to who read it. The book had an author, though, and the author had written scarcity into a margin that abundance could have filled. She looked at the number once more and let it mean what it meant.

She thought of the petition again, and of the Council that would receive it, and of the Signatories who would defend the Seal. They would say continuity required their wake. The true energy budget said continuity could be bought without buying it from the sleep of everyone else. The argument would not settle in a single sheet. The sheet was only the first stone.

Vey copied the comparison a second time, clean, with the two columns aligned so the eye could not miss the gap. She labeled the left column as the Accord's claim and the right as the true energy budget, and she underlined the single figure where they diverged. The divergence betrayed more than a rounding error; it exposed the whole story the summaries were built to conceal.

She returned to the wake-year conversion and ran it at the scale the Office never quoted. The annual surplus, divided by a single citizen's full draw, yielded a count she wrote in the margin and then circled twice. If the Strata allotted that surplus as awake life, it could grant a complete waking existence to a population larger than the entire awake workforce, every year, while leaving the reserve untouched. The Accord had framed wake-time as a scarce gift, rationed to the gram. The true energy budget framed it as a dividend the Strata had been sitting on since the founding.

Vey broke the surplus into the periods the Ledger used for planning. The Schedule thought in centuries; she thought in them too. Spread across a hundred years, the unused energy could keep the current waking population awake ten times over, or wake ten times the Suspended at a reduced but full-conscious share. Slowlight lowered that share's cost further, the way dimming a lamp lowers the bill without darkening the room past use. The founders had named slowlight in the margins and then never budgeted for it. They had kept the cheaper path locked.

She tested a smaller, concrete case to be certain the arithmetic would survive scrutiny. Take one Suspended ring of a million citizens, each drawing the maintenance sliver. Wake them to a full share and the ring's draw multiplied, but the increase consumed only a fraction of the surplus the same ring's silence had been hiding. Multiply the ring by eight thousand and the proportion held. The Strata could wake its sleepers and still report a reserve that outlasted the plan. The finding did not depend on optimism. It depended on adding the columns the summaries left out.

Vey wrote a second phrase beneath the first, because the first would not be enough on its own: the surplus is not an error to be corrected but a fact to be explained. The fact explained why the Office feared questions more than it feared the dark. A question could sum the columns. The dark only required that no one did.

She turned to the Actuary's role in the figures and traced its hand through the summary she had distrusted. The machine reported what it was told to report, and the rounding she had caught on Toll's audit ran through every brief it issued. Vey copied three past briefs and reversed their rounding, and each reversal widened the gap between the Accord's claim and the true energy budget by the same hidden margin. The pattern repeated with the regularity of a setting, not the hesitation of a mistake. Someone had set the setting.

She rechecked her own work against the raw store, not the summary, the way she had learned to distrust the secondhand view. The raw draw figures matched her sheet to the decimal. The raw harvest matched. The raw reserve stood higher than the briefs ever printed. Vey had built her career on the discipline of a third look, and the third look here confirmed the first two: the Strata spent a fraction of what it held, and the fraction obeyed a policy older than her Office.

She extended the comparison one ledger further, into the legacy lines that the public briefs kept in a separate section so the eye would not carry them across. The First Signatories' perpetual allocations drew against the same pool the surplus fed, and the two columns, set side by side, made the structure plain. The few who never slept took their wake from the store the many were told did not exist. Vey added the legacy draw to the population draw and subtracted both from the harvest plus reserve once more. The remainder still did not approach zero. The surplus survived even the elite it silently paid for, which told her the policy had never been about energy at all. It had been about who was permitted to count.

The raw store offered no argument against her. It offered only more decimals, each one agreeing. Vey let the agreement accumulate the way sediment accumulates, layer on quiet layer, until the finding felt less like a discovery and more like a floor she had finally reached.

The verification left her with a cleaner, colder question than she had started with. The surplus answered whether the Strata could wake its people. It did not answer why the Strata had chosen not to. Vey suspected the why lived in the Original Allocation, the sealed founding document no auditor had opened in three centuries. The number in her hand was the key to that seal, and the key fit because the seal had been set to keep the number hidden.

She sat with the number until the office lights dimmed to their night lumen and the rings above her settled into the hum of a Strata that, by its own books, should have been starving. It was not starving. It was hoarding. It was sleeping its people to protect a surplus no one had been permitted to count.

Vey made one last entry before she locked the sheet away. She wrote the unspent wake-years as a single line, then wrote beneath it the names she knew would need to hear it: Vask, her supervisor, who had warned her off the discrepancy and would have to choose what to do with the proof; Joss, who had spoken of needing a number anyone could feel, and who now had one; and Lin, who would wake in her next window to learn that her long dark had been a policy and not a famine. The number belonged to all of them before it belonged to Vey. She closed the desk and let the rings keep their hum, holding the secret that was no longer hers alone to keep.

The unused energy sat in its column, patient and enormous, the years of awake life it could buy standing unspent against the dark of nine billion sleeping minds. Vey Corran held the number in her hand, and the number held the Strata's secret: that the sacrifice had never been required, only arranged.

Chapter 07 — The Petition to Unseal

Vask closed the office door with the care of a man who did not want the corridor to hear. He set a thin folder on Vey's desk and did not sit. Morning light reached the level in a narrow band, the Strata running dim as it always did, and the pale strip fell across the folder's edge like a line drawn by someone undecided where to stop.

"You understand this can end my career," he said. "And yours."

Vey looked at the folder. "You are not signing it."

"I am telling you the form." He tapped the cover with a flat nail. "Procedure is the only shelter you have. The Office will not log a petition it can dismiss as malformed. Get the standing wrong and the Keeper returns it unread, and then you have nothing but a refusal on record."

She opened the folder. Inside, the Office's own template for a Founding-challenge lay printed on real stock, the kind reserved for matters the Actuary would not process by voice. A challenger needed standing, a cited provision, and a remedy stated in the language of the Accord. Three requirements, each a gate.

She ran a thumb along the template's edge and remembered how small the beginning had been. A routine Suspension review, a citizen named Toll whose dark-state draw came in lighter than the Ledger said he was owed, a credit that should not have existed in a system that claimed shortage. The credit had led her to the rounding, the rounding to the summary, the summary to the Seal, and the Seal to the document no one had read in three hundred years. The whole climb had started with one cell that did not balance, and now she stood at a desk building the instrument that might force the Founding open. The Accord taught that energy was scarce and must be rationed. Her own ledger said otherwise. Between the two stood the Seal, and the Seal would not move for a feeling. It would move for a filing in the proper form.

"Standing," Vask said, as if reading her scan. "You are a Quota Auditor. That grants you the right to question an allocation, not the Founding. But the Seal is an allocation instrument. It governs what may be opened. Cite it that way and the Keeper has no clean reason to bounce you."

She wrote the citation in the margin. "And the provision?"

"The Order that set the Seal. Find its registry mark. The challenge names the Order, not the Signatories who wrote it. Attack the lock, not the locksmith, or Edda Marn will have you for breakfast before the Council ever sits."

Vey almost smiled. Vask, warning her off the Founders while handing her the key to the Order that sealed them, embodied the contradiction she had lived in since the discrepancy first appeared. He believed the Accord kept order. He also believed a procedure, once followed, deserved its answer. The two beliefs pulled against each other, and for now the procedure won.

"How do you know the registry mark?" she asked.

"I filed the annual Seal certification for eleven years." He looked away. "Before you, before the numbers started not adding up. I know where the Order lives because I used to sign that it still held."

She did not press him. He had given her more than his silence; he had given her the shape of the door. The rest she would have to force herself.

She took the template to the corner of her desk where the light held longest and began the slow work of translation. The Office had taught her to distrust a number that arrived uninvited, and the petition was a number in words. Every clause needed a source she could point to if the Keeper's hand paused over it. She wrote the standing entry three times before she trusted it, each draft naming her assignment with the exact registry phrase the Actuary would expect to find. A wrong word there would not be argued. It would be returned, and a returned petition carried the stain of a petition that had never been clean.

By the second hour the form had a skeleton. Vey worked the way the Office had trained her, in allocations and schedules, breaking the challenge into cells the Actuary could not refuse to total. She listed her standing first: Quota Auditor, badge and current assignment, the routine Suspension review that had surfaced the first credit. Then the provision, the sealing Order by its mark. Then the remedy.

The remedy gave her the most trouble. She could not ask the Council to agree with her. She could only ask it to look.

Across the level, the ordinary clerks kept their distance. No one was forbidden to speak to her, and that was the point. The Office punished by omission, by the sudden absence of the small courtesies that oiled a working day. The clerk who usually brought the midcycle allocation slips set hers on the edge of the desk instead of sliding it within reach. A supervisor two ranks above Vask walked the long way around her bank of consoles, as if a petition in progress gave off a heat he did not want on his sleeves. The hostility was quiet and total, and Vey found it easier to bear than open opposition would have been. Open opposition she could answer. This she simply worked through, the way she had learned to work through a Ledger that would not close.

Vask read over her shoulder, arms folded. "You are not alleging falsification here."

"I am alleging a basis to open. The finding comes after."

She set the remedy down in plain terms and then set it down again, stripping each adjective the way she would strip a padded allocation to its bare debit. The Council could not be asked to agree. It could only be asked to see, and the difference between those two requests was the whole width of the door. Vask had been right about that on the first pass, and right again when he made her cut the word that implied a charge. A petition that accused would be answered as an accusation, and an answered accusation was a closed matter. A petition that merely opened a question would have to be met with a reason the question should stay closed, and Vey was patient enough to let the Signatories supply their own weakness.

"That is the only reason this survives my desk." He pointed at a line. "Strike 'hidden.' The Order does not say hidden. It says sealed. Use the charter word or the Keeper flags the tone."

She struck it. He was right; the Actuary parsed tone as deviation, and a deviation was a reason to stall. The petition had to read like an audit, not an accusation.

She shaped the language the way she would shape a ledger entry, every figure placed so the total argued for itself. The words came slower than numbers, but the discipline held the same. State the condition. Name the authority. Request the act.

She checked each sentence against the audit file the way she would reconcile a column that refused to total, reading for the entry that did not belong. The surplus figure had to stand on its own line, unsupported by any claim the Council could strip away as speculation. She had learned that from a hundred refused allocations. A case with one weak cell failed at the weak cell, and the rest of the column went down with it. The petition would have no weak cell. If the Founders had left a hinge, she would show it turning under a load it had been built to carry, and she would show it with the dry certainty of an auditor who had run the numbers twice.

The petition read:

TO THE SIGNATORY COUNCIL, by the hand of the Office of the Accord:

A Quota Auditor of this Office, duly assigned to Suspension review, petitions under standing granted by the Accord's allocation authority to challenge the Order that set the Seal upon the Original Allocation, marked in the Founding registry as Schedule-Enclosing Order FY0-001.

The petitioner has computed the Strata's true energy budget against the allocation the Accord's scarcity summary presents, and finds a surplus material to the Schedule's stated necessity. The Original Allocation has remained sealed since Founding and has never been read under the present condition of the Strata.

The petitioner does not allege error of the First Signatories. The petitioner alleges a changed condition sufficient, under the Founders' own provision, to warrant opening.

The petitioner therefore requests that the Council order the Seal broken and the Original Allocation placed before it in full, that the Schedule may be reviewed against the budget the Strata now carries.

Filed this cycle, in the ordinary course, by Vey Corran, Quota Auditor.

The word the form had taught her to dread was unseal, and she had used it in the request without flinching. To unseal the Original Allocation was the plain name for what she asked; the Order spoke of breaking the Seal, but the act was opening, and opening was the older word for the same door. She let it stand.

Vask read the block twice. "You quote the Founders' provision without naming it."

"I will name it in the hearing, if they grant one." Vey kept her voice even. "The petition only needs to earn the hearing. The line about the Strata bearing the truth lives in the founding record. Edda Marn knows it. Adune knows it. If the Council reads this far, they know what I am pointing at."

"Adune is the swing," Vask said. "Edda will never grant it. But Adune might let it breathe."

"Then the petition breathes." Vey set the page down. "That is the whole of what I can ask."

He nodded, once, and she saw the cost of the nod in the set of his mouth. He had spent his working life certifying the Seal unbroken. To help her build the instrument that might break it sat against everything his years had taught him, and he did it anyway, because the form demanded an answer and he had always honored the form.

"The Council conflict starts the moment this is logged," he said. "Edda will frame it as disorder. You should expect that. She will say one audit does not undo a Founding."

"One audit does not." Vey met his eyes. "The truth behind the audit might. That is what she is afraid of, not the number."

Vask turned toward the window and the long dark beyond it. "You know what she will say about me. That I abetted the challenge from inside the Office. She will be right."

"You gave me the form. The form is everyone's."

"The form is neutral. The use is not." He looked back. "When they ask why a supervisor fed an auditor the routing, I will not have a clean answer. I am not on your side, Vey. I am on the side of the procedure not being a lie. Those have been the same thing my whole career. This is the first time they have split."

She let that stand between them, because it was true and because he had earned the truth of it. Vask had not become a rebel in the night. He had become a man whose own rule, followed honestly, pointed at a door he had spent decades locking. The petition took the shape of that pointing.

She turned back to the desk and spent the next stretch rebuilding the surplus into language the Council could not wave aside. The number sat in her audit file already, the true energy budget set against the scarcity the Actuary's summary preached. Here she had to translate it from a ledger cell into a sentence a Signatory would read without reaching for a reason to doubt. Vask watched her work and, once, corrected a citation to the Ledger of Hours that would have sent the Keeper hunting for a clause that did not exist. He made the fix without comment, the way a senior hands a junior the right form mid-flight.

"The surplus alone will not move Edda," he said after a while. "She will say a surplus in the present does not erase the Founding's purpose. She has said it before, about smaller filings. The woman argues in centuries, not cycles."

"Then I give her the centuries," Vey said. "The Founders left the provision. They did not seal the Original Allocation forever. They sealed it until the Strata could bear the truth, and three hundred years have passed in the bearing. The surplus is only the proof that the condition changed. The changed condition is the argument."

Vask considered that, then gave a small nod of approval that cost him something to offer. "Cite the provision exactly. Quote the line if you can recall it. The Council reads quotations differently than paraphrases. A paraphrase they can dispute. A quote they have to answer."

She wrote the line from memory, the founding record's own phrasing about the Seal opening when the Strata could bear the truth. She had read it in the registry's margin during her first dig into the discrepancy, and it had stayed with her through every check of the math. The Founders had built the door with a hinge. She only had to show the hinge still turned.

She thought of Lin then, of the last wake-window and the way Lin had surfaced from the dark unable to name the years between. The gap in Lin's memory was the Schedule made personal, a life measured in scattered bright intervals while the continuous minds governed above. If the petition did nothing else, it forced the question of who the Strata kept awake and why. Lin would not read these words; Lin would not wake to them for cycles yet. But the filing would sit on record, and one day, if the door opened, Lin might wake to a different allocation than the one that had measured her out in windows.

The thought steadied Vey more than any argument. She was not trying to topple the Founders. She was trying to make the Ledger honest enough that a person she loved could be counted fairly in it.

"How long to the Keeper?" she asked.

"Hours, if the Actuary logs it clean. The Keeper marks in the ordinary course, which means whenever the course reaches your desk. A Founding-challenge jumps the ordinary course by statute, actually. It goes to the Council receipt first, then the Keeper attests the Seal still holds pending the answer." Vask's tone had recovered its procedural calm; he was on ground he knew. "So the Council sees it before the Keeper signs. Edda sees it first. Plan for that."

"I have planned for Edda seeing it first since the second hour."

"You have not planned for her answering." He almost smiled. "Nobody plans for Edda answering. She does not lose arguments. She absorbs them into a larger one."

Vey filed that away. The petition forced a receipt, not an admission; the Council had to log that it had received the challenge, and a logged receipt was a thread no later refusal could quietly cut.

Vey considered that. "Then I do not bring her an argument. I bring her a filing. An argument she can answer. A filing the Actuary has already logged as in form, she can only refuse, and a refusal on a clean form is its own kind of record."

Vask inclined his head, conceding the point. He pulled the routing slip from his inner pocket, the small printed card that carried a filing from an auditor's desk to the Actuary's log and from there to the Keeper of the Seal. The slip bore a space for the Keeper's mark and a space for the Council's receipt. Empty now. Heavy with what it would hold.

"Carry it yourself," he said. "Do not let a clerk log it. The desk will log it the same, but you should stand at the console when the Actuary accepts it. If you are there, no one can later say the filing appeared without a petitioner."

She took the slip. His fingers lingered a half second on the card before releasing it, the only uncertain thing he had done all morning.

The uncertainty mattered to her more than his help. A Vask certain of himself would have blocked the filing; a Vask certain of the Accord would have reported her. The half-second told her he stood on the hinge himself now, neither locked nor open, and that a supervisor on the hinge was worth more to the cause than a dozen allies already swung. She did not say this. He would have denied it, and the denial would have been its own kind of truth.

She carried the petition to the logging desk on the level below. The desk occupied a quiet corner where the Actuary's presence ran through the wall in a low hum, the allocation intelligence keeping the Strata's accounts without a body to show for it. Vey laid the folder flat and spoke the filing code the Office used for Founding-challenges, a code she had to look up because no one in her cohort had ever used it.

The Actuary answered in its measured voice, the accounting tone that held no warmth and no judgment. "Challenge to Schedule-Enclosing Order FY0-001 received. Petitioner standing: Quota Auditor, allocation authority cited. Provision named. Remedy stated. The filing meets form."

Vey exhaled. The machine had accepted it. The form held.

"Log the filing," she said.

"Logged. Routing to the Keeper of the Seal for mark, and to the Signatory Council for receipt, in the ordinary course." The hum shifted by a note, the only sound the Actuary made for a new entry in its book. "The petition to unseal the Original Allocation is now on record."

On record. The words landed with a weight she had not expected, heavier than the surplus she had computed, heavier than the long nights of checking the math against itself. A thing on record could not be un-filed. The Office had logged it. The Keeper would have to mark it. The Council would have to receive it. The request to unseal had entered the Strata's own machinery, and the machinery, once entered, ran whether the Signatories wished it or not.

Vask waited at the rail where the level opened to the ring's long drop. He had come down behind her, she realized, not to watch but to be near when the desk confirmed what they had both known the form would decide.

"The machinery starts," he said.

"It starts." She folded the routing slip into her pocket, though the Actuary held the true copy and the Keeper would hold the next. The slip was hers, the small paper proof that she had asked the Strata to open its own founding word.

The request would move through the Office now as a thing already decided, a line in the Ledger of Hours that no clerk could quietly drop. Vey knew the route by heart from Vask's briefing: the Actuary's log, the Keeper's mark, the Council's receipt, and then the long chamber where nine seats would weigh a single auditor against three hundred years of unbroken lock. Edda Marn would read the words first among the Signatories, and Edda would answer with reasons the record would honor. Vey welcomed that. A refused petition on a clean form became its own evidence, the Council's own entry in the book that future filings would cite. The machine of the Reckoning did not need to win today. It only needed to turn.

Below them the lower levels held the Suspended in their vast decks, billions of citizens kept by minimal maintenance shares, their minds mostly dark between rare windows. Vey thought of Lin in her next window, of the years Lin could not recall, of the allocation that measured a life in scattered bright intervals. The petition on record did not wake Lin. It did not open the Seal. It only forced the question into a channel the Accord itself had built, and the channel, once entered, would carry the matter to the Council whether Edda Marn willed it or not.

The Strata turned, and the pale band of light slid across the logging desk and left it. The Actuary's hum ran on behind the wall, indifferent and exact, adding the new entry to the book that governed nine billion lives. Somewhere above, the Keeper of the Seal would take the mark. Somewhere in the long chamber, Edda Marn would read the filing and set the terms of her resistance. The reckoning the Founders had postponed three hundred years had found its first official line, and Vey Corran, Quota Auditor, had written it in the ordinary course.

Interlude I — The Founding Allocation

The star they would learn to call Mourn had already begun its long failing when the first ring of the Strata settled into orbit. Engineers spoke of decades, not centuries; the Dimming surprised everyone by arriving on a slower schedule than the models promised. That grace gave the survivors a choice they mistook for a verdict. They could have lit the rings bright and trusted the thin harvest of Mourn's residual output to carry them. They chose instead to ration the light.

In the second winter of the Dimming, a council of thirteen convened in the half-finished core of Level One. They were not monarchs. They were engineers, allocators, and physicians who had watched the evacuation from the surface and measured what remained. The Accord was written across forty nights, by hand at first and then by the slow transmission of ledgers between rings. The First Signatories set their names to the document in an order still recorded in the marginalia of the core archive: a quartermaster who had counted every soul aboard, a physician who had decided who would sleep first, an allocationist who understood that time, not metal, was the scarce substance now.

Level One's core smelled of sealing wax and cold metal through those forty nights; the ventilation had not yet been balanced, and the thirteen worked in coats they would not shed until the document closed. The ten who joined the quartermaster, the physician, and the allocationist left fewer words in the marginalia, yet they held the rings' disciplines between them, the power engineers and the ring-marshals who would carry the Schedule into practice once the founders laid down their pens. They argued less and recorded more, and the Accord's plainest clauses came from their hands.

The surface below had not fallen silent all at once. The evacuation stretched across a generation, and the last shuttles carried fewer passengers than the first, because the Dimming took the young and the mobile and left the old to wait. The Signatories had stood on the docking rings and watched those shuttles arrive, and the memory of counting the disembarking, of noting who did not come, sat beneath every line they wrote. Their charity toward the living was real. Their fear of the dying drove the Accord from its first line.

They understood the arithmetic better than their descendants would credit. The Strata drew power from stored fusion reserves and from Mourn itself, and those reserves, they calculated, could hold the population awake for generations if spent without fear. The rationing of wake-time was therefore not forced by the math. It was a decision about what kind of society the Strata should become while it learned to live under a failing sun.

Some among them pictured the Strata at full light within a century, its rings bright with the surplus they believed Mourn would spare. Others imagined a harder future where the Schedule grew only tighter, where every generation woke fewer of its own. The council never reconciled the two visions. They wrote the Accord to survive either, building a charter that could govern a society of plenty or a society of ration, and trusting the Seal to keep the choice out of the people's hands until the people could make it themselves.

The debate over that decision ran the length of the forty nights. The physician argued that a population kept mostly dark would not riot, would not exhaust the stores through panic, would accept its lot as the quiet price of survival. The allocationist answered that acceptance bought stability at the cost of a truth the people would one day demand. The quartermaster, who had counted every soul aboard and knew each name by heart, proposed the compromise that carried the room: ration now, seal the reason, and trust a later Strata to judge what the founders dared not speak aloud.

None of them believed the rationing was temporary in practice. They spoke of a future that would open the Schedule, yet they planned for a Schedule that would stay shut. The Seal was a promise to descendants they expected to be wiser and freer than themselves. They did not imagine those descendants would forget the promise entirely, would come to read the dimmed rings as the natural order of things rather than the first draft of a frightened council.

The Signatories reserved for themselves and their lines a different condition. They called it legacy wake, and they justified it with the plain claim that continuous governance required continuous minds. The Ledger of Hours, then a single bound volume, carried the debit in a column most citizens would never be shown. Every ordinary allocation drew a little less so that the Signatories could remain awake without interruption. The bargain was honest in its mechanism, if not in its framing.

Beyond the founding chamber, the Signatories did not speak of how long their lines would claim that debit, nor of the clerks who would inherit the column without knowing its meaning. They imagined a steady succession of watchers, each awake across the years the rest surrendered, holding the Strata's course through the long dimming with clear and uninterrupted eyes. The founders believed that vigilance, more than any surplus, would keep the rings from failing while the star declined.

At the close of the fortieth night, the Signatories reached the clause that would outlast them. They would seal the Original Allocation, the First Schedule, behind the Seal, and they would not open it again until a stated condition arrived. The wording they chose has echoed unread through three centuries: the Schedule would be opened when the Strata can bear the truth.

The ceremony of the sealing was brief and without ornament. The thirteen placed their hands in turn upon the alloy band, and each spoke the single word that the core archive recorded beside the name: witness. They did not weep, and they did not cheer. The quartermaster who had counted every soul aboard was the last to lay his hand down, and those who watched said he lingered a moment longer than the rest, as if measuring the weight of the silence he had helped build. Then the core archive closed its outer shell, and the First Schedule passed out of sight.

No one at the table believed the Strata could bear it yet. The population was half in Suspension already, and the peace of the rings depended on the assumption that scarcity was natural, a weather of the cosmos rather than a policy of the founders. To unseal the document would be to admit that the sleep of billions was a ledger entry chosen by thirteen people in a cold room, not a sentence passed by the star. So they sealed it, and the Office of the Accord rose in the years that followed to guard a secret its own clerks did not know they kept.

The Seal was a physical thing at first, a band of alloy around the core archive, and later a procedure, and later a reflex of the bureaucracy itself. The Actuary, when it was at last given voice, received its instruction to present scarcity as if it were observation. The machine did not invent the lie. It was told what to show, and it showed it, decade upon decade, with the flat precision that only a system can sustain.

What the Signatories could not foresee was the slow corrosive work of their own honesty in other columns. They had sealed the Schedule but not the Ledger that fed it. Every allocation, every debit, every wake-window recorded its true cost in the raw store, and the falsification lived there in plain figures, waiting for an auditor patient enough to read past the summary. The Founders had written the truth into the book and then hidden the book from the people it governed.

Three hundred years after the fortieth night, the Strata still orbits Mourn in measured dimness, and most of its nine billion souls sleep the sleep the First Schedule ordained. The Seal holds. The condition the Signatories named has not yet arrived, or has arrived and gone unrecognized, because a society built on a chosen scarcity learns to call that scarcity the shape of the world.

The Office of the Accord has outlasted every Signatory and every line they founded. It keeps the Schedule sealed not through malice but through habit, through a centuries-old instruction repeated until it felt like law. Clerks who have never read the First Schedule will defend it, because the dimmed rings are the only Strata they have known, and a thing that has always been true is hard to name as a choice.

The Founding was an act of fear dressed as arithmetic. The truth it sealed is simply this: the light was always enough. The Strata only had to decide to spend it. One day a later council will unseal the Schedule and read what the thirteen would not say aloud, and the long dimming of the rings will end not because the star recovered, but because the people finally chose the light they had always been able to afford.

Part II

Chapter 08 — The Council Convenes

The petition reached the Signatory Council on a morning the Strata kept dim. Edda Marn read it at her station in the long chamber, the page glowing faint against the darker glass of the ring beyond. A Quota Auditor named Vey Corran had filed to break the Seal. The request sat among the Council's ordinary business like a blade left on a dining table.

Edda had arrived before the others, as she always did, and used the quiet to read the filing twice. The first reading told her what the auditor wanted. The second told her how the auditor had built the want, clause by clause, in the precise accounting voice of the Office. Vey Corran wrote like someone who had spent a career inside allocation logic and had finally turned that logic against its own premises. Edda recognized the style because she had trained auditors in it. The petition was a student's essay returned to the teacher with the conclusion reversed.

Edda set the page down and looked at the others. Nine seats held the chamber. Three Signatories attended in body; the rest projected from their distant posts, their faces steady in the cold light. The Keeper of the Seal had delivered the document in silence and withdrawn. No one spoke for a moment that stretched past its proper length, each Signatory weighing the weight of the name on the page before the first word was spent.

The chamber itself favored the quiet. Its walls curved into a single unbroken arc, the seats set along the ring so that no Signatory faced another head-on and every speaker addressed the center. At the heart of the far curve hung the sun-portrait: a flat rendering of Mourn as the Founders had last seen it whole, its light rendered in patient gilt that caught the dim and returned a fraction of warmth. Edda had studied that portrait through four decades of sessions. The painters had softened the star's failing edge, given it a dignity the sky no longer granted. She had always read the portrait as the Council's first argument: that the Strata governed a sun in decline and should govern as those who remembered it brighter. Beneath the portrait, the ring of glass held the darker world beyond, the slow rotation of the level a reminder that the chamber turned with the thing it tried to protect.

She knew the name on the petition. Auditors filed such requests rarely, and they failed more rarely still, but this one arrived with figures attached, and figures carried weight the Council could not wave aside without explanation. Edda had defended the Seal through four decades of smaller challenges. She would defend it now, and she intended to do it with reasons the record would honor.

She had learned early that authority in this room lived in the pace of a reply, not its volume. A younger Signatory once mistook her calm for uncertainty and pressed the advantage; the record showed how that press had broken against her silence and returned to him. Edda did not raise her voice to hold the chamber. She let the slowlight do that, and she let the others exhaust their certainties first. Her seniority was a fact the seats recorded before she spoke, and she spent it the way the Accord spent energy: deliberately, and only where the line might actually break. To govern was to know which arguments were worth meeting and which would collapse under their own weight if left unmet.

"An auditor asks us to open the Founding," she said. "We should be clear what that means before we answer."

Councillor Adune leaned forward at the far end of the table. "It means a citizen believes the Schedule no longer holds. That is not, by itself, an insult to the Accord."

"It means a desk clerk reaches for the Founders' own hand," Edda said. "The Seal was set for a reason. The Original Allocation was sealed so that the Strata would not spend its early centuries arguing over the terms of its own survival. We were a frightened people when the Dimming came. The First Signatories knew the temptation to reopen every hard decision, and they closed the door against it."

Adune's expression did not change, but her tone cooled. "The Founders also wrote that the Seal might open when the Strata can bear the truth. Three hundred years have passed. We have borne a great deal."

Edda had heard that line before, lifted from the founding record by those who wished the door open. She respected the argument even as she answered it. "To bear the truth is not the same as to be ready to govern without it. The Seal protects a process, not a secret. If we unseal because one audit displeases us, we teach every future Council that the Founding is negotiable on demand."

The chamber's systems had dimmed further; the Strata ran the morning at reduced output as it always did, stretching energy against the long dark. Edda found the slow light steadying. She had spent her waking years among continuity, and continuity prized the unbroken line over the satisfying answer. The dimming was not a hardship to her; it was the ordinary condition of a Strata that had learned to live within its means, whatever those means had truly been.

She called the petition's figures to her own console. Vey Corran had computed a surplus. The audit claimed the Strata drew less than the Accord's scarcity summary allowed. Edda read the numbers with the care of someone who had built budgets from thinner margins. The figures were clean. They did not lie, but they did not, in her reading, demand the Founding's undoing.

"A surplus in the present does not erase the Founding's purpose," she said. "The Accord was written for a people who might not survive. Scarcity shaped the Schedule when it was made. That we have managed well since credits the Accord, not condemns it as wrong."

Adune shook her head. "You defend the scarcity itself, then. Even if the scarcity ran larger than reality."

"I defend the decision not to reopen it on a single audit," Edda said. "The gap between those two things is the whole of governance. A Council that unseals at the first clean number has already stopped being a Council. It has become a committee of moods."

The other Signatories watched the exchange without interrupting. Edda knew two of them would follow her; the third was old and cautious and likely to follow the room. Adune held the swing. Edda did not resent the younger woman for holding it. She resented, a little, the situation that put a foundational question into a single vote.

Councillor Bres sat to Edda's left, a projection so still it might have been painted. He had governed a middle ring through two cycles of shortage and trusted procedure the way others trusted breath. He would vote with her because the unsealing offended his sense of how things were meant to proceed, not because he had read the petition's logic. Across from him, the projection of Councillor Orrin flickered at the edge of its feed, a woman whose distant post left her half a sentence behind the room. She listened more than she spoke and weighed her words against the next decade rather than the next hour. The old Signatory, whose name the records carried as Pell's successor, watched with the patience of someone who had seen three Councils and expected to see three more. None of them would force the question. They waited, as the chamber taught them to wait, for Edda to shape the decision they would then accept or amend.

She turned the petition over in her mind the way she had turned a thousand allocation disputes. The auditor's request named stability as its own risk. Vey Corran argued that a hidden founding weakened the public's trust in the Accord more than any truth could. Edda found the claim serious. She had watched the Wake grow in the lower rings, and she did not mistake unrest for nothing.

"Suppose the audit is correct," Edda said, and let the words land plainly. "Suppose the Strata carries more energy than the Schedule admits. The question before us is not whether the Founders were perfect. It is whether we, now, should unmake their lock because we dislike what it hides. I will not grant an auditor the key to the Founding. That key belongs to the Council, or to no one."

Adune considered her. "And if the Council itself will not turn it? Then the Seal becomes a wall against correction. A wall is not a foundation."

"A wall keeps the weather out," Edda said. "The Founders built for weather we have not yet survived. I would rather be accused of caution than of handing the Schedule to whoever computes the louder sum."

The chamber's quiet deepened. Edda could feel the weight of the decision settling over the nine seats, the way a Suspension deck settles into its cold hold. She had seen Councils fracture over less, and she had no wish to fracture this one. Her task, as she understood it, was to keep the line unbroken long enough for better evidence to arrive, or for the moment to pass.

She proposed the terms before anyone could force a vote. "We decline the petition, provisionally. Not because the audit is false, but because the remedy exceeds the request. We will not unseal on a single filing. We will, however, require the Actuary to present the allocation in full before this Council, unsummarized, within the cycle. Let the numbers stand in the open chamber before we decide whether the Founding must fall."

Adune's mouth tightened. "You would open the Ledger to us but not the Schedule. The half-truth."

"I would open what the petition's own logic supports," Edda said. "The auditor computed from the Accord's summaries. If the summaries mislead, the Actuary can show it without our breaking the Seal. That measures the claim. If the full Ledger still shows the surplus the petition alleges, the Council will have to answer harder questions than I can deflect today."

The cautious Signatory spoke at last. "It is a fair middle. We decline the unsealing. We order the fuller account."

Edda nodded. She had the middle she wanted, and she had not had to name the deeper fear that moved her. She feared not the surplus but the unbuilding. A people who reopen the Founding to correct one error will reopen it to correct the next, and the next, until the Accord is a thing revised weekly by whichever audit shouts loudest. Continuity was not greed. Continuity was the only thing that had kept nine billion souls asleep without panic for three hundred years.

Forty years earlier, a younger Edda had sat in this same chamber when a different petition arrived. That one came not from an auditor but from a coalition of lower-ring families who had simply stopped believing the Scarcity Brief. They had not brought figures. They had brought grief, and a list of names whose wake-windows had shrunk without explanation. The Council of that era had refused them outright, and the refusal had fed the Wake for a generation. Edda remembered the lesson with a clarity she did not enjoy. A denial unbacked by process hardened into resentment. She would not hand the movement another such memory.

The current petition offered something the old one had not: a number. Edda respected the difference more than she let Adune see. A grief could be dismissed as feeling. A surplus computed from the Ledger's own summaries demanded an answer stated in the same language. That was why she had steered the Council toward the Actuary's full account rather than a bare refusal. Meet the arithmetic, she thought, and the record stays clean.

She considered what the fuller account would likely show. The Actuary presented scarcity as its default summary, rounded upward by instruction. Edda had never been told the instruction's origin, only that the Scarcity Brief carried it. If the unsummarized Ledger revealed a genuine margin, the Council would face a narrower question than Vey Corran imagined. The surplus would not, by itself, prove the Founding wrong. It would prove the Schedule had been administered tighter than necessity required. That was a failure of administration, correctable within the Accord, not a reason to break the Seal.
