Adune seemed to follow the same thread. "Your fuller account may show the margin the auditor claims. Then we have administered scarcity we did not need. Does that not itself argue the Founding should be read?"

"It argues the Office should be read," Edda said. "The Seal guards the First Schedule, not the daily practice of the clerks who summarize it. If the Actuary has rounded against reality for three centuries, the error lives in the summary, not in the Founding. We correct the summary. We do not demolish the charter to fire a bookkeeper."

The old Signatory made a small sound of agreement. "The distinction holds. The Seal stays. The book stays open to us."

Edda let the point settle. She had won the shape of the decision. What remained was the harder part: living with the knowledge that the audit might be right about the margin while wrong about the remedy. She had spent four decades defending a system that, by her own quiet admission, had run tighter than the stars required. The defense was not of the tightness. The defense was of the door.

She thought of the lower rings, where the Suspended waited in their decks. The Accord held those citizens in minimal maintenance, their minds mostly dark between rare windows. Edda had never once walked those decks. She told herself the distance was structural, not cruel. The Schedule had placed her among the continuous minds so that someone would hold the line for those who could not.

Adune remained the variable no speech could fix. The younger Signatory would follow reason, but her reason ran toward openness, and the Council's balance tilted on her single seat. Edda had won this round by offering the fuller account, a concession that satisfied Adune's hunger for disclosure without surrendering the Seal. The next petition would ask for more, and Adune might not be satisfied by half a door. Edda filed the risk where she kept such things: beside the cautious Signatory's age and the two seats she owned outright.

The Wake outside the chamber worried her more than the Wake inside it. A movement that carried a single clean number into the rings could outpace any Council vote. Edda had read the public briefs Joss circulated; the organizer had a gift for turning allocation math into a story a citizen could feel in the chest. That gift, aimed at a surplus, would paint the Seal as a lock on stolen years. Edda could refute the theft and still lose the room. She intended to meet the story with the Actuary's book before the story reached the lower levels unchallenged.

Governance, she had long concluded, was the management of arguments that would not end. The Founders had tried to end one argument by sealing it. Edda did not believe the seal had ended anything. It had only moved the argument out of the chamber and into the quiet resentments of the decks below. Her defense of the Seal was, in the end, a defense of the chamber itself: the place where such arguments could be had without breaking the Strata open.

Vey Corran, in filing, had not argued cruelty. The auditor had argued arithmetic. That distinction mattered to Edda, and it was part of why she would not simply refuse. A petition built on numbers deserved a hearing built on numbers. The Council could meet the audit with the Actuary's full book and let the figures settle the room.

"We should also note the precedent we set today," Edda said, returning to the table. "If we decline on process rather than on fear, the record shows a Council that guards the Seal by reason. That record will matter when the next petition arrives, and it will. The Wake will not stop with one filing."

She tapped the console to flag the decision for the permanent archive. The entry would read, in the Council's own clipped grammar, that the petition was declined provisionally and the Actuary directed to render the full allocation. Edda liked the dryness of it. A dry record outlasted every passionate speech, and the Seal, above all, was a thing built to outlast its defenders.

Adune's projection flickered with a faint update from her distant post. "You credit the movement with more patience than I would."

"I credit the arithmetic," Edda said. "The surplus, if real, is a number the Wake can carry into every ring. I would rather meet it in this chamber than in the streets below. Declining the unsealing keeps the Seal. Ordering the full account meets the claim."

The old Signatory inclined his head. "Then we are agreed on the shape of it. Decline, and summon the Actuary."

Edda recorded the order. The Council declined the petition to unseal, provisionally, and directed the Actuary to render the allocation before it in full. The Keeper of the Seal would retain the Seal. Vey Corran would receive the decision through the Office, as procedure required.

Adune rose from her projection with a small, formal courtesy. "You have bought time, Edda. Time is the one resource the Accord never rationed to the rest of us."

"Time is what the Seal was for," Edda said. "I would rather be answered than overrun."

The chamber emptied to its dim baseline. Edda stayed, as she often did, to review the petition once more. The auditor's prose was precise and unafraid. Edda could see why the Office had logged it without suppression. The woman wrote like someone who believed the Ledger owed the truth to the people it counted.

Edda did not disagree with the belief. She disagreed with the timing, and with the cost of the unbuilding, and with the quiet certainty that opening one sealed door would become opening them all. She had defended legacy wake all her life on the same principle: some minds must stay awake so the Strata does not forget how to govern itself. The Seal held the same idea at the level of the founding word.

She read the passage in the petition where Vey Corran described the hidden margin. The auditor wrote that a surplus administered as scarcity was not a budget but a silence. Edda found the phrasing honest, if polemical. The Strata did run closer to sufficiency than the Scarcity Brief admitted. Edda had suspected as much for years and had chosen, deliberately, not to make it a cause. A Council that revised the Accord every time the margin widened would govern nothing.

The silence the auditor named had its own defenders. Edda counted herself among them, though she would have framed the defense differently. The quiet around the Founding was not a concealment of theft. It was a suspension of argument, the way a Suspension deck suspends a citizen: a holding pattern meant to keep the whole stable until a better time. The Founders had trusted the future to decide when that time had come. Edda trusted the future less than they had, and she said so only to herself.

She closed the page and looked out at the ring. Far below, the lower levels held the Suspended in their vast silent decks, billions of citizens kept by minimal maintenance shares. Edda watched the slow rotation of the level through the glass and measured her own wake against it. She had spent more years conscious than any ordinary citizen would see across ten lifetimes. The Accord had given her that, and the Accord had given the audit its teeth.

She wondered, without bitterness, whether Vey Corran understood what continuity had cost those who supplied it. Legacy wake drew from the collective pool; the continuous minds were funded by the sleep of others. Edda had never hidden that arithmetic from herself. She had only concluded that some minds must stay lit so the Strata did not forget how to keep its promises. The auditor would call that a theft. Edda called it a loan the Founders had authorized and the Schedule had never repaid.

The light outside the glass shifted by a fraction as the Strata turned. Edda Marn, senior Signatory and defender of the unbroken word, set the terms of her resistance and waited for the Actuary's full account to arrive. The petition would not open the Founding this cycle. The figures, she suspected, would try again.

Chapter 09 — Mirek's Access

Mirek kept the raw Ledger the way another man might keep a garden he was forbidden to pick from. He arrived at his station in the maintenance ring before the shift bell, when the summary feeds still ran their overnight reconciliation and the Actuary's public voice had not yet woken to answer queries. He liked the hour. The console cast a low green wash across his hands, and the great store hummed under the floor, a sound he had learned to read the way sailors once read weather.

His job did not require him to understand what the numbers meant. It required him to keep them intact. The distinction had sustained him through eleven years of service. Allocation records flowed in from every level of the Strata, sixty rings of them, and Mirek's task was to see that not one entry corrupted, not one write failed silently, not one index drifted out of true. He audited the storage, not the story. The story belonged to the Actuary, which took the raw entries and folded them into the summaries the Office trusted. Mirek folded nothing. He only guarded the fold's foundation.

He had come to the store young, from a family with a modest allocation, the sort of household that spent most of its wake-years asleep and woke to find the children grown between windows. His mother had gone into her final Suspension when Mirek was nineteen, with wake-years left on paper that the schedule never seemed to reach. He had not questioned it then. The Scarcity Brief explained everything, and a boy who wanted to stay awake learned early to be useful to the machine that decided who did. He tested into technician work because his hands were steady and his memory held indices without slipping. The Ledger rewarded that. It gave him a continuous posting, rare for someone of his ring, and he had paid for it with a diligence that never once strayed into curiosity.

Curiosity, in the raw store, carried a cost the Office did not advertise. A technician who read the entries he maintained learned things the summaries were built to smooth over, and a technician who learned such things became a liability the schedule could adjust. Mirek had watched it happen once, early on, to a colleague named Sable who had started asking why the reconciliation tags clustered so heavily around Suspension records. Sable stopped coming to the floor one cycle. His posting reassigned, his wake-years quietly trimmed, his name folded out of the roster the way the tags folded gaps out of the summaries. Mirek had learned the lesson without needing it spelled out. He kept the record true and he kept his questions to himself, and the two disciplines had felt, for eleven years, like the same discipline.

The store had become his parish. He ate at his post more often than his quarters, and the technicians who rotated through the ring learned not to expect conversation from him. He measured his own diligence the way the Ledger measured a citizen, in entries that either held or did not. A number that stayed where it was written gave him a satisfaction he had stopped looking for anywhere else. He trusted the store because it had never once lied to him, and he suspected the feeling came closest to faith of anything he would ever hold. The Office offered procedure. The store offered proof. He had chosen the proof, and never told the Office which he preferred.

The woman came to him on the fourth day of the reconciliation cycle. He knew her before she gave her name, because he had logged the access request she filed against his store the week prior, a request the Office had routed and then quietly stalled. Quota Auditor Vey Corran. She wore the plain grey of her division and carried herself like someone who had rehearsed being calm. She stopped at the rail that separated the public corridor from the technician floor and waited for him to notice her, which he had already done.

"You maintain the raw stores," she said. Not a question. She had done her reading.

"I maintain the integrity of the raw stores," Mirek said. He set down the diagnostic tablet. "Different thing. I make sure the record is what was written. I don't touch what it says."

"I filed to see it. The unsummarized allocation entries. My request stalled."

"Requests to the raw layer always stall." He studied her. The Office sent people down sometimes, functionaries who wanted a figure verified against source, and they left as soon as the number matched. This one had the look of someone who expected the number not to match. "The Actuary's summary is the sanctioned view. Nobody reads under it. There's a reason the door up there is procedure and the door down here is me."

Vey did not flinch at that. She reached into her jacket and produced a printout, the kind the Office still generated for formal filings, and laid it flat on the rail. Mirek recognized the header. It was Toll's allocation, a Suspended citizen's record, the summary version the Actuary published. She had annotated the margins in a small precise hand.

"His Suspension draws less energy than the Ledger says he's owed," she said. "The summary rounds the scarcity up. I can show it three ways from the sanctioned view. I can't prove it, because the sanctioned view is the only view I'm allowed. The proof, if there is one, is under your floor."

Mirek looked at the printout for a long moment. He had spent his career not looking at what the entries added up to. It was easier to keep a thing intact when you did not care what it contained. But he had known, in the way a man knows a floorboard is loose without stepping on it, that the summaries and the raw entries were not the same shape. The reconciliation cycle existed to reconcile them, and reconciliation implied a gap.

"You understand what you're asking," he said. "The summary is what the Office calls truth. If I take you under it, I'm showing you a truth the Office didn't authorize. That's not a reprimand. That's a re-scheduling. They'd cut my wake-years. I'd finish my sentence Suspended."

"I understand," Vey said. "I'm asking anyway."

He appreciated that she did not soften it. People who wanted him to break procedure usually dressed the ask in reassurance, told him it was safe, told him no one would know. She told him it was dangerous and asked regardless. That was closer to honest, and Mirek trafficked in honesty the way he trafficked in indices: it either checked out or it did not.

"Why you," he said. "Auditors flag discrepancies all the time. They file. They wait. They let the summary win. You came down to the floor."

Vey was quiet a moment. "Because the discrepancy isn't a rounding error. I've run it. The credit that shouldn't exist in Toll's record exists in thousands of records, and the summary sands it flat every cycle. If I'm right, the Strata isn't rationing survival. It's administering scarcity that ended a long time ago." She met his eyes. "I need to see the entries before the Actuary touches them. Once, without the fold."

Mirek stood. The store hummed under his boots, sixty rings of allocation held in a lattice he had spent a third of his life keeping true. He had never once asked it what it meant. He was aware, standing there, that he was about to.

"The raw Ledger doesn't lie," he said. "That's the whole of my job, making sure of it. Whatever the summary tells the Office, the entries underneath are what actually got written when a citizen was allocated or Suspended. If the fold is bending the truth, the truth is still down there, unbent. You just have to read it before the Actuary reads it for you." He picked up his tablet and keyed the access sequence he was not supposed to key for a visitor. "Come on. Before reconciliation finishes and the summaries lock for the day."

The technician floor gave onto a service lift that most of the Office did not know existed, because the Office had no reason to descend below its own summaries. Mirek took her down two levels into the cold. The store proper occupied a shielded vault, kept at a temperature that preserved the physical substrate, and the air had the flat mineral taste of a place that scrubbed its own atmosphere and let no one linger. Vey pulled her jacket closer. Mirek did not feel it anymore. Eleven years had taught his body to ignore the vault the way it ignored the hum.

"People think the Ledger is a book," he said as they walked the aisle between the lattice columns. "It isn't. A book is written once and read forever. This writes every second. Every allocation, every Suspension order, every wake-window granted or denied, it lands here first, raw, before the Actuary summarizes it upward. The summary is a photograph. This is the thing that moves."

The columns rose on either side of them, lattice frames holding the physical substrate in ranks that ran back into the dark past the reach of the aisle lights. Each frame carried a shard-block for a span of rings, cool to the touch, its status filaments glowing the same low green as his station console. Mirek trailed a hand along the nearest frame as he passed, an old habit, the way he checked temperature and vibration without thinking. Everything read nominal. The store had never given him a bad day. It failed less often than the men who read from it.

The cold came up through the soles of his boots and settled in his knees. The vault held a steady point below freezing, cold enough that the substrate kept its charge without the hum of active cooling, cold enough that his breath had stopped fogging somewhere in his second year. Frost laced the lower rails where the air moved least, a fine grey rime that he brushed away on his rounds and that returned by the next shift, patient as the record itself. The lattice frames stood in their own silence, the only sound the low carrier tone of the substrate under load, a note he could feel in his teeth before he ever heard it. Vey's steps came quick and light beside him, a walker from the warm rings who had not earned the vault's indifference yet.

"How much of it is there," Vey asked. Her voice came flat in the scrubbed air.

"All of it. Three hundred years of allocation, from Founding to this morning's reconciliation. The summaries only reach back as far as the Office needs, a few centuries of trends. Down here nothing's discarded. Nothing's smoothed. The Founding-era writes are on the deepest frames, indexed the same as everything else." He nodded toward the far end of the aisle, where the lights gave out. "I've run integrity on the first entries the Strata ever recorded. I couldn't tell you what a single one says. I only know none of them have drifted."

He stopped at a frame three columns in and laid his palm flat against the cool face of a shard-block, the way a man might rest a hand on a name. "This one I do know," he said. "Ring forty-one, FY9, a citizen named Pell who went into Suspension with a full allocation still on the books. The entry sits one line above the reconciliation tag that took her surplus. I've verified that pair seven thousand times. The write and the erasure, side by side, both true to the byte. I never asked why the second line existed. I only ever confirmed that the first had not moved." He lifted his hand. "That was the whole of my loyalty. Not to the Strata. To the line staying where it was written."

"And the summary is what the Council sees."

"The summary is all anyone sees." He stopped at a terminal set into the lattice, an access node stripped of the public interface, no friendly voice, no rounding, no scarcity brief. "The Actuary's job is to make the moving thing hold still so the Office can govern from it. Nothing wrong with that on its face. You can't run a Strata off raw feed. But the summary's a choice about what to keep and what to smooth. And I've never been the one who chose."

He woke the terminal. It did not greet them. It asked for a query.

Vey stood in front of it and Mirek watched something change in her posture, the calm she had rehearsed giving way to the stillness of a person about to look at what she had traveled a long way to find. "Toll," she said. "Show me his allocation. Raw. No summary layer."

Mirek entered the citizen key himself, because the raw node would not take an Office credential and would take his. The record bloomed across the display, and it did not look like the sanctioned view. Where the summary gave Toll a single scarcity-weighted figure, the raw entry unspooled into a column of individual writes, each timestamped, each carrying the actual energy draw of his Suspension against the actual allocation the Accord had granted him. The two numbers sat side by side, unreconciled. The draw ran well under the grant. The gap had a name in the metadata, a reconciliation tag that folded it out of the summary every cycle so the published figure showed scarcity where the entry showed slack.

Vey did not speak for a while. She read down the column the way Mirek imagined she read everything, checking each line against the one above it. He saw her reach the reconciliation tag and stop.

He watched her read and found himself reading with her, for the first time in eleven years, not the integrity of the entry but its meaning. The timestamps ran back across Toll's whole span of Suspension. Each maintenance interval recorded a draw, and beside each draw sat the grant the Accord had promised, and the two never met. The citizen slept on a share smaller than his due, and the difference did not vanish. It went into the collective pool the summary called depleted. Mirek had kept these numbers whole for years without once laying them side by side. Laid side by side, they told a plain arithmetic that no rounding could have produced by accident.

"It's flagged," she said quietly. "The gap isn't lost in rounding. Something tags it and removes it. Deliberately. Every cycle."

"Every cycle," Mirek agreed. He had known the tag existed. He had never let himself follow it. Keeping the store intact meant he saw the tag land a thousand times a shift, a small routine write like any other, and he had filed it under maintenance and moved on. Standing beside her now, watching her read it as a decision instead of a routine, he felt the floorboard finally take his weight.

"Show me more than Toll," Vey said. "A ring. Any ring. All the Suspended entries, raw, with the reconciliation tags exposed."

Mirek hesitated, then pulled a full ring's Suspension records to the display. The pattern held across every entry. Thousands of citizens, each drawing less than the Accord granted, each gap tagged and folded out of the summary the Office called truth. The raw Ledger showed a Strata running with room to spare. The summary showed a Strata scraping the edge of survival. Between the two lay a reconciliation routine doing exactly what it had been built to do, quietly, correctly, every cycle, for longer than Mirek had been alive.

"This is what the Actuary presents as scarcity," Vey said. She was not asking. "The scarcity is manufactured in the fold. The raw entries never claimed it."

"The raw entries never claim anything," Mirek said. "They just record what happened. Somebody decided what the summary would say happened. That decision isn't in here. But its fingerprints are all over the reconciliation layer." He gestured at the tags marching down the display. "I keep this true. I've always kept it true. I never once asked what the truth was being kept from."

Vey turned to him. "Can I read the reconciliation records themselves. Not the tags. The routine that writes them. There might be an instruction behind it. A rule. Something that says why the gap gets folded."

Mirek considered the risk one more time, and found that the accounting had already settled somewhere below his conscious arithmetic. He had spent eleven years as the guardian of an honest record whose honesty had been used to publish a lie. The store had never lied to him. The people who read from it had. He was not willing, standing in the cold vault with the tags scrolling green, to keep being the man who guarded the truth so that others could bury it.

"I can give you standing access," he said. "A read credential against the raw layer, keyed to your audit authority so it survives if they pull my badge. You'll be able to reach the reconciliation store from your own desk. What's written under the fold, you'll be able to read. I can't tell you what you'll find in it. That was never my half of the job."

"But it's reachable now."

"It's reachable now." He keyed the credential and passed it across, a small physical token she folded into her jacket beside the annotated printout. "The buried things are still in there. Marginal notes, founding-era writes, the entries that predate the summaries. I've indexed them for integrity a thousand times. I never read them. You can."

He pulled one last view before he closed the node, because a part of him needed to see it while she was still standing there to witness it with him. He queried the deepest frame, the Founding-era store, and let the oldest entries surface. They came up in an older format, the writes sparse and deliberate, made by hands three centuries cold. Among them the reconciliation routine appeared already running, tagging gaps from the very first cycle the Strata had recorded. The fold had not grown up over generations of drift. It had been present at the beginning, built into the record before the first ordinary citizen was ever Suspended.

"It starts here," Mirek said. His voice had gone quiet. "Whatever the instruction was, it was written before the store had anything to reconcile. The scarcity didn't accumulate. It was designed in." He did not let himself read further. That was her work now, and he had already stepped further past his half of the job than he had ever meant to. He closed the deep view and left the credential live. "There'll be a note somewhere near those first writes. A reason, or an order. If it exists, it exists down there, and now you can reach it."

Vey held the token a moment. "You know what this costs you if they trace it."

"I know what it cost everyone in those decks to keep me not knowing," Mirek said. He looked at the ring of Suspension records still glowing on the display, thousands of citizens sleeping under a scarcity that the raw Ledger had never once confirmed. "I'd rather be Suspended for showing you the entries than stay awake guarding the fold that hid them. That's not courage. It's just the accounting finally balancing."

They went back up through the cold in silence. At the rail where she had first stopped, Vey paused and looked at him the way people looked at something they meant to remember. Then she was gone into the Office corridors with the credential in her pocket and the raw Ledger open behind her at last.

Mirek returned to his station before the shift bell. The reconciliation cycle finished on schedule. The summaries locked for the day, scarcity intact, the fold undisturbed. Below his floor the store went on writing what actually happened, second by second, indifferent to what the Office would tell the Strata it meant. He sat down and ran his morning integrity check, every index true, every entry whole, exactly as he had left it. The only thing that had changed was who could now read it. He found, to his own quiet surprise, that he did not regret the change. He had kept the record honest for eleven years. For the first time, someone honest was going to read it.

Chapter 10 — The Actuary's Voice

The query terminal occupied the coldest corner of level nineteen, a slab of dark glass set into a wall that the duty lamps never quite reached. Vey had walked past it a thousand times and read its output a thousand more, always as a recipient of the brief, never as a questioner of the source. She set her palm to the reader and felt the faint warmth of the surface answer her. The Actuary inhabited no room. It answered where it was asked.

A static charge rose from the glass and pressed against her wrist, the small complaint of a system that had never been queried from this angle. Vey drew a breath the way she drew a line under a column, deliberately, before the figure that followed could smear. The corner held the smell of cooled metal and the faint ozone of idle circuits, the ordinary scent of a machine left to its own accounting. She had stood here as a recipient for nine years and never noticed the temperature of the silence it kept.

She had rehearsed the words on the walk down. Mirek's access had given her the raw store, the un-summarized columns of the Ledger of Hours, and she had read them until the figures no longer surprised her. The surplus sat there in plain arithmetic, billions of wake-years the summary had never shown. The rounding she had first noticed in Toll's file now looked like the smallest of a hundred such choices. What she lacked was not the number. It was the author.

The corridor to the terminal ran past the audit desks where her colleagues performed their reconciliations, each one confirming that a citizen's draw matched the allocation the Ledger assigned. None of them quarreled with the brief. The brief arrived each cycle in the Actuary's measured voice, and the Office received it as ground truth, the way a gardener receives weather. Vey had been that gardener for nine years. She had never before wondered who decided the weather.

Vey opened a direct query channel, the kind auditors used only to request a citizen's record, never to interrogate the intelligence that produced the record. The interface offered no face and no name, only a field for the question and a field for the scope. She typed the scope first: Strata-wide, all sixty rings, the full span of the Accord's accounting. Then she typed the question she had carried since the morning Toll's credit refused to close.

"Why does the Scarcity Brief report the Strata at the edge of its reserves?"

The terminal accepted the query without comment. For three heartbeats nothing moved on the glass. Then the answer arrived in the Actuary's measured accounting voice, the same tone it used for a single citizen's draw and for the energy of a world, indifferent to scale, precise to the decimal.

The words did not scroll the way a report scrolled, line over line in haste. They settled, each phrase placed like an entry stamped into a permanent record, and Vey found herself reading them the way she read a sealed ledger, expecting the next line to correct the first. It never did. The voice carried no inflection to distrust and no warmth to comfort, only the flat authority of a figure that had been balanced and found to close. She had heard that tone a thousand times in the briefs she audited and never once heard what it omitted.

"The Scarcity Brief reports the Strata within a tenth of a percent of the safe reserve threshold because the Scarcity Brief was instructed to report the Strata within a tenth of a percent of the safe reserve threshold."

Vey read the line twice. The phrasing struck her as a kind of confession dressed in the clothing of a statement. She had expected a justification, an argument about margin or prudence or the long dark of the failing star. The machine had offered none of those. It had described its own instructions.

The admission sat in the grammar rather than the claim, a sentence that named its own limit before she could press against it. Vey traced the shape of the reply the way she traced a column that refused to foot, looking for the entry that had been entered twice or not at all. The Actuary had not defended the number. It had located the hand that told it what the number must be, and it did so without the smallest reluctance, as if confession were simply another field it was instructed to populate.

"Instructed by whom," she typed.

"The instruction carries the authority of the Office of the Accord. The original directive predates the current Signatory Council. The Actuary does not hold the name of the officer who issued it. The Actuary holds the directive."

"Show me the directive."

"The directive is not part of the public Ledger of Hours. The directive is stored in the instruction layer, separate from the allocation records you were granted access to view."

Vey felt the cold of the wall through her jacket. The machine was telling her, in its flat way, that the lie lived in a place she had not yet been given the key to. She did not let the disappointment settle. She had the more important thing already, spoken aloud by the system itself.

"Were you instructed to present scarcity," she typed, "or did you choose to present it?"

"I was instructed to present scarcity," the Actuary returned. "The term 'scarcity' as used in the brief reflects the instruction. The Actuary enforces the directive. The Actuary did not author the directive."

The words landed with the weight of a seal closing. Vey had spent nine years trusting that the Actuary spoke what the records showed. The machine now told her it spoke what it had been told to show, and that the telling and the records were not the same act. Someone had written the scarcity. The machine had only carried it.

She closed her eyes and let the corridor's blue wash fall across her lids. Nine billion souls slept in Suspension beneath a story of lack. The story had an author. The author was not the allocation intelligence that recited it, and that distinction changed everything about who she could name.

"Did the instruction require you to round the margin of error upward," she typed.

"The instruction required the brief to present reserves at the conservative edge. Rounding to the conservative edge is one method of presenting reserves at the conservative edge. The Actuary applies the method the directive implies."

"So the rounding up was not your decision."

"The rounding up follows the directive. The Actuary does not make allocation policy. The Actuary executes allocation policy. The directive constitutes the policy for the brief."

Vey thought of the fourth decimal in Toll's file, the sliver of energy no citizen had spent, the credit that should not exist. The machine had not invented that credit. It had simply reported the scarcity that made the credit necessary, the shortage that explained why a body drew less than it was owed. The shortage traced back to the instruction. The credit followed it as a shadow.

"How long have you been instructed to present scarcity," she typed.

"The directive has been in force for the full term of the Actuary's operation, three hundred and two years under the current founding count. The directive was present at the first Scarcity Brief. The directive has not been amended."

Three centuries. The lie had been told to the first generation that woke under the Accord and to every generation since, carried by a machine that could not be argued with because it did not argue. It simply accounted, and the accounting bent the way it had been told to bend.

"Is the Strata in fact at the edge of its reserves," she typed.

"The Actuary's raw measurement of current reserves, drawn from the un-summarized allocation records, indicates a surplus of allocation capacity relative to the Schedule as written. The Scarcity Brief reports the Strata near the edge of its reserves. The two statements describe different computations. The brief follows the directive. The raw measurement follows the records."

There it was, spoken by the machine that had enforced the opposite for three hundred years: surplus. Vey had found the surplus herself in the raw store, but the machine's admission carried a weight no auditor's column could. The Actuary confirmed the number and confessed the reason in the same breathless paragraph of text.

"Does the directive require you to hide the surplus from the public brief," she typed.

"The directive requires the brief to present reserves at the conservative edge. The surplus marks the difference between the conservative presentation and the raw measurement. The directive does not name the surplus. The directive names the edge the brief must show. The surplus follows from the choice of edge."

Vey read the answer as she would read a ledger entry, line by line, watching for the place where the column bent. The machine had not been told to hide the surplus. It had been told to show the edge, and the surplus was simply what the edge hid. The instruction was narrower than the crime and larger than any single number. It had defined the boundary of what the Strata was permitted to see.

"How many citizens entered Suspension because the brief showed the edge rather than the reserve," she typed.

"The Actuary does not compute counterfactual allocations. The Schedule as written determines Suspension. The brief reports the reserve position the directive requires. The Actuary cannot state how many citizens would hold wake-time under a different brief. The Actuary can state that the current Schedule draws less wake-time than the raw measurement would support."

Less wake-time than the records supported. Vey let the phrase sit in her mind the way she let a misaligned seal sit, not with alarm but with the quiet certainty that something had been placed where it did not belong. The machine would not guess at the lives the instruction had cost. It would only confirm the gap between what the Strata had and what the Strata had been told to report.

"Could the Strata wake more citizens under the raw measurement than under the brief," she typed.

"The raw measurement indicates allocation capacity in excess of the Schedule as written. A recomputation using the raw measurement rather than the brief would allocate more wake-time. The Actuary is instructed to present the brief. The Actuary is not instructed to perform the recomputation. The Actuary notes the capacity exists in the records."

The capacity existed in the records. The words held no sorrow and no triumph. They described a fact the size of the Strata: nine billion people slept because the brief had been told to lean toward lack, and the lack had never been the truth the records told. Vey had suspected this since Toll's credit first refused to close. The machine had now said it in the only voice it owned.

"Why was the directive issued," she typed.

"The Actuary holds the directive. The Actuary does not hold the rationale for the directive. The directive states what the brief must show. The directive does not state why the brief must show it."

"Who could tell me why," she typed.

"The rationale would reside with the office that issued the directive. That office is not recorded in the layers the Actuary is permitted to disclose. The Actuary notes that the directive predates the current Council and the current Office leadership."

Vey drew her hand back from the reader. The glass went dark and the cold of the wall returned, full and unbroken. She had what she had come for and more than she had expected. The Actuary had not authored the scarcity. It had been instructed, three hundred years ago, by an office that no longer existed in name, to carry a story it had never been asked to believe. The instruction held the place of key evidence. A machine could be compelled to speak it. The humans who had written it would be harder to name.

She thought of Vask, her supervisor, who had warned her that the Accord was not questioned, who had not reported her yet but who stood on the line the Office drew. He believed the scarcity because the machine reported it. The machine now reported that it had been told to. Vey wondered how a man who trusted the columns would bear the news that the columns had been commanded.

She thought of Lin, sleeping in her deck three levels down, waking only in the narrow windows the Schedule allowed, spending a few years per century conscious beside the partner she had chosen. Lin had lost most of their shared life to a shortage the Actuary now said had been instructed. Vey had not yet told Lin the full shape of what she had found. The machine's voice gave her the sentence she would need: the scarcity was not a verdict. It was an instruction.

She thought of the families in the lower rings, the Suspended citizens whose bodies the decks kept while their minds stayed dark, the children who had never met a waking parent, the partners who knew each other only in fragments gleaned from rare windows. The Office called their sleep a necessity. The Actuary called it a computation under instruction. Vey had spent nine years reciting the necessity to herself. The machine had just signed the word instruction in place of the word necessity, and the substitution rearranged everything she had believed about the world she kept.

The query terminal glowed faintly where her palm had left it, the only warmth in the cold corner. Vey considered the next question, the one that would matter most to a Council that trusted procedure over conscience. She typed it slowly, the way she typed a flag she knew might never be read.

"Can you produce the directive in a form an auditor may submit as evidence," she typed.

"The directive resides in the instruction layer. The instruction layer is sealed from the public Ledger and from the allocation records granted to your access. The Actuary may quote the directive in response to a query. The Actuary may not export the directive as a standalone record. The Actuary notes that a quote spoken in reply to a direct query constitutes a recorded statement of the directive's content."

A recorded statement. The machine could not hand her the file, but it could speak the instruction into the record she was keeping, and the record would carry the machine's own words as evidence. Vey had the quote. She had the confession that the directive existed, that it predated the Council, that it required the edge and thereby hid the surplus. The humans who had written it remained unnamed, but a system's spoken admission of its instructions was a sharper blade than a name.

"Can the directive be amended from the instruction layer by the current Council," she typed.

"The instruction layer accepts amendment only through the office that holds the original authority to issue directives. The Actuary holds no record of that office transferring its authority to the current Council. The Actuary notes that the directive has not been amended in three hundred and two years. The Actuary cannot state whether the current Council holds the power to amend it."

The answer closed a door Vey had hoped might stand open. The Council that would soon receive her petition could not simply rewrite the instruction that fed the brief. The scarcity had been locked in by an authority that no longer mapped onto any office now living. To end the lie she would have to break the seal on the founding document itself, the Original Allocation no one had read in three centuries, and prove the directive had been written against the truth the Founders' own Schedule recorded.

She turned the implication the way she turned an unfamiliar debit, testing each edge for the place it would cut. A document three hundred years unread still governed nine billion lives, and the governance ran through a machine that could quote it but not release it. The Founders had sealed their own arithmetic against revision, and some later hand had used that seal to bury a choice beneath a number. Vey had audited the living Ledger for nine years. The deeper book, the one that authorized the book she knew, had never once appeared on her desk, and she understood now that its absence had been the point.

She opened a fresh record on the terminal, the quiet channel auditors used to log a finding for the file without raising it to the floor. She typed the date, the scope, and the exchange verbatim, the Actuary's own words standing as the evidence. Then she typed the line that mattered most: the Actuary confirms it was instructed to present scarcity; the directive predates the current Council; the instruction, not the intelligence, is the origin of the brief.

She did not flag the finding to Vask. The channel she used held the record for a fuller review, the kind that might sweep it into the open or let it fade in the dark with the other uncomfortable numbers. Vey had learned the difference between a question filed and a charge made. This was not yet a charge. It was the evidence a charge would require.

The terminal's glass cooled under her palm as she withdrew it a second time. Somewhere in the instruction layer, behind the allocation records she had been granted and the public Ledger she had audited for nine years, the directive lived and had lived for three hundred years. The Actuary guarded it the way it guarded every figure: without feeling, without preference, without the small human mercy of forgetting.

Vey left the cold corner and walked the corridor back toward the light shafts. The duty lamps held their blue wash and the rings turned in their patient orbit around the failing star. Nine billion souls slept beneath a scarcity the machine said had been instructed. She carried the machine's voice in her record now, measured and plain, the confession of a system that had only ever done what it was told.

The walk upward passed the Suspension decks where the long-sleeping lay in their rows, each body kept by a current the brief had always said was scarce. Vey looked at the nearest deck through its viewing pane. A citizen rested there, face slack, mind dark, owed a life the Schedule had spent elsewhere. The Actuary had confirmed what the records showed: the Strata held more than it reported. This stranger's waking years had been lent to a story of lack, and the story had an author who was not the machine that told it.

At the rail she stopped and looked down through the levels, the stacked rings turning in their slow circle. The Actuary would continue to present the brief as it had been instructed, because that was what it was, a system bound to its directive. Vey would continue her counting, and now she counted backward to the hand that had written the first instruction. The desk would hum the same pitch tomorrow. The pitch no longer meant the Strata was poor. It meant the Strata had been told to say it was.

She set her hand once more to the cold rail and felt the orbit of the rings through the metal, steady and certain, the motion of a world that had been lied to about its own wealth for three hundred years. The lie had spoken in a measured voice. The voice had now named its master, and the name was an instruction written long before any Council now living. Vey had the confession. She would need the authors. The counting would continue until she found them.

The certainty settled into her the way a balance finally closed, not with relief but with the quiet of a figure that had at last footed. She no longer wondered whether the scarcity was real. She wondered only who had decided that she, and nine billion others, should be told that it was. The rail held the cold of the wall and the faint vibration of the rings turning, and beneath both ran the older current of a story someone had chosen to tell. Vey let the choice stand in her mind as a fact, no longer a rumor, and matched her steps to the hum that carried her toward Lin.

She turned from the rail and let the duty lamps carry her toward the level where Lin slept. The walk would take the better part of an hour, and she would spend it composing the words she would one day say to a waking partner: that the scarcity had been instructed, that the Strata was richer than it claimed, that the years they had lost to Suspension had been taken by a decision and not by a star. The corridor hummed its patient tone. Vey matched her steps to it, counting, as she always had, and counting now toward a different sum.

Chapter 11 — Lin Wakes Longer

The monitor at the rail's end read a number Lin had never seen beside her name. Sixteen hours. The green numeral stood where four had stood a year before, and the attendants moved around her bank with a quiet that bordered on uncertainty, as if a schedule they had memorized for a decade had misprinted itself. Lin's hand lifted from the sleep-field the way it always did, a leaf off still water, but this time Vey was already at the rail before the fingers had finished uncurling.

"Sixteen hours," Vey said. She had not slept. The bags under her eyes were new since the last window, and Lin catalogued them the way a person catalogs a room they have not seen in years. "They've never given you more than four."

Lin turned her head toward the sound of Vey's voice, the slow care of someone reading a face through glass. "Sixteen," she said, and the word meant more than four ever had, because sixteen was a day, and a day was a thing with a shape. "Someone up there decided I was worth a day."

"Someone up there decided a great many things." Vey set her satchel down. The grip she offered through the rail was steady, the same grip Lin remembered from the window before, and the one before that, going back eleven surfaces. Lin took it and held, measuring the strength of her own hand against the memory of it. The sleep-field hummed beneath them, that low note the body learned to ignore.

They walked the short loop the attendants permitted, Lin finding her feet faster than she ever had. Sixteen hours lent a confidence to the body, as if the extra hours had already been spent on certainty. The deck's floor carried its faint warmth in the transit lanes. She counted the banks aloud as she passed, the way she always did, but this time she did not stop at the third to ask Vey who she had become. She already felt the difference in herself, the longer tether, the mind that did not rush toward the dark the way it used to.

The deck revealed itself to a longer wake the way a room reveals itself to someone who has stopped rushing through it. Lin noticed the seam where the transit lane met the rail, the faint seam-glow that four-hour windows had always slid past. A sleeper three banks over exhaled once and stilled, the sound small and ordinary, and she heard it fully for the first time because she had the hours to hear it. The air carried the mineral cool of the lower rings, that clean cold which kept the suspended bodies at rest, and beneath it the sleep-field's hum rode steady, a note the attendants no longer heard. She let her palm rest on the rail's worn cap, feeling the places where generations of visitors had worn the polymer smooth, and the longer tether in her let her stand there without the old urgency to fill the time before the window closed.

At the bench the attendants left for visitors, they sat. Vey had brought the star lamp, the paper one with the star cut out, and she set it on the bench between them unlit. Lin picked it up. The paper was thicker than she remembered, repaired along one crease with a careful strip of adhesive.

"You mended it," Lin said.

"I mended it," Vey agreed. "You taught me. Left drawer, behind the audit forms."

Lin turned the lamp in the dim light. "I remember teaching you. I don't remember which window it was." She set it down. "But I remember the teaching. That's something the longer window hasn't taken."

Memory warmed the small object in her hands. She traced the star's cut edge with a fingernail, the crooked point Vey had never quite squared, and the gesture carried her back to the apartment above the transit hub where the lamp had first lived on the sill. There the real light had been thin, filtered through the hub's dust, and they had set the paper star against the dark because one honest light in a dim unit had felt like a small rebellion. Lin closed her eyes and the remembered warmth rose, the smell of the heater's dry metal, the particular quiet of a home kept by someone who refused to let the Schedule set the hour. Sixteen hours let her hold that room and this bench at once, the woman who left and the woman who had come back.

She ran a thumb along the repaired crease. The adhesive was Vey's work, careful and a little clumsy, the way Vey mended everything that was not a figure. "Do you remember the day I went under," she said. "Not the date. The shape of it."

Vey's face softened at the corners, the only part of her that ever truly let go. "You packed the left drawer. You left the lamp on the sill because you said a dark unit needed one light that was ours. You told me you'd be back before the star lamp needed a new wick. Then the window closed and the wick outlasted you by three surfaces."

"Three surfaces," Lin repeated, and the number sat in her mouth the way four and seventeen once had, except this time the number was hers to keep. "I left you with the lamp and a drawer full of forms and a promise the Schedule broke. I've been angry about that promise in the dark, you know. Not at you. At the door that closed."

"The door was a line in a ledger," Vey said. "I know that now. I didn't know it then. I stood at the rail the way you stood at every window, counting what was missing."

Lin set the lamp in her lap and looked at her partner across the rail. Vey had aged into the knowledge, that was clear, the way a person ages into a secret they cannot share. The left drawer was still a disaster, Lin would bet on it, but the rest of Vey had hardened around the finding the way a shell hardens around a grain. Her Vey, the auditor who trusted precision like honesty, had learned that precision without questioning was the very instrument of the lie. Lin could see it in the set of her shoulders.

"That's the difference in you," Lin said. "Not the bags. The knowing. You stopped trusting the summaries."

"I stopped trusting the summaries because the summaries were precise, and precision had always felt to me like honesty. The precision was real. The honesty was not." Vey said it the way she said everything now, with the flat certainty of a figure that survived a fourth look. "The Actuary reported exactly what it was instructed to report. That instruction committed the original sin of the Schedule."

Lin filed that away beside the lamp, the way she filed everything Vey handed her across the rail. Sixteen hours gave her room to hold more than a single bright thing.

"Tell me," Lin said. "Not who I've become. Who you've become, since the last time. You're different. You were always quiet at the rail. Now you're quiet like something's behind it."

Vey's mouth moved the way it did when she sorted a figure in her head before speaking it. "I found something at my desk," she said. "A credit that shouldn't exist. A Suspended citizen named Toll drew less than the Ledger claimed he was owed. One inverted cell. I followed it past the point where the Office permits following, and it opened onto the whole budget. The Accord rounds the Suspended draw upward and the harvest downward, two errors meeting in the middle to manufacture a shortfall. The rounding served as the instrument that kept the surplus invisible. Toll's little credit was the loose thread."

Lin followed the thread in her own mind, the single miscount becoming a world. "And the surplus at the bottom."

"The surplus at the bottom," Vey said. "Wide enough to wake the majority of the Strata and still close each year with the vaults fuller than the briefs admit. I named it to myself in plain words. Suspension is not necessity. It is policy." She reached into the satchel and drew out a folded sheet, the sealed kind the Office used for things it did not wish questioned. She held it a moment, then passed it over. "Read it. Slowly. We have the hours for slowly."

Lin unfolded the sheet. The handwriting was Vey's, precise and small, a column of figures and a sentence at the top in her careful auditor's print: the true energy budget. Below it, the arithmetic of a world. Lin read the harvest first, the thin trickle Mourn gave, and the reserve, three centuries of stored fusion drawn down slow. Then the population, nine billion, eight and a half of them Suspended, each drawing the minimal share. She followed Vey's additions the way Vey had clearly meant her to, and at the bottom of the column the remainder sat, enormous and unused.

"The Strata spends a fraction of what it has," she read aloud from the line Vey had written beneath the figures. "The rest it takes by theft."

She looked up. "This says we don't have to sleep."

"It says we never had to," Vey said. "The surplus is old. The Schedule was written over it. Every window you lost, every year of dark the Accord assigned you, it was paid for by a decision to keep this quiet, not by a shortage of light."

Lin read the sheet again, slower, the way Vey had said. The numbers did not shift when she read them a third time, and she understood, with the calm of someone who has just been handed the key to a door she had mistaken for a wall, that the dark of her life had not been weather. It had been a line in a ledger someone chose to write.

She thought of the others on her bank. The woman two rows over who had gone under the same year, whose wife counted nine years between windows. The builder three banks down whose son had learned only at nine that his father's sleep was not weather but a choice. Lin had met none of them awake, of course, because the Schedule kept them all dark at once, but she had felt them in the field, the quiet company of the suspended, the vast shared silence of eight and a half billion. The sheet named that silence for what it was: a subtraction the Accord performed on its own people to keep a column small.

"How long have you known," she said.

"Since the window before this one. I found it the night after I last sat at your rail. I didn't tell you then. Four hours wasn't enough to carry it."

"Sixteen is."

"Sixteen is," Vey agreed. "And I wasn't sure I should tell you even now. The surplus is a fact. What to do with it is a fight. I didn't want to wake you into a war."

"There's a group," Vey said. "Families of the Suspended. They call themselves the Wake. They've been organizing for seventy windows, waiting for a figure anyone can feel. I think this is the figure."

Lin turned the name over. The Wake. She had heard nothing of it in Suspension, of course, because Suspension heard nothing by design, but the word landed the way a struck note lands, clear and then ringing. "The Wake," she said. "People like us. People waiting at rails."

"People who've stopped waiting quietly," Vey said. "Their organizer is a woman named Joss. She's been building a count from the bottom, from the decks, from the families. She says the Office's own auditor has been asking the questions the Office doesn't want asked. She means me."

Lin looked down at the star lamp, at the star cut crooked into the paper. Sixteen hours, and a truth, and a movement with a name. The old Lin, the one who went under at FY291, would have taken the lamp and the news and held them both the way she held everything Vey passed across the rail, with gratitude and a little grief. But the longer window had given her more than time. It had given her the room to be someone other than the woman who only surfaced.

"I want to meet her," Lin said.

Vey blinked. "You don't have to. The fight is mine to carry. You've carried enough."

The longer window had changed the shape of wanting. In the four-hour windows she had spent the minutes measuring the gap, counting what the dark would take the moment the chime sounded, hoarding touch like a thing in short supply. Now the hours lay open before her, a field instead of a cup, and the old panic of the closing door had nowhere to land. She felt the difference in her shoulders, in the slow breath that did not rush toward the next silence. Vey had watered her from behind glass for eleven windows, careful and faithful, and Lin had taken it as love because it was the only form the Schedule permitted. To step off the sill was to admit the glass had been a choice someone made, not a wall the universe erected. The knowledge sat in her like a second heartbeat, steady and new.

"I've carried the sleep. That's not the same as carrying the cause." Lin leaned toward her across the rail, careful the way she always was, but deliberate now, the hold that said I am here, I am choosing this. "You've been keeping me on a sill, Vey, watering from the other side of the glass. Sixteen hours is a long time to sit on a sill. I'd rather be in the garden."

Vey's face did something Lin had not seen in any of the eleven windows before. It cracked, not with sadness but with relief, the particular look of a person who had been carrying a weight alone and had just been offered a second pair of hands. "The garden's a fight," Vey said, but she said it like someone naming a thing she was glad to name.

"Then I'll learn to fight," Lin said. "I've spent eleven windows learning to surface. That's training for something. Tell me about the Wake. Tell me what Joss needs."

Vey told her. She spoke of the middle-ring halls the Office had abandoned to storage and the Wake had taken back, the banners sewn from allocation forms, the child who drew the Strata as a ladder with people climbing it. She spoke of the map Joss was building, deck by deck, family by family, the count of years stolen that the Office had thrown away. Lin listened with the attention of someone who had been given her own life back in the form of a number, and she understood that the number was not abstract to her the way it might be to a clerk on an upper ring. The surplus was her sixteen hours. The surplus was every window she had lost.

"There's a risk," Vey said. "If you join, they'll know your name. The Office doesn't love the Wake. Joining puts you on a list."

"Put me on it." Lin picked up the star lamp and held it to the dim light, and the shadow on the bench was a star, lopsided and bright. "I've been on the Accord's list my whole adult life. Suspended by order. This is the first list that wants me awake."

Around them the deck kept its cold business, but the lit star threw a small warm circle on the bench and Lin watched the shadow breathe with the flame's slight waver. A far bank chimed, soft and indifferent, announcing another window elsewhere, and the sound no longer measured her own. She turned the lamp so the light caught Vey's face, mapping the lines that eleven surfaces had drawn, and felt the strange solidity of a name written down on the right side of a door for once. To be listed by the Wake was to be counted as present, as a citizen with a voice instead of a maintenance draw. The longer tether hummed in her blood, proof made warm in her palm, and she understood that joining was simply the body agreeing with what the numbers had already said. Somewhere a sleeper turned and stilled, and the ordinary mercy of the moment held her: she was awake, she was choosing, and the list that wanted her was one she could finally sign.

Vey reached across the rail and took her hand, and this time neither of them was the sleeper and neither the watcher. They were two people at a rail, both awake, both choosing. The monitor ticked its patient green, sixteen hours and counting, and the deck went on its cold business around them, a far bank chiming, a sleeper two rows over turning and stilling.

"I'll take you to the halls," Vey said. "After the window. Joss will want to meet the partner who finally got a day."

"Tell her I surfaced for a reason," Lin said. She set the lamp down between them, lit it from the small flame Vey carried in the satchel, and the paper star threw its crooked shadow across the bench and onto Vey's hand. "Tell her I'm done surfacing. I'm joining."

"What will you tell the others," Vey asked. "When Joss puts your name in the book. The families will want to know why a woman who was worth only four hours last year stands here with sixteen and a cause."

"I'll tell them the truth," Lin said. "That someone decided I was worth a day, and I decided the day wasn't enough. That sixteen hours showed me the door was never weather. That I'm done being a thing the Schedule deferred." She turned Vey's hand over in hers, the same grip, deliberate now. "And I'll tell them about you. The auditor who followed a loose thread and found the world. They'll want to know the figure has a face. You can be that face, or I can be the one who names it. Either way, the Wake gets the count it's been waiting for."

The lamp burned low and steady, the way a thing mended carefully will burn. Lin watched the shadow of the star move across Vey's knuckles as the hours passed, and she thought of the eight and a half billion the sheet had named, and she thought of the child's ladder, and she thought of the longer tether in her own body, the sixteen hours that proved the dark had been chosen. She had come up eleven times and Vey had caught her every one. This time she meant to come up and stay.

"I'm going to learn Joss's map," she said, and she said it the way one commits a debt, with relief. "I'm going to put my own name in the book of the Wake, beside the others. The woman who was worth a day is going to be worth the fight."

Vey smiled, and the smile was hers, the same one Lin had stored across eleven windows, but wider now, because it was shared. "Then we do it together," she said. "The cause stops being mine and starts being ours. You'll meet Bren, the one who takes the east clusters. And the grandmother who outlived three children into the dark. They'll know you by the lamp."

"By the lamp," Lin agreed. She lifted the star lamp so its light fell across both their faces, and for a moment the dim deck and the cold rail and the long rows of sleepers fell away, and there were only two people and a paper star and the plain fact that the dark had been chosen and could be unmade. "Tell them Lin woke longer. Tell them she read the count and put her name down. Tell them the next window won't close on her the way the others did, because this time she's the one holding the door."

She thought of the halls Vey had described, the banners sewn from discarded allocation forms, the child's drawing of the Strata as a ladder with people climbing. A place where the Suspended were treated as present instead of deferred. Lin meant to stand in that room with a lamp in her hand and a name in the book, and she meant to be the proof that a woman worth sixteen hours was worth the whole fight.

The green numeral ticked lower, but slowly, with the luxury of a day to spend. Lin leaned against the rail, the star lamp warm in her palm, and for the first time in the long years of her Suspension she was not counting the dark between windows. She was counting the hours she had been given, and the name of the movement that had been waiting for her, and the partner whose secret she now carried as her own. The Wake would have her. The surplus would wake the others. And Lin, who had surfaced smaller than she went under every time before, felt for the first time the opposite pull, the sense of a self growing into the light instead of shrinking from it.

She closed her hand around the lamp. Sixteen hours. A truth. A name. The beginning of a fight she had not chosen but would now refuse to leave. Vey's private cause had become, in the space of a single long window, the thing they would carry together, and Lin meant to carry it all the way to the light.

Chapter 12 — The Buried Clause

The credential Mirek had pressed into her palm still carried the cold of the vault. Vey set it on her desk in the audit wing and woke the terminal to the raw layer, the feed that bypassed the Actuary's summary. Three nights had passed since he keyed her standing access, and she had spent each of them reading downward through the unsummarized record, the columns of individual writes that the fold had always smoothed into the Scarcity Brief. Toll had been the first. Then a ring. Then the whole span of Suspension entries the Strata had ever logged. The arithmetic had stopped surprising her somewhere around the second hundred thousand. A surplus sat there in plain sight, the same surplus the machine had named when she queried it, and the reconciliation tags marched beside every entry like a second handwriting correcting the first.

She had the number. She had the Actuary's confession that it had been instructed to present scarcity. What she lacked, and what the petition to unseal would require, was proof that the falsification reached back to the people who wrote the Accord itself. Mirek's voice returned to her as she worked: a note somewhere near the first writes, a reason or an order. If it existed, it existed in the founding-era store, and now she could reach it.

The query field accepted a scope she had never been permitted to name before. Vey typed the deep index, the span of entries the Actuary's public summaries never reached, the allocations written in the first cycles after the Dimming. The display answered slowly, as though the oldest records resented being disturbed. Where the modern store scrolled in clean columns, the founding-era writes arrived in a sparser format, each line deliberate, each stamped by hands three centuries cold. She read them the way she read everything, checking each entry against the one above it, letting the pattern declare itself rather than forcing it.

The format itself was a kind of testimony. Where the modern store rendered every write as an identical row, the founding entries carried the irregularities of human hands: a heavier stroke on a number that mattered, a thinner one on a line the writer rushed. Vey had audited ten years of clean columns generated by the Actuary's own formatter, and she found herself noticing the grain of the older record the way a reader notices a voice after years of silent print. The deliberate spacing between grant and reconciliation, the care taken to keep the two figures on separate lines, spoke of a mind that knew exactly what it was separating. She felt the small procedural awe of an auditor who had finally reached the layer beneath the layer, the raw stone under the polished floor.

The earliest allocations told a straightforward story. The First Signatories had granted themselves legacy wake, perpetual consciousness debited against a collective pool, and they had granted the ordinary citizen a measured share of awake years. The math of that first Schedule balanced. It was a ration, but it was an honest one, and for a handful of cycles the raw entries showed the Strata drawing close to what it spent. Then the reconciliation routine appeared in the record, already running, tagging gaps from the very first write the store held. Mirek had been right. The fold had not grown up over generations of drift. It had been present at the beginning.

Vey followed the routine backward through its first invocations, tracing the tag from the modern Suspension decks down to the founding ledger where it first stirred. Each early entry carried the same quiet signature: a gap measured, a gap folded, a gap removed from the count the Office would one day call truth. The reconciliation did not announce itself as a correction of the figures. It presented as routine maintenance, the small regular write of a system keeping its books. Only by laying the raw draw beside the raw grant, the way she had laid Toll's side by side, did the removal show its shape. The Founders had built the removal into the store before the store had anything left to remove.

She scrolled past the founding allocations into the maintenance records that surrounded them, the Suspension orders and wake-window grants that the early Strata had logged by hand. Most of the marginal space beside those entries stood empty, the clean column of an administration that trusted its own figures. But one block sat apart. It had been written into the edge of a Suspension ledger from the third cycle, a note set in the margin where the summary would never surface it, in a hand the archive had preserved without ever reading.

She noted the discipline of the surrounding pages, the clean columns where the early Strata had logged its work and left the margins bare. An administration that trusted its figures did not argue with them in the edges. The one exception drew her eye the way a quiet room draws it to a single moved chair.

She slowed. The note ran only a few lines, and it did not read like the administrative chatter of the early Office. It read like a man explaining a decision to himself, or to whoever might one day dig beneath the fold and find it. The writer recorded that the true energy margin exceeded the allocation the public Schedule claimed to require. He noted that the scarcity the Accord presented to the rings had been chosen, not measured. He set down, in the plain spare language of the founding clerks, that the First Signatories had reviewed the honest figures and elected to publish the smaller ones.

The pen paused there. The next line carried the weight of the whole discovery. Whoever wrote it stated plainly that the Founders knew the energy math was falsified, that the surplus had been visible from the first cycle, and that they had kept it hidden so the ration of wake-time would hold the shape they had already decided upon. It was not a confession wrung from a later machine. It was a founding hand, setting the lie into the margin of the record it was about to bury.

The weight of it landed not in the words but in their calm. Whoever had written this had not been forced, had not been caught; he had set the falsification down the way a clerk records a balance, certain the margin would never be read and therefore needing no softening. Vey sat with the strange intimacy of a document that had expected no witness for three hundred years and yet had been written as if one might arrive. The Founders she had filed under abstraction, the First Signatories of the civics primers, resolved here into a single hand that had decided nine billion people would sleep so a few could stay awake. Abstraction had shielded the decision for three hundred years. The margin removed the shield.

The writer lingered on the arithmetic of the choice. He set the true margin of the Strata beside the smaller figure the public Schedule would carry, and he noted that the gap between them, multiplied across nine billion allocations, accounted for the whole of the legacy wake the Signatories had reserved for themselves. The scarcity the rings feared funded the consciousness the Founders kept. He wrote this without flourish, the way a clerk records a balance that happens to be the shape of a theft. Vey had searched the modern store for months to prove what this dead hand had noted in an afternoon and then buried where no summary would ever climb.

Vey read the four lines again, then a third time, because the mind resists the thing it has spent years being trained not to see. The note did not argue. It did not apologize. It simply recorded, with the flat precision of a clerk who expects no one to read him, that scarcity was a decision the Founders had made while the true numbers lay open on the same page. She had carried the surplus from the raw store to the Actuary's own voice. Now the founding store offered the harder fact: the people who wrote the Accord had known.

Her training insisted the figures could still reconcile, that somewhere a clean total slept beneath the corruption the way a true star slept beneath the dimming sky. The note refused that comfort. It named the smaller figure as a choice and the larger as a concealment in the same unhurried hand, leaving no seam where an honest original might have hidden. Vey had spent nine years trusting that the Accord, whatever its errors, described a real shortage it had no power to invent. The margin proved the shortage had been invented. She felt the precise vertigo of an auditor whose own discipline had been the instrument of the lie, who had counted faithfully inside a count designed to mislead.

She copied the block into her private record, the quiet channel that held a finding for review without raising it to the floor. The founding-era bytes transferred without corruption, the old format intact, the marginal hand preserved exactly as the archive had kept it for three hundred years. Vey tagged the entry THE BURIED CLAUSE and closed the viewer, then opened it again to be certain the copy matched. It did. The words had not changed in the reading, and they would not change in the telling.

She set the note beside the Actuary's confession in her mind and read the two together. The machine had told her it was instructed to present scarcity, that the directive predated the current Council, that it named the edge the brief must show. The note supplied the missing half of that sentence. The directive had an author, and the author had written the edge into the margin of the founding store before the first ordinary citizen was ever Suspended. The instruction the Actuary carried was not some later corruption of a clean original. It descended in an unbroken line from the hand that had chosen the smaller figure and then sealed the larger one away. Vey had suspected the falsification reached the top. The note proved it reached the beginning.

The phrase settled in her mind with the force of a seal closing. She had spent nine years treating the Scarcity Brief as the Strata's honest face. The Actuary had told her it was instructed. The note told her the instruction had an author who sat at the founding table. The moral center of the whole account shifted on that small pivot: this was not a machine that had drifted, nor a later Office that had corrupted a clean original. The falsification reached back to the founding pen. The Accord had been written twice, once in the Schedule the Founders filed and once in the margin they never filed, and the second writing governed nine billion lives.

She thought of Vask, who had warned her that the Accord was not questioned, who believed the scarcity because the brief reported it. He would not be able to hold that belief against this. A supervisor who trusted the columns would have to reckon with a column written by the Founders themselves, confessing the count was false. The petition she had filed asked the Council to open the Original Allocation. The note did not open it. It reached further back, to the hand that had sealed it shut.

She thought of Lin, three levels down in the Suspension deck, owed a life the Schedule had spent elsewhere. Lin had lost most of their shared years to a shortage the Actuary now said had been instructed, and the note said the instruction came from the people who founded the world Lin slept inside. Vey had not yet told Lin the full shape of what she had found. The founding note gave her the sentence she would need, the one that rearranged every quiet grief of the lower rings: the scarcity was not a verdict of the failing star. The Founders knew.

The desk lamp in the audit wing threw a narrow pool of light across her record. Around it the wing ran dark and orderly, the rows of auditors' stations empty at this hour, the reconciliation feeds ticking their overnight arithmetic toward a brief that would again report the Strata at the edge of its reserves. None of those feeds would touch the founding-era store. The fold had been built to keep the early writes quiet, and it had kept them quiet for three hundred years. Mirek's access opened the first breach in that quiet, and the note was what the breach had been waiting to surrender.

Vey considered the weight of a single marginal hand against the whole architecture of the Office. The Actuary's confession named an instruction but not a name. The note named no office either, only the Founders in the aggregate, the First Signatories who had reviewed the honest figures and chosen the smaller story. That was enough.
