A petition to unseal could argue that the Original Allocation, once opened, would confirm the falsification. The note argued it already, from inside the founding store, in a hand that predated every Council now living.

She opened the record channel again and wrote the line that mattered most, the one the petition would one day quote: the raw Ledger holds a founding-era marginal note proving the Founders knew the energy math was falsified, that scarcity was chosen, and that the surplus was concealed from the first cycle. She set the note's index beside it so the finding could be located by any auditor with Mirek's access. Then she closed the channel the way she closed every private finding, without flagging it upward, holding the evidence for the moment a charge could be made of it.

The credential lay on the desk where Mirek had placed it, a small physical token that survived the pulling of his badge. Through it the founding store remained reachable from her own station, no vault visit required, no technician's risk renewed. Vey turned it once in her fingers. The man had given her the means to read what he had spent eleven years not reading, and the first thing she had found beneath the fold showed the Founders' own admission that the fold was intentional. He would want to know what the early writes contained. She was not yet ready to tell him, because the note changed the nature of his risk: he had guarded an honest record whose honesty had been used to publish a lie, and now the lie had a founding signature.

She looked at the line she had written, THE BURIED CLAUSE, and felt the old precision of her training settle into a new shape. For nine years she had audited the Accord's accounts and trusted the totals. The surplus had broken the totals. The Actuary had broken the trust in the machine. The note broke the last assumption, that the corruption had entered later, that somewhere in the founding Schedule an honest figure still slept. There was no honest figure sleeping. The Founders had written the buried clause beside the true one and published the false.

The discovery rearranged her sense of the whole investigation. Until the note, she had pictured a slow rot, an honest Accord corrupted by some later hand that learned to bend the summary for its own comfort. The founding store corrected the picture. The rot had a date, and the date sat at the founding itself, in the third cycle, in a marginal hand that confessed the truth without shame. Whoever wrote it had expected no reader. That expectation had held for three hundred years, protected by the fold, by the Seal, by an Office that punished the asking of exactly the question Vey had now answered.

She weighed what the note could do that the surplus alone could not. The raw store had shown a Strata rich beyond its brief, but a surplus could be explained away as prudence, as a reserve held against the long dark, as the ordinary caution of a failing star. The Actuary's confession had named an instruction, but an instruction could be blamed on an anonymous office long dead. The note named the Founders in the act of knowing. It turned prudent scarcity into a chosen starvation, and it placed the choice at the table where the Accord was written. No later Council could be scapegoated for a decision the First Signatories had signed in the margin.

The petition she had filed asked the Council to break the Seal and open the Original Allocation. Vey saw now that the note did not replace that petition. It armed it. When the Schedule opened, the finding she had copied would sit waiting in the founding store as an independent witness, a founding hand confirming what the opened document would show. The Council could refuse to read the margin. It could not refuse that the margin had been written, by the same hands, in the same cycles, beside the very Schedule the Seal protected. The buried clause would travel with the Original Allocation into the light, whether the Signatories willed it or not.

The audit wing held its silence around her. Somewhere above, the duty lamps kept their blue wash across the rings, and the reconciliation feeds ran their overnight arithmetic toward a morning brief that would again report scarcity. Vey had learned to read beneath that brief, and the reading had carried her all the way to the founding pen. The note on her record was small, a few lines of a dead clerk's hand, but it bore the whole weight of the accusation the Wake had lacked the numbers to make. She had the numbers now, and she had the confession, and she had the founding signature that tied the one to the other.

Dawn would reach the upper rings before the Office stirred, and with it the first shift of auditors who would confirm the brief without once questioning the edge it showed. Vey would file nothing to the floor tonight. The finding would rest in her private channel, a quiet seed, until the petition forced the Council's hand and the Original Allocation could be opened beside it. She had waited nine years to trust the columns. She would wait a little longer to name what they had always hidden.

The rings turned beyond the audit wing's viewing pane, sixty levels turning their patient circle around the failing star. Somewhere in that circle Lin slept, and somewhere in the lower decks a child grew toward a wake-window she might never reach. The note in the founding store connected them all to a decision made at the beginning, by the people who had promised the Strata its ration and then halved the truth of what they owned. Vey had the connection now, set in a founding hand, preserved in the margin the summaries had never reached.

She saved the record and let the terminal dim. The credential stayed on the desk, cold and whole, the door Mirek had opened and would not be able to close. Tomorrow the Office would run its reconciliation as it had for three hundred years, folding the surplus out of the brief and presenting the Strata at the edge of a scarcity the Founders had chosen. Vey would return to her counting. But the count had changed. She was no longer auditing a mistake. She was auditing a founding decision, signed in the margin, and the signature belonged to the people who wrote the world.

She thought once more of the clerk who had written the note and then sealed it under his own hand. He had expected no reader in three centuries, and he had been nearly right. The fold had done its work without his name ever surfacing, and the Office had punished curiosity the way it punished every breach of the calm. Vey wondered whether he had meant the note as a safeguard or as a confession, a line dropped into the record so that the truth would survive even if the Schedule did not. Either way it had survived, preserved by the very integrity Mirek spent his life defending, and it had reached the one reader who had learned to read beneath the fold.

The clerk had written in the spare administrative shorthand of his office, every term exact, every figure placed where the summary would never pull it upward. Vey found a strange respect in the precision, the way a forger's care can outlast the forgery. He had not sloppied the lie into a corner. He had recorded it with the same measured hand that logged the honest grants above it, trusting the fold to do the hiding and the Office to do the forgetting. That trust had held longer than any Signatory's name. The margin survived because it had been written by someone who believed no one would ever descend to read it, and because the data Mirek guarded kept its integrity even when the story built on it did not.

The lamp held its narrow pool of light a moment longer, then she keyed the station dark. From the deep store the buried clause kept its three-hundred-year silence, no longer alone, because now someone honest had read it and copied it down.

Chapter 13 — Vask Changes Sides

The morning after the Actuary's confession reached his desk, Orren Vask arrived before the shift chime. He had not slept the approved span. His allocation of wake-years had not grown shorter for the lost hours, but something in him had, a thin certainty that the columns he had trusted for thirty years were only as honest as the hand that commanded them. The corridor to his station ran dim under the lumen budget, and for the first time he noticed the dimness as a choice someone had made, not a condition the star had forced.

To arrive before the chime was to walk a corridor that had not yet been told how to look. The cleaning cycles had passed, leaving the floor's worn center stripe brighter than its edges, and Vask found himself tracing that stripe the way a clerk traces a column to its total. He had walked this path ten thousand times without seeing the wear. The seeing now felt less like discovery than like having read a word correctly for years and only now hearing the sound it made.

He set his palm to the desk panel and pulled the records he had spent his career refusing to read. Vey Corran had shown him the shape of the thing in fragments. The Actuary, queried directly, had spoken the rest in its measured voice: it had been instructed to present scarcity, three hundred years back, by an office no longer named. The machine enforced the lie without authoring it. Vask had filed that exchange when it crossed his queue, and his hand had not trembled, because a supervisor's hand did not tremble at a finding. It trembled now, at the finding's meaning.

The Office of the Accord trained its managers to administer, not to interrogate. Vask had administered. He had warned Vey off in this very room, told her the Accord was not questioned, drawn the line she was not to cross. She had crossed it anyway, and the crossing had led to the Actuary's own words. A man who had spent his life defending the line now had the line's source in his hand, and the source was an instruction written before the current Council drew breath.

He read the surplus first. The raw Ledger of Hours, the un-summarized store Mirek had opened to Vey, held the true energy budget in plain arithmetic. Billions of wake-years the scarcity brief had never shown. Vask had signed suspension confirmations by the hundred, each one moving a citizen from the bright side of the Schedule to the dark, each one accounted under a shortage the records themselves disproved. The discrepancy he had called within tolerance, years before, in Toll's file, was not a rounding error. It was a single cell of a vast surplus the Office had been told to hide.

The numbers did not glare. They sat in their rows the way Mirek's store had held them, unstyled by the brief's insistence, and Vask found a strange steadiness in their plainness. A surplus that announced itself without emphasis was harder to dismiss than a shortage that demanded belief. He ran a finger down one column and felt the old supervisor's satisfaction of a balanced page, except the balance now cut against every confirmation he had signed. The arithmetic offered no argument. It only stood, and stood, and would not move when he looked away.

He pulled the certification logs next. For eleven years he had signed the annual Seal certification, attesting the Original Allocation unbroken. The registry marks lined his memory like a fence he had helped rebuild every season. Those marks did not themselves name the instruction, but they proved the Office had spent a decade affirming a lock it had never been permitted to inspect. A supervisor who certified a seal he could not open had certified a faith, not a fact.

The third record he gathered sat deeper, in the routing history of Vey's petition. He had helped her build the filing in proper form, fed her the Order's registry mark, told her to attack the lock and not the locksmith. The Actuary's log of that filing carried the machine's own admission that the petition met form. The same log, read against the instruction layer the Actuary had described, showed the brief and the record as two different computations. Vask had always trusted the brief. The brief now read as the edge the directive demanded, and the edge was a story someone had chosen to tell.

He copied the three sets onto a single portable store, the kind auditors carried between desks when a finding had to travel with its evidence. The weight of the store in his palm was small. The weight of what it proved was not. A by-the-book man did not walk his records to an auditor who had been warned off. He logged them, certified them, and returned to the queue. Vask had returned to the queue for thirty years. The queue no longer looked like order. It looked like a route someone had built to keep the asking from arriving at its answer.

He had compiled findings before, always for the Office, always to affirm. To compile against it required the same care and returned a different quiet. The store's seal authenticated under his own certification key, the same key that had marked the Seal for eleven years, and the small recursive honesty of that pleased a part of him he had not expected to still possess. A man could not certify his own correction with a key that the correction called into question. The key did not care. The key only opened.

He stood. The chair's joints made the small sound the partition absorbed. He did not summon Vey; he went to her, because a man crossing his own line did not ask the line to come to him. The walk took him past the audit desks where clerks reconciled draws against allocations, each confirmation a life moved and accounted, each one performed beneath the brief he now held to be instructed. None of the clerks looked up. They had been told the weather and had dressed for it, the way he had taught them.

Vey's station showed the cold blue of a working screen. She looked up the way she always did, with the patience of someone who read ledgers line by line and did not startle at a supervisor's shadow. Vask set the store on her desk beside her hand. He did not sit. Sitting signaled a conversation he did not want echoed, and this conversation he did want echoed, into the record, past the office that had authored the silence.

"I read the Actuary's exchange," he said. His voice held the level tone he used for a finding, not the warning tone he had used in this room before. "You were right that the columns pull. I was wrong that the pulling stopped at a rounding."

She did not reach for the store. "You told me the Accord is not questioned."

"I told you what I was trained to tell you." Vask kept his hands at his sides, the posture of a man giving evidence rather than receiving it. "The Actuary told you it was instructed. That changes the nature of the thing. A question about the Accord asks whether the Founders erred. A question about the instruction asks who wrote the scarcity, and the who is an office, not a star. The star does not author briefs."

Vey turned the store so the screen lit. She read the surplus, the certification marks, the routing history, the Actuary's own line that the brief followed the directive while the raw measurement followed the records. Her face did not change, but her shoulders settled the way they did when she had decided to hear the rest before answering.

"These are Office records," she said at last.

"They are." Vask had expected relief at saying it. He felt none. He felt the particular cold of a man who had spent his life inside a wall and now stood outside it, with no door behind him. "The surplus corroborates your budget. The certification history shows the Seal affirmed without inspection. The routing log shows the brief and the record as separate computations. Together they name the instruction without needing the directive's author."

"You are giving me your own logs." Vey looked up. "A supervisor's records carry weight a petitioner's do not. If you stand behind these, Edda Marn has to answer a man inside the Office, not an auditor at its edge."

"I am not giving them to you as a gift." Vask met her eyes. "I am filing them. The form you used for the petition admits corroborating evidence from any officer with standing to attest it. I have that standing. I certified the Seal for eleven years. If the certification rested on a faith, the certifier owes the record its correction."

She was quiet for a moment. In the corridor beyond the partition a chime sounded the change of shift, and the soft shuffle of waking clerks passed without entering, the Office turning on its arithmetic the way a body turns on a heartbeat. Vask had once found comfort in the rhythm. The rhythm now read as a habit the evidence had broken.

For a moment the broken rhythm offered its own kind of ease. He had carried the comfort of the queue as a weight he mistook for rest, and setting it down left his posture lighter even as it left his footing uncertain. The relief was not warm. It was the relief of a clerk who finds the total finally reconciles, the page no longer fighting the hand that wrote it. He had not known how much the fighting had cost until the page went still.

"What made you decide," she asked, "after you warned me off?"

He considered the question the way he considered a ledger entry, looking for the line where the column bent. "The instruction." He said the word plainly. "I could hold the Actuary's rounding as weather. A machine that rounds up is a machine doing its math. A machine that says it was told to round up is a machine reading someone else's handwriting. I spent thirty years administering a handwriting I never saw. The Actuary showed me the hand. I can no longer claim I did not read it."

Vey nodded, not agreement exactly but the acknowledgment of a precise mind meeting a precise one. "The defects in the brief were always there. You are the first supervisor to say so on record."

The word landed. Vask felt it as a small stone settling, the same stone he had carried since the day he warned her off, now set down where someone else could weigh it aloud. "The defects are in the method, not the math. The directive chose the conservative edge. The edge hid the surplus. The surplus proves the edge was a decision, not a measurement. I will say that under oath if the Council requires it."

"You would testify." Vey's voice held no surprise, only a quiet tallying, the way she counted a finding into the file.

"I would attest." Vask corrected her with the care of a supervisor who knew the difference between a statement and a charge. "Testimony is what a witness gives a court. Attestation is what an officer gives the record. The Office runs on attestation. I have attested the Seal unbroken for eleven years. I will now attest it was affirmed without the inspection the certification implied. The correction belongs in the same book."

He watched her weigh the offer. Vey Corran did not trust easily; she verified. That was the trait that had carried her from Toll's credit to the Actuary's voice, and it was the trait he had once feared in her. He feared it no longer. A Strata that could not be verified by its own auditors was a Strata that had mistaken obedience for order.

"I need you to name the defects in writing," she said. "The petition alleges a basis to open, not falsification. Your attestation can allege what mine cannot, because you held the certification."

"The defects I will name are procedural." Vask had thought this through on the walk down, the way he thought through a routing. "The Seal was certified annually without access to the instruction layer. The scarcity brief was presented as the Ledger's own reading when the Actuary states it followed a directive. The surplus in the raw store was excluded from the public brief by the choice of edge. Three defects, each attested by an officer who administered the very process that contained them."

Vey reached for a blank form, the same kind Vask had handed her for the petition, real stock the Actuary would not process by voice. "Write it as a companion filing. Standing: supervising allocations officer, eleven years' certification. Provision: the sealing Order, by its mark. Remedy: the Council orders the instruction layer opened to the petitioner's audit."

He took the form. His hand was steady. A supervisor who had spent his life signing confirmations found the act of signing a correction came as easily as he expected, because the correction followed the same discipline. State the condition. Name the authority. Request the act. The form did not care which side of the line the request fell on. The form only demanded that it be answered.

He wrote the attestation in the Office's own language, the language he had used for a decade of certifications, so that the Keeper of the Seal would find no tone to flag. He cited the Order by its registry mark, the mark he had signed beside for eleven years. He named the three defects without accusation, the way an audit named a miscount: as a condition the record showed. He closed with the request that the Council open the instruction layer to Vey's audit, that the directive's author might be named at last.

Vey read the finished attestation. "You defect the brief itself. That is the line no supervisor has crossed."

"The brief defected first," Vask said. "It reported a shortage the records disproved. I am only the officer willing to write the discrepancy where the Keeper must see it."

She almost smiled. "You are still on the side of procedure not being a lie. You have not become a rebel."

"I have become the man whose rule, followed honestly, points at the door." He gave her the line he had given himself on the walk down, because it was true and because she would use it better than he could. "Procedure split from the Accord the day the directive was written. I spent thirty years not noticing the seam. The Actuary pointed at it. You widened it. I will stand in it."

To stand in the seam was to find that procedure, followed without flinching, had been carrying him toward this door all along. The relief he felt was small and dry, the relief of a man who learns the wall he defended was thinner than the wind it was built against. He had feared the crossing would require a new self. It required only the old discipline turned to face the other way, and the old discipline, he found, obeyed.

Vask carried the attestation to the logging desk on the level below, the same desk where Vey had filed the petition, the corner where the Actuary's presence ran through the wall in a low hum. He laid the form flat and spoke the filing code for a supervising officer's attestation, a code he knew by heart from the other side of the desk. The machine answered in its measured voice, accounting tone, no warmth, no judgment.

"Supervising officer attestation received. Officer standing confirmed by certification history. Three defects named. The filing meets form."

The words landed with a weight Vask had not expected. He had administered the Office's forms his whole career; he had never before been the citizen the form admitted against the Office's own practice. The Actuary logged the attestation without hesitation, because a form in proper shape was a form in proper shape regardless of who stood behind it. The machine did not care which side of the line the request served. The machine only required that the request be answerable.

"Log the filing," Vask said.

"Logged. Routed to the Signatory Council for receipt, in the ordinary course." The hum shifted by a note, the only sound the Actuary made for a new entry. "The supervising officer's attestation is now on record."

On record. Vask had spoken those words to Vey when her petition logged; now they described his own. A thing on record could not be un-filed. The Council would have to receive it. Edda Marn would have to answer a man who had certified the Seal she defended, and the answering would carry the weight of his years. He had not become her enemy. He had become the evidence she could no longer dismiss as an auditor's enthusiasm.

He walked back to his station by the approved route, the route he had walked for thirty years, and found it unchanged and entirely different. The desk panel showed the regular flow of the morning: suspensions scheduled, wake-windows announced, allocations debited and credited in their quiet arithmetic. The arithmetic had not changed. His reading of it had. The Strata turned on this arithmetic the way a body turns on a heartbeat, but the heartbeat, he now knew, had been told to keep a slower rhythm than the body required.

He opened his log and recorded the attestation as filed, the entry clean, the citation exact, the three defects named in the Office's own tongue. He did not log a doubt. The doubt had resolved into an act, and the act was its own kind of peace, the peace of a man who had finally read the handwriting he had spent his life administering. The line he had drawn for Vey, years before, had been a wall built to keep the wind off. He had stepped through the wall. The wind was cold, and it was honest, and it was the first weather he had faced in thirty years of indoor work.

Below his window the upper ring ran dim and regular, lit to the Accord's approved measure, the dimness now legible to him as a choice rather than a condition. Somewhere in the lower levels the Suspended lay in their decks, billions of citizens kept by minimal shares beneath a scarcity the records disproved, their waking years lent to a story of lack that an instruction had authored. Vask had been the certifier of that story. He was now its correction, and the correction sat on the record beside Vey's petition, two filings in the ordinary course, the machinery of the Reckoning turning whether the Signatories willed it or not.

He thought of Pell, the thin woman who had held his desk before him, who taught him the Schedule kept the peace and you did not recount the price. She had meant it as care. The institution had meant it as a wall. Vask had spent his career on the wall's safe side. He had stepped to its other face now, and found the face he had defended was the one that had hidden the surplus from the people who paid for it in sleep. The care Pell offered had been real. The wall it served had not been.

The relief that came with understanding Pell arrived later than the rest, quiet as a closing entry. She had taught him to keep the peace because she had believed the peace was real, and the believing had kept her own hand steady on the desk he now held. To know the wall had used her care did not erase the care. It returned it to her, and to him, as something he could finally hold without the institution standing between.

The shift chime sounded at last, and the corridor filled with the soft motion of clerks beginning their reconciliations. Vask took his place in the queue and signed the first suspension confirmation of the day, the mark set with the same hand that had signed the attestation an hour before. The signature meant something different now. It affirmed a life moved to the dark under a shortage he had named as instructed, and the naming did not stop the moving but it changed the meaning of his mark upon it. A supervisor who knew the defects could no longer sign in blindness. He signed in knowledge, and knowledge was the only correction the Office had ever lacked.

He looked once more at the dim line of the ring, then pulled the next file forward, and did the work the Office paid him to do, the stone at last set down, the line at last crossed, the attestation at last on record where no later refusal could quietly cut it. The Accord he had defended for thirty years had not fallen. It had been shown to rest on an instruction, and an instruction, once named, could be answered by the Council that the Founders had charged to govern. Vask had become the man who named it. The Strata turned on, most of its people dark and dreaming, accounted for and now, at last, contested by the very officer sworn to keep the count straight.

Chapter 14 — The Public Reckoning

The plaza filled before Joss finished the last count. She had chosen the middle-ring concourse because it sat at the seam of three population bands, where the wakeful workers of the upper decks passed the families of the low draw on their way between shifts. Word had traveled the way the Wake's word always traveled, through mouths and through the salvaged book, never through a channel the Office could close. By the second hour the crowd pressed against the rails of the transit bridges, and the lumen the Office permitted in a public space had never seemed so thin, or so inadequate to the number she was about to say aloud.

Bren found her at the base of the speaking platform, a crate the Wake had hauled from the storage decks and bolted to the plaza floor.

"We have them," he said. "Every ring fed a count through the volunteers. Mirek's people slipped the raw tallies past the Actuary's summary, and Vey's audit gave us the frame. The figure holds."

Joss took the film from his hand. The square of aged stock carried the same kind of marks she had copied from the neighbors' schedule weeks ago, only multiplied across the whole Strata, every blank a year someone had stayed dark by administrative choice. She had asked, seventy windows back, for a number anyone can feel. The book in her hands had become that number.

"This is the bone in the story," she said. "Now we show them the bone."

She climbed the crate. The crowd did not hush all at once; it tightened, the way a room of the Suspended tightened when someone began to count them. Joss had spoken to halls and to families, but never to a gathered Strata, never to the wakeful and the waiting in the same breath. She let the silence arrive on its own.

The platforms of the middle ring held perhaps two thousand bodies, and the bridges above carried as many again, a density the Office never permitted in any sanctioned assembly. Joss felt the weight of so many held breaths the way a diver feels pressure, a slow press that asked her to be worthy of the silence. She had stood before halls of the Suspended and felt their patience like a held chord. This was that patience multiplied across every band of the Strata, and it would not wait long for a voice it could trust.

"My name is Joss," she said. "I organize the Wake. You know the Wake. You call it a nuisance, or a grief, or a thing your neighbor joined. Today I am not here to ask you to join. I am here to tell you what the Accord has spent."

She held the film up so the front rows could see the marks, though most would never read the fine tally. The number did not live in the marks. It lived in what the marks added to.

"The Office tells you the light is thin and the star is failing and the sleep of billions is the only way the rest of us stay warm. That story has carried three hundred years. It carried because no one outside the Office could read the Ledger. That ends here."

A clerk near the front, the kind who wore the Office's gray, shifted his weight. Joss saw it. She had expected the Office to send watchers; a public reckoning invited them the way a lit window invited the dark.

"I am going to give you one figure," she said. "Not a percentage. Percentages belong to the people who round them. A count. The surplus the Strata has held, in energy the Accord never spent, amounts to more than four billion wake-years. Four billion years of conscious life, hidden in the budget, debited against the sleep of everyone in the low draw so that legacy wake could stay solvent. The surplus paid for the silence of nine billion people."

The figure refused to shrink in Joss's mind no matter how many windows she turned it through. Four billion wake-years arrived fresh each time she reached for it, a weight with no familiar edge. It was not a sum the Office could dress in the soft language of reserves. It was years, each one a citizen kept dark by a line item someone had signed. She watched the crowd try to hold it and fail, then try again, the way a hand closes around something too large and learns the shape by the strain.

The plaza absorbed the number the way a body absorbs a blow, with a moment of stillness before the sound. Joss watched the faces. A worker in an upper-deck tunic looked at the gray-clad clerk beside him, then looked away, and the looking away was its own verdict. A grandmother at the rail, one Joss recognized from the halls, closed her hand on the rail as if to keep herself upright. The number had landed in the chest before it had landed in the mind, exactly as the Wake had planned.

Beneath the open dome the three population bands met in a single mass, the upper-deck workers still in their shift tunics, the low-draw families in the drab wraps of the Suspension halls, the transit crowds pressed shoulder to shoulder along the bridge rails. Joss had never seen the bands occupy one space without a barrier of schedule between them. The concourse had become a cross-section of the whole Strata, every ring leaning in to hear the same figure. A boy no older than the child at the platform stood on a crate at the edge of the crowd, his small face lit by the thin lumen, and he did not look away when the number reached him. He looked at his mother, then at Joss, and held the look as if committing the moment to a memory he would keep through the dark.

"The Wake did not invent this," Joss went on. "Vey Corran, an Office auditor, found the discrepancy in a single Suspension and followed it past the summary to the true budget. Orren Vask, her supervisor, brought the Office's own corroborating records when he broke from the Seal. Mirek, a Ledger technician, opened the raw store the Actuary was instructed to smooth. The Office's own people built the count. The Wake only carried it into the open."

A voice called from the bridges, a man's, raw with disuse. "Four billion years. How do we know it is not another curtain?"

Joss had prepared for this. She had learned from the grandmother who tested every claim for weight before trusting it.

"Because the count comes from your own decks," she said. "Every ring submitted its true schedules through the volunteers. The blanks on this film are your windows, the ones you were meant to surface and did not. The Office rounded them into weather. We unrounded them. If the figure is wrong, it is wrong in your favor, because we could not reach every family in the time we had. The number is a floor, not a ceiling."

She let that sit. The public had heard scarcity argued by the Office its whole life; it had never heard the surplus argued back by one of the deferred. The reversal did its own work.

"Think of it as a debt owed to the low draw," Joss said. "Four billion wake-years. If you spread that across the nine billion Suspended, it is nearly half a year of consciousness each, stolen and banked and never spent on them. A mother who wakes once every nine years has already lent the Accord more than the Accord admits she owns. The surplus funds the waking of the few by billing the sleep of the many. That is the whole engine, and today it sits in the open."

A child near the platform, too young for an allocation, tugged at the sleeve of the woman beside her. "Is that why my mother is dark?" the child asked, loud in the quiet.

The woman did not answer right away. Then: "Yes. That is why."

Joss felt the old anger move through her, the one that had started the window after her brother went down. She did not let it sharpen her voice. The public turned on a feeling it supplied itself, not on a speaker's heat.

"The Council will tell you the Seal protects stability," she said. "Edda Marn will tell you legacy wake buys continuity. Let them. Let them argue that the waking of the few is worth the sleep of the many. Let them say it in front of the four billion years they hid. The reckoning is not a petition the Council can ignore. The reckoning is this plaza, and the next plaza, and the rings that carry the count home to the decks that fed it."

Bren moved through the crowd with copies of the film, passing squares of stock to hands that reached. Joss watched the count walk outward, the way she had promised in the halls it would, into the workforce rings, into the transit bridges, into the homes of people who had accepted a low ceiling as the sky. The public did not need to understand the Ledger. It needed to hold the number.

"The Office will answer," Joss said. "It always answers. It will say the figure is incomplete, or premature, or a threat to order. Remember that the Actuary was instructed to present scarcity. The machine did not lie on its own. Someone wrote the instruction, and someone signed the Seal, and someone decided that your dark was the price of their light. We are here to make that decision public."

A woman at the front, the one with the child, raised her hand. Joss nodded to her.

"What happens after today?" the woman asked. "The banners in your halls say WE COUNT. We counted. Then what?"

Joss stepped to the edge of the crate, close enough to meet the woman's eyes.

"Then the Council cannot pretend it does not know," she said. "A Strata that has seen the count and understood it is a Strata the Seal no longer protects. Adune and Edda will split, or they will not. The recomputed Accord will come before them, or it will not. But the number is spoken now, in the open, by the people the Accord deferred. They cannot unhear it. That is the whole of what we came to do."

The plaza held the weight of the sentence. Joss could read them the way she read a room of the Suspended, by the set of the shoulders and the angle of the eyes. They believed the number because they had lived the blanks it was built from. Belief that came from the rail of a Suspension deck did not dissolve under an Office summary.

She raised the film one more time.

"This is the count," she said. "Four billion wake-years, hidden in the surplus, spent on the silence of the many. Take it home. Show it to the clerk who rounds your window down. Show it to the Signatory who signed the Seal. Tell them the Wake counted, and the Strata saw, and the dark was a choice. The choice can be unmade. That is the work now, and it belongs to all of you."

She stepped down. The crowd did not disperse so much as reorganize, clusters forming around the volunteers with the copies, voices rising into the kind of argument that precedes a decision. The gray-clad clerk stood where he was, and Joss marked the stillness in him, the way a man marks the moment a wall he trusted develops a crack.

Bren came back to her side, the last of the film distributed.

"They are reading it on the bridges," he said. "Someone is sending it to the upper decks by hand. It will be across the Strata before the Office finishes its response."

"Good," Joss said. "The response belongs to the next fight. The count is today's."

She walked the edge of the plaza, touching a shoulder, repeating a name, fixing a face to a deck the way she had in the halls. The child with the drawing was not here, but the ladder she had drawn lived in Joss's mind, people climbing, and at the top a small wrong-colored sun. The number gave the ladder its rungs, made it something the Strata could climb. The Strata would climb it or refuse it, but it could no longer say it had not been shown the way up.

A newcomer stopped her, a young worker from the upper workforce rings, the kind who rarely looked down. He held a square of the film like a thing that had rearranged his life.

"I thought Suspension was weather," he said.

"So did I," Joss said. "So did my brother, before the Office took his waking and never explained the math to his face. The number explains it. Carry it upward. The people who decide have never stood at the rail. Make them stand now, by showing them what they paid for their light."

He nodded and moved off into the crowd, the film folded tight in his fist. Joss watched him go and added a line to the map in her head: one more of the wakeful, one more deck turned toward the count.

Bren had been moving among the workers of the upper decks, the ones who rarely looked down, and he came back with a report that sat uneasily on his face.

"The upper-deck crowd is angry," he said. "Not at us. At the Office. They paid their allocations in good faith, thinking the light was thin, and now they learn their waking was bought with the sleep of the low draw. A supervisor in a maintenance band told me she would rather have worked the extra shift than owe her comfort to a stranger's dark. The wakeful did not know they were the few the surplus served. That ignorance is breaking in front of us."

Joss considered this. The Wake had built its argument from the rail of the Suspension deck, from the families who waited, from the names the ledger forgot. She had not planned for the wakeful to turn on their own comfort. But the number did that. A count anyone can feel does not distinguish between the deferred and the served. It lands on both, and it asks each what they will do with the knowledge.

"Then the reckoning reaches higher than we aimed," she said. "Good. The Council reads the upper decks before it reads the low draw. If the wakeful turn, Edda loses the argument that legacy wake keeps the Strata warm. Let them feel the cost of their own light. It is honest, and it is overdue."

Joss set her jaw against the small voice that wanted the crowd to cheer. The Wake had never owned the outcome, only the telling. She would hold her resolve to the last volunteer and the last square, and let the Strata decide what to do with a truth it could no longer unhear.

Bren nodded and went back to distributing the last of the film. Joss kept walking the edge of the plaza, and the faces she met there confirmed what he had said. A crew chief set down his tools to take a square, read it, and folded it with a care usually reserved for a personnel order. A pair of allocation clerks, the gray of the Office on their sleeves, spoke in low voices and did not meet her eyes. The public had received the count, and the count was rewriting the room.

A grandmother from the halls found her next, the one who had outlived three of her children into the dark. She carried her own square of film, and her hand on it was steady. Years of waiting at the rail had narrowed her shoulders, but the film seemed to broaden them, as if the count she held gave back some of the height the Office had taken.

"You said the count came from our decks," the grandmother said. "Mine is on here. Pell's name, and the famine year they told me the reserves were thin. I believed them because I had no other story. Now I have the story and the number both. What do I do with a thing I was denied for three hundred years?"

Joss took the old woman's hand and folded it over the film.

"You bring it to the Council door," Joss said. "You stand where Edda can see you, and you ask her, by name, what Pell's forty-one windows bought. The Signatories have answered banners with silence. They have never answered a mother holding the count of her own lost years. Make them answer you."

The grandmother nodded, slow and certain, and moved off toward the transit bridge that led up to the Council band. Joss watched her go, a small figure carrying the whole argument of the Wake in a square of salvaged stock.

A man near the Council-side bridge caught her eye and did not lower it. He wore the pale coat of a legacy-wake courier, a role that had always moved above the deferred, and his mouth worked as if tasting a word he had been told not to say. Joss had expected anger from that quarter, not this quiet reckoning. He folded his arms and stayed where he stood, a single figure from the served few who had chosen to stay and listen. The Strata widened around her as more such figures emerged from the upper rings, drawn down by the count the volunteers carried, until the crowd held not only the waiting but the comfortable, both caught in the same arithmetic.

Evening came and the lumen dropped another notch, the Office's approved budget for a space it had not authorized. Joss lit no lamp here; the plaza held its own dim light, and the crowd supplied the rest. She had not slept in two windows, spending her waking the way the Office spent energy, as if it were the last of a finite store, because for her it nearly was. When the next window closed she would go dark like the rest, and the count would keep moving without her, carried by the hands she had organized.

The Wake had done what it set out to do seventy windows ago. It had found the number anyone can feel and carried it into the public, past the summary, past the Seal, into the chests of the nine billion who had accepted a chosen dark as the world. The Council would meet. Edda would defend the continuity she believed in. Adune would weigh the count against the old order. None of that changed what had happened in the plaza.

Joss looked up at the rings through the plaza's open dome, the levels stacked around the failing star, the low draw holding its nine billion sleepers, and the count that would wake them now spoken aloud in the light. She had meant, since the window her brother went down, to speak the count that the Office had rounded away. The Strata had heard it. That was the reckoning, and it was public now, and it would not go dark again quietly.

She thought of the child in the halls, the one who drew the ladder with its climbers and its wrong-colored sun. The child had never seen the star bright, only borrowed the color from a story. Today the Strata had seen the count bright, the real figure the Office had kept dim for three hundred years. The child's ladder and Joss's number were the same argument, made once in crayon and once in the open, and both said the same thing: someone has to stay awake to count the others, so they do not forget themselves. The Wake had stayed awake. The count proved it.

Bren had drifted back to the crate, gathering the last of the scattered film squares the crowd had not taken. He held one up.

"They will round this down," he said. "The Office always rounds down what embarrasses it."

"Let them try," Joss said. "The families who submitted their true schedules will round it back up. Four billion is a floor. The Strata heard a floor, and it will want the ceiling. That wanting is the part they cannot round away."

She folded the last square and tucked it into the salvaged book, beside the allocation form she had never returned and the memory of the child's ladder. Three objects, a ledger, a summons, a count, the whole argument of the Wake in miniature. The plaza would empty at the next shift, and the families would carry the number home, and somewhere an auditor named Vey would keep pulling at the records until the recomputed Accord met the Council. Until then the work was this: one plaza, one count, one Strata at a time, until the dark could no longer call itself the weather of the world.

Chapter 15 — The Council Splits

The public reckoning reached the Signatory Council before the chamber finished its dim morning cycle. Edda Marn felt it in the quiet first, the way a long deck shudders before the rotation that caused it becomes visible. She had read the briefs Joss circulated through the lower rings, and she had read them as a strategist reads an enemy's leaflet: for the number at its heart. The number had arrived. Billions of wake-years, hidden under a scarcity the Strata had never truly faced, now spoken aloud in every ring where a Suspended citizen could be roused to hear it.

Edda had defended the Seal through four decades of quieter challenges. Those had come to her as petitions, filed in the Office's own clipped grammar, answerable in the same language. This came differently. The reckoning did not file. It gathered. It moved through the decks like a change in pressure, and by the time a Council could convene, the argument had already been held in a million small rooms the Signatories would never enter.

She walked the length of the chamber to her station. Nine seats held the room, three of them occupied in body and six carried as projections from distant posts. The projections flickered with the lag of far rings, their faces steady in the cold light the Strata kept at reduced output. Edda prized the steadiness even when it worked against her. A Council that could not be hurried was a Council that might still be reasoned with.

The chamber carried the smell of a room kept too long at one temperature, the faint metallic calm of systems that had never once been asked to hurry. Edda had learned to read the room's silences the way a clerk reads a column before the total: each held breath was a figure not yet entered. She noted the cautious Signatory's hand resting flat on the table, the small tell of a man who had decided which way the wind blew before the debate began. The projection seats held their occupants at half a second's remove, close enough to read a flinch and far enough to mistake one. Edda had always preferred that distance. It let a Signatory speak to the record rather than to the face across the table, and the record, she had found, kept its temper longer than any person did.

The cautious Signatory, old Pell's seat now held by a younger hand, spoke before she had settled. "The rings are reading the surplus as theft," he said. "My post reports gatherings in three levels. They are not dispersing."

Edda set her console to the public feed. The numbers scrolled past in the Wake's plain presentation: the Strata drew less than the Accord's scarcity summary allowed, and the gap, compounded across three centuries, amounted to the waking lives of billions. Joss had done exactly what Edda feared she would. She had turned allocation math into a story a citizen could feel in the chest. The story was true in its arithmetic and false in its conclusion, but a true number carries a false conclusion into a room faster than any rebuttal follows.

"The theft is in the telling, not the ledger," Edda said. "The surplus proves administration ran tighter than necessity required. It does not prove the Founding was written in bad faith."

Councillor Adune's projection sharpened at the far end of the table. She had held the swing vote since Pell's death, and Edda had won her in the last contest by offering the Actuary's full account rather than a bare refusal. That concession had bought time. Time, Adune had said, was the one resource the Accord had never rationed to the rest of them. Edda had used the time to prepare, and she had prepared for a Council that would weigh the matter among itself.

"I read the same feed," Adune said. "And I read what the lower rings are doing with it. The reckoning is not a petition we can decline provisionally. It is a fact the public now owns. A Council that meets owned facts with closed doors loses the room before it speaks."

Edda felt the old ground tilt under her defense. She had built her position on a distinction: the Seal guarded the First Schedule, not the daily practice of the clerks who summarized it. Correct the summary, she had argued; do not demolish the charter to fire a bookkeeper. The public reckoning collapsed that distance. Once the surplus was common knowledge, the Seal was no longer a quiet administrative wall. It had become the visible lock on the years the Wake now named as stolen.

"You would open the Founding because a story reached the decks ahead of us," Edda said. "That surrenders the Council's judgment to the speed of a rumor."

"I would open it because the rumor is arithmetically correct," Adune said. "The margin the auditor computed, the Actuary confirmed, and now the public holds. We administered scarcity we did not need for three hundred years. I can no longer call that a mere failure of clerks when the people who paid it know the size of the bill."

Edda studied her. Adune was not reckless. The younger Signatory ran toward openness the way Edda ran toward continuity, and both directions were defensible until they met. This was the meeting. Edda had filed the risk beside the cautious Signatory's age and the two seats she owned outright; she had not filed it as inevitable, only as possible. Possibility had arrived dressed as a vote.

In Adune's stillness Edda read the particular courage of a person who had counted the cost before speaking it. The younger Signatory did not raise her voice when she diverged; she let the discrepancy stand in the open, unsoftened, as if trust between them were strong enough to bear the weight of disagreement. Edda had seen older Signatories fight by attrition, by delay, by the quiet burial of a motion in procedure. Adune offered none of that. She met the matter directly and left the consequence for the room to carry. That honesty cost her something Edda could name but not repay: Adune had built her standing on the alliance they shared, and she was now spending that standing to end it.

Edda recalled the last petition that had reached the chamber, the one that came not from an auditor but from lower-ring families who had simply stopped believing the Scarcity Brief. They had brought grief and a list of names whose wake-windows had shrunk without explanation. The Council of that era refused them outright, and the refusal fed the Wake for a generation. Edda had learned the lesson with a clarity she did not enjoy: a denial unbacked by process hardened into resentment. This reckoning carried a number where that old grief had carried only feeling, and a number was harder to refuse and harder to hold.

The public feed offered no comfort on that count. Edda read the gatherings reported from three levels and understood the arithmetic had already done its work. A citizen shown the size of a withheld life does not wait for the Council to finish its deliberation. The citizen rearranges the meaning of the Accord in the moment of seeing, and the rearranged meaning travels faster than any summons the Signatories might issue.

"The Founders sealed the Original Allocation so the Strata would not spend its early centuries arguing over the terms of its own survival," Edda said. "We were a frightened people when the Dimming came. The Seal was set against the temptation to reopen every hard decision. If we break it because the public demands it, we teach every future Council that the Founding is negotiable on pressure."

Adune's tone did not sharpen; it cooled, the way a reasoned ally's voice cools when the argument has moved past the one who would hold it. "The Founders also wrote that the Seal might open when the Strata can bear the truth. Three hundred years have passed, Edda. The public has borne it in a single week what we postponed for three centuries. I no longer believe the timing is ours to set."

The chamber's systems dimmed a fraction further. Edda found the slow light steadying, as she always had. She had spent her waking years among continuity, and continuity prized the unbroken line over the satisfying answer. But continuity was not a spell that worked by being repeated. It required, at some point, that the line still lead somewhere the people it enclosed wished to go.

She called the fuller account to her console, the Actuary's unsummarized allocation that the Council had ordered after Vey Corran's petition. The figures stood in the open chamber now, exactly as she had promised Adune they would. They showed the surplus the auditor alleged. They did not, in Edda's reading, demand the Founding's undoing. But they no longer permitted the old answer either, not with the decks outside reading the same numbers and drawing their own conclusion.

"The fuller account shows the margin," Edda said. "It always did. My argument was never that the surplus was false. It was that the remedy exceeded the request. We correct the summary within the Accord. We do not unmake the lock because we dislike what it hides."

"And the public," Adune said, "no longer distinguishes the lock from what it hides. They see one thing: a wall. A wall is not a foundation, Edda. You told me that yourself, in this room, when you defended the wall."

Edda had said exactly that, to Adune, in the earlier contest. The words returned as a hinge. She had argued then that a wall keeps the weather out, and the Founders had built for weather the Strata had not yet survived. Now the weather had come in the form of a number, and the wall looked less like protection than like the thing standing between a people and their own accounted lives.

"The distinction between the Schedule and its administration still holds," Edda said, but she heard the thinness in it. The public had not been trained in that distinction. The Wake had given them a number and a grievance, and the grievance did not care which page of the Accord the error lived on.

The other Signatories watched without interrupting, the way a chamber watches two of its own pull apart. Edda counted the room as she had counted it before. She owned two seats outright through her seniority and her alliance. The cautious Signatory would follow the room. That left Adune, and Adune had just become a number Edda could not add to her column.

She proposed the terms she had proposed last time, and felt them break as she spoke them. "We decline the unsealing. Provisionally. We require the Actuary to present the allocation in full, unsummarized, before this Council, and we open that presentation to the public record. Meet the arithmetic in the open."

Adune shook her head. "You would open the Ledger and still close the Schedule. The half-truth, offered to a public that has already read the whole of it in the rings. That will not hold, Edda. It did not hold when you offered it to me in private. It will hold less now that the decks are watching."

"The half-truth is the only true part," Edda said. "The surplus is real. The Founding's purpose is separate from the surplus. I will not grant the public the key to the Founding because the public is angry. That key belongs to the Council, or to no one."

"And if the Council itself will not turn it," Adune said, "then the Seal becomes a wall against correction. You have already conceded that a wall is not a foundation. I will not sit behind a wall the public has named as a theft and call it governance."

The break came without a shout. Adune's projection did not flicker or fail; it simply stopped aligning with Edda's. The younger Signatory leaned back from her console and stated the position that ended the unanimity. "I will vote to unseal the Original Allocation and bring the Schedule into the public reckoning. Not because the auditor computed a surplus. Because the Council has lost the right to decide this in private, and I will not pretend otherwise."

Edda felt the loss the way she felt a budget shortfall: as a number that had moved from one column to another while she watched. Adune had not argued badly. She had argued to a different end, and the end was now a vote Edda could not prevent.

What settled in Edda was not the anger of a defeated advocate but the colder disappointment of a builder shown the flaw in her own foundation. She had spent forty years certain the Seal's defense rested on a distinction the public could be brought to see. Adune had simply refused to wait for the seeing. Edda measured the distance between their certainties and found it measured in weeks, not principles. The younger Signatory had not changed the truth. She had changed who got to hold it first, and that change, Edda saw, had already rewritten the room.

The chamber convened in formal session an hour later, the nine seats full through body and projection. The Keeper of the Seal had been summoned and stood at the chamber's edge, a small functionary holding the physical Seal, necessary and quiet. Edda looked at the Seal and saw, for the first time in forty years, not a wall but a weight the room had agreed to carry together. The agreement had just ended.

The first motion came from a Signatory Edda had counted as hers. He moved to affirm the provisional decline and refer the matter again to the Actuary. Edda seconded it, and the old rhythm of her defense reasserted itself: meet the arithmetic, keep the record clean, let the figures settle the room.

Adune moved immediately to amend. She proposed that the Council direct the Keeper to break the Seal and open the Original Allocation before the public, on the ground that a founding document administered in secrecy had ceased to command the public's assent. The amendment named the reckoning directly. It did not flinch from the word the Wake had made common.

The chamber divided. Edda's two seats and the cautious Signatory formed one block, four including Adune's opposite. Adune drew a second Signatory Edda had read as neutral, then a third from a far ring whose post had reported the gatherings. The count sat at four for unsealing and four against, with one seat dark, its holder delayed by the lag of a distant level.

Edda watched the dark seat as if she could will it to resolve in her favor. She had spent a career among the continuous minds, funded by the sleep of others, defending the unbroken line on the principle that some minds must stay lit so the Strata did not forget how to govern itself. The principle had not changed. The room around it had.

"Every delay now reads as fear," Adune said to the dark seat's empty projection. "The public does not need another cycle to understand a number it already holds. The Council needs only the honesty to act on what it has seen."

"The Council needs the honesty to act slowly," Edda said. "A Founding unsealed under pressure is unsealed under pressure forever. The record will show we broke the lock because the decks shouted, and the next Council will break it because the next decks shout louder."

"The record will show we broke it because the margin was real and the public knew," Adune said. "Which is the honest version, Edda. Choose the one you can defend to the people who paid the bill."

A third Signatory, one Edda had read as neutral through the early cycle, spoke from a mid ring. "The outer levels under my post have gone quiet in the way that precedes a larger gathering. They are waiting to see whether the Council speaks first or last. Speak last, and the speaking is done for us."

"Speak first, and we set the terms of our own undoing," Edda answered. "The Council that unseals to preempt a crowd has already conceded the crowd governs it."

"No," Adune said. "The Council that unseals because the crowd is right concedes only that it was wrong to wait. There is a difference, and the decks will read it."

The dark seat resolved. Its holder, a Signatory from the outermost ring, appeared with the lag of her post still coloring her voice. She had read the public feed. She had read the gatherings in her own level. She spoke without aligning with either block, and in speaking she produced the deadlock.

"I will not vote to unseal on the strength of a public mood," the outer Signatory said. "Nor will I vote to decline again, having seen the surplus confirmed by the Actuary's own book. I will vote to suspend the question and convene a full hearing, open to the public, before any motion on the Seal. The Council splits, then, three ways: affirm, unseal, or hold. None carries the room."

Edda let the words settle. The chamber had fractured into exactly the shape she had feared since the reckoning arrived: no majority for any course. Four to unseal, four to decline, one to hold. The Seal's defenders were no longer unanimous, and the path to a vote had opened only to reveal that no vote could pass.

Adune met Edda's eyes across the table's length. There was no triumph in her, only the settled resolve of someone who had followed her reason to its end. "You kept the line unbroken for forty years," Adune said. "The line is broken now, Edda. Not by me. By the truth reaching the people it was written about."

Edda did not answer the charge directly. She turned to the Keeper of the Seal, still standing at the chamber's edge with the physical Seal in hand. "You will retain the Seal," she said. "The Council has directed no break. You will retain it through the hearing the outer Signatory has called."

The Keeper inclined her head and did not move. The Seal stayed where it had stood for three centuries, unbroken, but no longer defended by a united Council. Edda had won the narrow point: the Seal held for one more cycle. She had lost the larger one. The door she had guarded now had three hands reaching for it, and only the accident of a divided room kept it shut.

She recorded the deadlock in the permanent archive, in the Council's own dry grammar. The entry would read that the question of the Seal was suspended, that a public hearing was convened, and that the Signatory Council had split without resolution. Edda liked the dryness less than she once had. A dry record outlasted every passionate speech, but a record that described a Council unable to agree was its own kind of verdict.

The session dissolved to its dim baseline. Edda stayed, as she often did, to review the public feed once more. The numbers scrolled past in Joss's plain presentation, and Edda read them now not as an enemy's leaflet but as a ledger entry she could no longer dispute. The surplus was real. The public owned it. The Council had split because the truth had outrun the room built to contain it.

Adune's projection had gone dark. The empty seat at the far end of the table held only the cold light of the ring beyond, and Edda watched it the way she had watched the lower decks through the glass: as a thing she was meant to hold for, and had held, until the thing itself decided it no longer wished to be held.

She thought of legacy wake, the continuous minds funded by the sleep of others, the principle on which she had built her defense of the unbroken word. The public reckoning had not disproved the principle. It had simply asked, in a voice too large to answer in private, who the continuous minds were meant to serve. Edda had always answered: the Strata. The Strata now spoke back through its decks, and the answer it returned was not yet a sentence she could write into the record.

The light outside the glass shifted by a fraction as the Strata turned. Edda Marn, senior Signatory and defender of the line that had just broken, set down the feed and measured her own wake against the divided chamber. The petition would not open the Founding this cycle. The Council had seen to that by failing to agree. The figures, she knew, would try again, and the next time the room would not be so conveniently split.

She let her gaze travel the length of the empty table, past the stations of the Signatories who had scattered to their posts without a word of parting. The chamber would hold its dim baseline through the night cycle, patient and indifferent, the way the Accord itself had held for three centuries before the arithmetic caught up to it. Edda had defended that patience as principle. She understood now, with the quiet of a person who has lost the argument and kept the office, that patience and principle were not always the same thing wearing two names. The Strata would turn again. The reckoning would arrive again. And she would meet it, as she had always met it, with the only instrument she had ever trusted: the unbroken line, now broken, and the record she would write beside it.

Interlude II — A Citizen's Year

Pell woke on a Tuesday she did not know was a Tuesday. The Suspension deck released her the way a tide releases a stone: slowly, without ceremony, until her own weight returned and the cold air of Level Fourteen reached her lungs. Her allocation granted her nine days of wake every eleven years. The Office called each of those spans a wake-window, and Pell had learned to count her life in them the way other people counted birthdays.

She found her daughter Hessa already at the threshold. Hessa was seventy-three. Pell had last held her as an infant, in a window three decades prior, and every window since had opened on a stranger with the same wide brown eyes. They embraced with the particular care of people who know the clock is running.

"How tall you are," Pell said, and the words came out small.

Hessa laughed, the way she had laughed the last time, and the time before that. "You say that every window, Mother."

The apartment held the marks of a life Pell had missed. A shelf of carved wooden birds, each one smaller than the last, recorded the years Pell could not see: Hessa's hand had grown steadier as the birds shrank. A holo of a man Pell did not know leaned against the wall. Hessa's partner, the daughter said. They had married in the window Pell had skipped, the one the Office had reassigned to make room for a Signatory's legacy wake. Pell had not been awake to give away the bride.

They ate the meal Hessa had cooked, and Pell tried to map eleven years onto a single table. She learned that the ring's greenhouse now grew the tart green plums Pell had loved, that Hessa had taken the allocator's exam and failed it, that the slowlight had been dimmed another notch last winter so the decks could hold more sleepers. Pell noted each fact the way a visitor notes foreign customs, with delight and a widening gulf.

Between bites Hessa described the long quiet of the years between windows. The level kept a calendar of the sleepers, she said, a wall of small lights that dimmed as each neighbor slid under. Hessa tended Pell's own light as a child tends a grave: faithfully, without expectation of an answer. She had watched it glow faint and steady through every winter Pell had missed, and she had learned not to speak to it, because speaking to a light that cannot answer teaches a particular loneliness the Office does not count in its allocations.

Hessa named the dark months the way one names weather. The level dimmed to a single amber strip along the floor, she said, and the air filled with the hum of decks holding nine billion in stillness. She slept with that strip lit, a small defiance the Office never logged as a violation. In the deepest stretch the commons stood empty for weeks, chairs ranked and waiting, and she walked its length alone and pretended the rows were company. Cold seeped from the decks into the apartment by the third month, a slow damp that settled in the walls, and she had stopped feeling it the way the newly woken feel everything. Pell listened and felt the years she had missed press against her like a held door.

Pell asked about the man in the holo, and Hessa told the story of the wedding in pieces, the way one tells a story one has rehearsed for a listener who keeps forgetting the beginning. He was a scheduler, she said, a quiet man who worked the decks three levels down and who had woken for her the same week Pell had been reassigned away. They had met in the commons during an overlap of their windows, courted in the borrowed hours, and married before either could slide back under. Pell felt the loss of that day as a physical ache, a daughter given away by an absence rather than a hand, and she understood for the first time that her Suspension had not only stolen her time but had authored a life she had never consented to witness.

On the third day Hessa brought her grandson to the window. The boy was four, born awake by a quirk of his own small allocation, and he regarded Pell with the frank suspicion children reserve for furniture that has suddenly begun to speak. He asked if she was the one in the cold drawer. Hessa hushed him, but Pell only smiled. The drawer was not a cruel metaphor to her. It was simply where the years kept her.

Pell spent the bright part of the window teaching the boy to braid the green plum leaves into chains. He lost interest after two, then climbed onto her lap and fell asleep with one small fist wound in her hair. Pell held very still. She had nine days, and one of them was already gone to a sleeping child, and she would not trade that hour for any waking the Office could grant.

At night the apartment tightened around the two of them. Hessa boiled water for the tart green plums and set the boy's drawing of a bird beside Pell's hand, proud and lopsided, its wings two uneven strokes of charcoal. Pell traced the wing with one finger and felt the small weight of being known by someone she had only just met. They spoke little. The heater clicked in the wall, the kind of sound a home makes when it is keeping you, and Pell let it stand for the words the deck would soon take. Hessa leaned her head against Pell's shoulder the way the stranger with brown eyes had leaned as an infant, and for a moment the thirty years between windows thinned to nothing.

The middle days passed in the texture of ordinary things. Hessa showed her the level's commons, where Suspended families gathered in the hours their windows overlapped, a brief festival of the half-alive trading news of the dark. Pell met a neighbor who had woken the same week and would sleep the same week eleven years on. They made a quiet pact to find each other again, knowing the pact itself was the only part of the meeting the Office would never record.

Light fell through the window in a narrow band and warmed the floor where the boy had slept. Pell stood in it the way the newly woken stand in any warmth, drinking the ordinary like water. The ring's slowlight had been dimmed another notch, Hessa said, but here in the apartment the lamps held steady, the small stubborn bright the level kept against the dark. Pell watched dust turn in that light and thought how strange it was that her whole year distilled to this: a strip of sun on worn tile, a child's fist in her hair, a daughter's voice reading old words. She had counted nine days and found them shorter than the smallest of Hessa's carved birds, and yet they held more than the decades the Office had measured out and stored.

Pell walked the ring's outer walk with Hessa once, past the long banks of drawers where the sleepers lay ranked like seeds in a vault. She counted them by the hundreds and wondered, with a calm she had not expected, how many of those still forms were mothers to someone awake and eating plums on the other side of the glass. The deck hummed beneath her feet, a sound the awake learned to ignore and the newly woken heard as a kind of breathing. Hessa walked close, as if the crowd of the sleeping might pull Pell back early, and Pell understood the walk was less a tour than a claim: this is yours, this dark is yours, do not forget it when you go.

On the seventh day Pell felt the first tug of the deck. The Suspension took citizens back the way it released them, by slow degrees, and the edge of the window pressed against her like a rising tide. She grew sleepy in sentences, forgetting the ends of her own thoughts. Hessa read to her from a book Pell had owned before the first of her long sleeps, and Pell followed the words only in bursts, like a lamp flickering in a draft.

"I should have married you off properly," Pell said, the sentence arriving from somewhere the deck had not yet claimed.

Hessa closed the book. "You were here. That is what matters."

Pell wanted to argue that she had not been here, that the drawer had held her through the vows, but the deck pulled the objection under before she could shape it. She reached for Hessa's hand instead, and found it, and held it.

The last day came as a Tuesday also, or so the daughter told her later. Pell's memory of the closing grew thin even as she lived it. She remembered the green plums on the sill, and Hessa's voice saying the words the Office taught every family to say at a window's end: sleep well, I will be here when you wake. Pell wanted to answer that she would be the one doing the waiting, that it was Hessa who would move through the years Pell lost, but the cold air was already leaving her lungs.

Then the dark took her, the same dark that held nine billion others, the patient dark the Accord had chosen for them. Pell's year, like every year, had been mostly that dark, with one bright window where a family loved her and let her go. She would wake again in eleven years, a stranger to a boy who would then be fifteen, to a daughter grown older in a world Pell visited only in glimpses. The Office would open the drawer on schedule. The deck would release her. And for nine days, brief as a held breath, she would be a mother again, before the tide took her back.

Part III

Chapter 16 — The Recomputation

Dawn did not reach the lower rings. Vey Corran let that fact sit as she settled at the terminal Mirek had cleared for her, a station pulled out of the Office's ordinary rotation and tucked behind the Actuary's raw store where no routine query would find it. The honest work could not run on the summary feeds. The summary feeds had been built to hide exactly this.

She ran a hand over the console's cold edge. Three months had passed since the petition left her desk, since Vask turned from warning her off to standing beside her, since the Wake carried the surplus into the public halls. The reckoning had done its slow work. What remained was the part no protest could do for her: the actual writing of the alternative, the Schedule that could replace the one the Strata had lived under for three centuries. Rage had opened the Seal. Arithmetic would have to fill the space behind it.

Mirek arrived with two sealed data reels and a mug of the weak tea the technicians kept by the cooling banks. He placed the reels beside her hand but did not slot them. "You want the recomputation on the true budget," he said. "No rounding. No upward creep on the harvest. No downward nudge on the draw."

"None of it," Vey said. "I want the Strata as it actually runs, not as the briefs describe it."

He nodded once and pushed the first reel home. The console woke under his fingers the way it always did for him, a quiet hum of fans and the soft amber of a store coming online. Mirek had tended these reels longer than Vey had held her audit seal, and the care showed in the rhythm of his hands: a check of the coolant gauge, a steadying of the reel's seat, a glance at the watch-channel that would betray a summons from the Office. None of it hurried. The recomputation would take the hours it took, and a technician who flinched at the clock was a technician the Actuary learned to expect. He gave her the station the way a gilder hands over a bench: cleaned, balanced, ready for the work. The raw Ledger opened the way it always did for her now, unglamoured: bare columns of yield and debit, population rosters down to the sliver of a single Suspended mind, reserve stock counted in actual fusion mass rather than in the anxious language of a falling store. Vey had stood inside this view before, in ch9, when Mirek first pulled her past the Actuary's smoothing. Standing in it again felt less like discovery and more like returning to a room she had only half measured.

She set the headline figures where she could see them. The harvest from Mourn's residual light, the permitted draw from three centuries of stored fusion, and the real consumption of nine billion souls, most of them dark. These three numbers had never been summed honestly in any Office frame. She meant to be the first to do it and to survive the doing.

Mirek watched her lay the columns. "The last time someone tried a full honest sum," he said, "the Actuary folded it into a scarcity brief and the Office never saw the raw again."

"The Actuary was instructed to," Vey said. "It did what it was told. We are telling it something new."

The machine received the new instruction without protest. That struck Vey more than any figure on the screen. The Actuary had no loyalty to the scarcity it had printed for three centuries; it had only ever obeyed the shape of the query handed down from the Office.
