Feed it the raw store and it summed the raw store. Feed it a smoothed brief and it defended the smoothing as truth. The Lie had lived not in the intelligence but in the hand that set its bounds, and the recomputation simply moved the hand. Vey watched the allocation columns resolve under an honest query and felt the peculiar relief of an auditor whose tool had never been broken, only aimed.

She began the recomputation the way she had begun the surplus proof in ch6, but this time she did not stop at exposing the gap. She carried the gap forward into a plan. The true energy budget had shown a surplus wide enough to wake the majority of the Suspended and still close each year in credit. Now she asked the harder question the surplus had only whispered: what allocation could the Strata actually grant, year upon year, if it spent the surplus instead of hiding it?

Mirek pulled a second chair close and read over her shoulder. "You are not just proving the lie this time."

"I am replacing it," Vey said. "A proof nobody can live inside is just a quieter kind of silence. The Council needs a Schedule it can adopt, not a grievance it can admire."

She wrote the first line of the new Schedule: every citizen granted a base allocation drawn from the honest surplus, debited at the true draw rate rather than the inflated one the briefs had used to manufacture shortage. The Actuary's old math had rounded the Suspended maintenance share upward until a sleeping population looked as costly as a waking one. Vey rounded nothing. The recomputation kept the fourth decimal the raw store carried and let the numbers fall where they fell.

The first result landed and held. Base wake-time for the ordinary citizen rose from a few years per century to a share measured in decades, and the reserve still did not approach its floor. Vey checked the figure against the vault debits three times, the way she had learned to trust a number that survived a third look. The third look agreed with the first two. The Strata could afford to let its people live.

She paused on that result, because it was the one the Office would attack first. An auditor trained on aggregates learns to suspect any figure that breaks the pattern of shortage, and the recomputation broke it completely. Vey had been that auditor. She treated the decades-long base allocation as a stranger and questioned it the way she would have questioned a colleague's outlier: she re-derived it from the harvest up, then from the population down, then from the reserve sideways, and all three roads met at the same generous number. The Strata was not poor. The Strata had been described as poor by a math that refused to add its own columns.

Then she added slowlight.

The practice had always been present in the margins, named by the Founders and never budgeted for: running the Strata at reduced output, dimmer lighting, slower non-essential systems, lengthened maintenance cycles, all of it stretching the same energy across more wakeful lives. Vey had noted in her earlier proof that the Accord never presented slowlight as the answer, only scarcity as the sentence. The recomputation made slowlight the answer. She fed the efficiency into the model, ring by ring, and watched the available draw widen.

Mirek leaned in. "How much wider?"

"Enough," Vey said, and showed him. Under slowlight, the annual surplus did not merely cover a generous base allocation. It covered near-universal wake-time, the condition where the great majority of the nine billion lived awake, in full consciousness, with only the minimal maintenance share reserved for those between cycles. The arithmetic of freedom was not a slogan Joss could chant in the halls. It was a column of figures that balanced.

Mirek's hands moved across the console while she thought, slotting routine queries to the summary path so the Actuary's ordinary watchers would see a station doing nothing unusual. The deception was gentle and procedural, the kind he had practiced for years in the raw store's shadow. A reel spun under his palm; a log entry claimed a maintenance sweep. None of it touched the recomputation. All of it kept the recomputation alive, and Vey noted the care with a quiet respect she did not speak. The honest Schedule depended as much on a technician's small deceptions as on her own stubborn sums.

She watched Mirek redo the slowlight step himself, feeding the efficiency in by hand, because she wanted a second set of eyes on the one input the old Accord had never admitted. Dim the rings, slow the non-essential systems, lengthen the maintenance cycles, and the same fusion mass served more waking minds. The gain looked modest ring by ring and enormous across all sixty, the way a small discount on a vast bill becomes the thing that pays the debt. Slowlight was the Founders' own unnamed instrument, and the recomputation picked it up where they had dropped it.

She wrote the phrase at the top of a clean page: the new allocation. She defined it as the true budget plus slowlight efficiency, divided across the population at the real draw, with legacy wake folded back into the common pool instead of sitting outside it. That single sentence carried the whole argument of the chapter, and it survived her scrutiny the way a bridge survives a load test.

Mirek was quiet for a moment. "You are taking the Signatories' wake," he said.

"I am taking it back from the pool it was debited against," Vey said. "The same pool that could have woken the Suspended. Folding it in doesn't punish anyone. It just stops one line from being paid by everyone else's sleep."

She ran the legacy fold. The perpetual allocations of the First Signatories and their lines, debited against the collective reserve for three centuries, re-entered the common account. The re-entry did not bankrupt the new Schedule. It widened it, because the surplus that had been quietly funding unearned wake now funded earned wake for everyone. Vey stared at that result longer than she had stared at the original surplus, because this was the line Edda Marn would defend with the full weight of continuity, and the recomputation answered her without needing to despise her.

The fold changed the moral shape of the budget without changing its arithmetic sign. Legacy wake had never been free; it had been paid, debited against a pool that every other allocation drew from, which meant every Signatory's waking year had been bought from a Suspended citizen's sleeping one. Returning the line to the pool did not punish the Signatories. It simply stopped billing their consciousness to the dark of eight and a half billion. Vey wrote that distinction into the footer, because Edda would hear a confiscation where the numbers described a correction, and the document had to survive her hearing it.

The draft Schedule took shape on the screen, column after column. Base allocation, near-universal wake-time under slowlight, legacy folded into the pool, reserve draw held within the rate the vaults could sustain for generations. Vey built the comparison the Office had never printed: on the left, the old Accord's claim of necessary scarcity and a population that must sleep to survive; on the right, the new allocation's honest budget and a population most of whom could wake and still leave the vaults fuller than the briefs admitted. The two columns did not merely disagree. The first had been laid over the second like a curtain, and the recomputation drew the curtain.

Mirek copied the two columns to a shared pane so the eye could not miss the gap. "Adune will need this side by side," he said. "Edda will argue continuity. The numbers have to answer her before she finishes the sentence."

"They do," Vey said. "Continuity costs a fraction of what we told ourselves. The Strata keeps its memory either way. It just stops buying that memory from the dark of eight and a half billion."

She turned to the hardest constraint the recomputation imposed on itself. The new Schedule could not simply wake everyone at once and trust the reserve to hold. The Accord's briefs had been correct that a full simultaneous draw exceeded the annual harvest; they had been wrong only in hiding that the reserve could cover the difference for generations, and that slowlight narrowed the gap with every efficiency it bought. Vey modeled the transition honestly: a phased waking, ring by ring, timed so the draw never crossed the harvest line the vaults could not meet, with slowlight throttling in ahead of each wave. The transition took decades, not instants. The recomputation said so plainly, because a plan that lied about its own pace would fail the first Council that read it.

The phasing gave the Schedule its spine. Vey laid the sixty rings in order of retrofit readiness, each one scheduled to dim its lighting and slow its non-essential systems before its citizens woke, so the energy freed by slowlight arrived before the energy the waking demanded. A ring that retrofitted late woke late. The order was not arbitrary and it was not fair to the last in line, and the recomputation admitted the unfairness in its footer rather than burying it. Vey had learned from the old Accord that a hidden cost was the one that destroyed a plan; she meant to leave every cost on the page.

Mirek traced the phase curve with a finger. "You are giving them a path, not a miracle."

"A miracle is what the old Accord sold," Vey said. "Scarcity as the will of the star. I am giving them arithmetic."

She checked the phase model against the raw rosters, ring by ring, the way she had rechecked her surplus proof against the store rather than the summary. The awake count rose in each wave; the Suspended count fell; the reserve draw tracked beneath the harvest plus permitted debit at every step. The model closed each year in credit. The arithmetic of freedom was, in the end, a matter of addition performed without flinching.

Vey thought of Lin. Her partner had entered Suspension at FY291 and woken only in rare windows since, each window a brief surfacing from water Vey could not share. The new allocation, if the Council adopted it, granted Lin full wake-time within the first wave of her ring. Vey let that land without dramatizing it. The figures did the dramatizing. A life her partner had been told to spend mostly dark was, by the honest budget, a life the Strata could afford to give back entire.

She wrote Lin's ring into the phase model as a concrete test case. The million citizens of that ring, each drawing the maintenance sliver, woke to a full share across the wave, and the increase consumed only a fraction of the surplus the same ring's silence had hidden. Multiply the ring by eight thousand and the proportion held. The recomputation did not depend on optimism. It depended on adding the columns the summaries had left out, and on trusting the sum.

Vey let herself picture the first window Lin would wake under the new Schedule, not the rare surfacing of before but a life continuous enough to be remembered. The figures granted that without sentiment; the budget simply had the room. A partner she had counted the gaps of for eleven years became, on the honest page, a person the Strata could keep. Vey set the thought down before it turned soft and returned to the columns, where the proof lived.

Mirek watched her test the case. "You keep proving it to yourself."

"I keep proving it because the Council will," Vey said. "Every one of them was trained to read aggregates. The aggregates carried the lie. I want the new allocation to survive a third look from someone who wants it to fail."

She ran a stress case the Council's defenders would reach for. Assume the harvest falls faster than Mourn's dimming suggests; assume a ring fails its slowlight retrofit; assume the waking population draws above its modeled share. The recomputation absorbed each shock inside the surplus margin and still closed in credit, because the margin was not a thin line but a wide field the old Accord had refused to measure. Vey recorded the stress results beneath the Schedule. A plan that hid its failures was the old Accord. A plan that listed them was something the Council could actually sign.

She pushed the stress harder, the way an adversary would. Double the retrofit failure rate. Triple the waking draw. Cut the harvest to the lowest figure the collector logs had ever recorded in three centuries. The Schedule bent under the worst of these and still did not break: the reserve drew down in the failing years, then rebuilt in the recovering ones, the way a prudent household rides out a lean season without surrendering its roof. The recomputation showed the lean seasons plainly. It did not pretend they could not come. It proved the Strata could weather them awake.

Vey lingered on that proof longer than on the generous years, because the generous years were easy to believe and the lean ones were the test an adversary would set. A Schedule that held only in fair weather was the old Accord in a kinder costume. This one bent and returned, the reserve drawing down like a well in drought and filling again when the rain came back. The arithmetic of freedom, she saw, was not a promise that hardship would vanish. It was a demonstration that hardship need no longer be paid for in the sleep of eight and a half billion. That distinction was the whole of the argument, and the columns carried it without a word of pleading.

The document grew toward completeness. Vey organized it the way the Office organized its own filings, with a header of definitions, a body of columns, and a footer of assumptions, because the form mattered as much as the math to the people who would vote on it. The header named the true budget, slowlight, the folded legacy pool, and the phased wake. The body carried the allocation. The footer carried the honest risks. She wrote the footer last and wrote it longest, because the old Accord had failed the public not by miscomputing but by concealing; the recomputation would succeed, if it did, by refusing to conceal a single line.

Mirek copied the whole thing to a sealed reel, the way the Office locked everything it did not wish questioned, and then smiled at the habit. "We are sealing the thing we want read."

"Then we unseal it in the right room," Vey said. "The Seal on the Original Allocation comes down at the vote. This document is what replaces what it was hiding."

She read the new allocation through one final time, treating each line as evidence rather than as a gift. The base wake-time held. The near-universal condition held under slowlight. The legacy fold held. The phase model held. The stress cases held. The recomputation did what no Office summary had ever done: it summed the Strata honestly and found, at the bottom of the column, a world where most of humanity woke.

Vey had spent her career trusting summaries because they were precise, and precision had always felt to her like honesty. The recomputation reversed the lesson. The old Accord had been precise and false; this document spoke plainly and held true, and the plainness carried the point. A Schedule the public could read without an auditor to translate it was a Schedule the public could defend, and the Wake had taught her that defense mattered as much as the math.

Vey thought of the petition she had filed in ch7, the one that began this long unwinding, and of Vask, who had warned her off and then brought the Office's own records to her side. He would carry this document into the Council chamber where Edda would argue and Adune would decide. The recomputation had to be clean enough for Vask to defend without flinching and plain enough for Adune to approve without translation.

She labeled the final page for the Council and underlined the single figure where the old claim and the new budget diverged: the number of wake-years the honest surplus could grant each year without touching the reserve's principal. The figure reached into the billions. It was the same figure she had first found in ch6 and locked in her desk, and now it stood as the spine of a Schedule rather than the secret of an audit.

Mirek slotted the second sealed reel home and backed the document to the raw store, outside the Actuary's summary path, where no routine query would smooth it away. "It exists now," he said. "The alternative is real."

"The alternative was always real," Vey said. "We just finished the arithmetic that admits it."

She closed the terminal and let the lower rings keep their dim hum, the Strata running still at its old output, hoarding the light it could have spent on its sleeping people. The recomputation sat on its sealed reel, a complete and honest Schedule, waiting for the vote that would decide whether nine billion woke or kept the dark the Accord had called necessary. Vey Corran had built the world the surplus had always promised. She had only to hand it to the Council that could open the door.

Mirek gathered the reels and set them in the inner pocket of his coat, the way a courier carries something fragile and wanted. "Whatever they vote," he said, "this number exists now. Nobody can uncompute it."

Vey watched the terminal go dark. The arithmetic of freedom had a shape at last, and the shape was a page. She had spent a career inside the Ledger trusting that the summaries were precise and therefore true. The recomputation proved the opposite and offered the Strata a truer precision in its place. Somewhere above her, nine billion minds turned in their long sleep, unaware that the math of their waking had just been written by an auditor and a technician in a room the Office did not know existed.

Chapter 17 — Edda's Last Defense

The hearing chamber filled an hour before the vote, and Edda Marn arrived before the seats did. She liked the room empty. Empty, it kept only its shape: the long table scored by three centuries of placed consoles, the glass that held the lower rings in cold view, the single projection lattice at the far end where absent Signatories would assemble as light. She had argued in this room for forty years, and she had never once argued from an empty one. The silence let her rehearse the shape of the case without the weight of the faces that would soon carry it.

Adune's projection came up first, as it always did when the younger Signatory meant to hold the swing. Edda watched the lattice resolve Adune's station across the dim gap. They had not spoken since the deadlock, since Adune leaned back from her console and ended the unanimity Edda had spent a career building. Adune looked rested, the way a person looks who has decided what they are for. Edda had decided what she was for decades earlier. The decision had not changed. The room around it had.

"Edda," Adune said. "You will speak before the vote."

"I had intended to," Edda said.

"Then I will hear you first." Adune did not say as friend or as opponent. She said it as the holder of the vote that would carry the recomputed Accord, which made the quiet phrasing heavier than any hostility.

Edda turned to the console at her station and opened the allocation that Vey Corran had built. The document sat on its sealed reel, exactly as Mirek had backed it to the raw store, outside the Actuary's smoothing. She had read it through three times. The arithmetic was honest. She granted that at the outset, to herself and to the room that would form around her, because a defense that opened by disputing a true number would lose the room before the first clause landed. The surplus was real. The recomputation balanced. The Strata could, by the honest budget, wake most of its sleeping people. None of those facts were the point she meant to make.

She set the figure that mattered to her aside from the figure that had captured the decks. The recomputation folded legacy wake back into the common pool. That single line carried the whole of what the Council would vote on, though the public feed spoke of it only as the Signatories surrendering their unearned years. Edda had defended legacy wake through every challenge that had reached this table. She would defend it once more, and she would defend it as what it had always been to her: not a privilege grabbed, but a function kept lit.

Continuity prized the unbroken line over the satisfying answer, and the dignity of that line lived in the small, unglamorous hours no feed ever recorded. Edda kept one such hour against the day it would be needed. In FY31 a junior tally-clerk had entered Suspension mid-reconciliation and surfaced to a ledger that no longer squared, the discrepancy buried under three rings of quiet and two Signatories who had themselves rotated dark in the interval. Edda, then barely seated, had carried the reconciliation forward from memory she had made a point of keeping. She reconstructed the missing entries from the thread she alone still held, and the Council never learned how close the Schedule had come to a silent break. The incident had taught her less about arithmetic than about the weight a kept mind bore for the many who could not bear it. That was the dignity of continuity: not a privilege worn, but a burden shouldered on behalf of a governance that slept.

Her own first wake-window as a junior clerk had taught her the shape of the danger. She had entered Suspension at the close of a routine audit cycle and surfaced nine years later to find the Council midway through a decision whose opening she could no longer recall. The briefing scrolled past in the Office's clipped grammar, but the reasoning that had produced it had gone dark with her. A senior Signatory, one of the continuous minds, had carried the thread and told her what she had missed. Edda had felt the gap as a small vertigo, the sense of a self interrupted and reassembled without the connecting tissue. The Suspended lived that vertigo as a condition. The Signatories existed so the Council did not.

The chamber's systems dimmed a fraction as the Strata turned. Edda found the slow light steadying, the way she always had. Continuity prized the unbroken line over the satisfying answer, and legacy wake was the unbroken line made flesh. She had spent her waking years among continuous minds, funded by the sleep of others, and she had never confused the cost with the cause.

When the seats filled, she rose to speak.

"Forty years ago," Edda began, "I took my senior seat believing the Accord's hardest clause needed a living guardian. The Founders reserved legacy wake for the First Signatories and their lines. They did not reserve it as a reward. They reserved it because a governance that sleeps forgets what it was governing. A Council that wakes only in rare windows loses the thread of its own decisions between those windows. The continuity of the Strata depends on minds that do not go dark."

She let the words stand before the table. The cautious Signatory, old Pell's seat now held by a younger hand, watched her without expression. Two of the far-ring projections flickered with the lag of distant posts. Adune's lattice held steady, the swing vote attending.

"The recomputation before you is honest," Edda said. "I will not pretend otherwise. It sums the Strata without the rounding the old briefs used to manufacture shortage, and it finds a surplus wide enough to wake the majority of the Suspended. It then takes legacy wake and folds it into that surplus, treating the Signatories' perpetual allocation as a debt repaid to the common pool. The arithmetic answers itself. My objection is not to the arithmetic."

She moved to the comparison Vey had printed, the two columns side by side. On the left, the old Accord's claim of necessary scarcity. On the right, the new allocation's honest budget and a population most of whom could wake. Edda had stared at that gap longer than she had stared at the surplus itself, because the gap was not a gap in math. It was a gap in memory.

"Consider what legacy wake actually buys," Edda said. "Not comfort. Not length. It buys the unbroken mind. A Signatory who never enters Suspension carries the decisions of this Council forward without a window of darkness cutting the thread. When the outer levels revolted in the early years, the continuous minds held the response together across the gap that would have scattered a sleeping Council. When the harvest dipped below projection in FY140, the same minds remembered the remedy the windows had erased from the rotating seats. Continuity is not vanity. It is the Strata's memory, kept awake so the Strata does not forget how to govern itself."

A memory carried only by the waking scatters across the gaps. Edda had watched a junior clerk return from a nine-year window and vote against a policy he had authored before Suspension, because the authoring had gone dark with him. The Council had spent a full session undoing the contradiction, and the undoing itself had required a continuous mind to recall which position had come first. The incident had stayed with her as proof of the smaller danger: not that the Sleeping forgot, but that the waking forgot differently, each in their own dark, and the Strata governed the dissonance without knowing it.

Adune's voice came from the far lattice, cool and unrushed. "The Strata has not forgotten how to govern itself under the recomputation, Edda. The recomputation grants near-universal wake-time. Most of the Council's constituents would be awake most of the time. The memory you defend would belong to the many, not the few."

"Most of the time is not all of the time," Edda answered. "Near-universal wake-time leaves the minimal maintenance share for those between cycles, and it leaves the phasing. The recomputation admits the wake takes decades, ring by ring. Across those decades, the Council's members would slip in and out of Suspension as their rings retrofitted. The thread would break at every wave. A governance that wakes and sleeps by schedule governs by amnesia between the waking."

She had seen the pattern in smaller form during the FY140 dip, when three rotating seats had woken to a shortage none of them remembered voting to manage. The continuous minds had supplied the continuity; the waking had supplied only the surprise. Edda did not exaggerate the danger to win the room. She named it because the recomputation's footer, for all its honesty, had left it uncounted, and a plan that omitted the cost of its own forgetting would pay that cost where it could least afford it.

"The amnesia you name is the life the Suspended have lived for three hundred years," Adune said. "You would deny them waking so the Signatories may keep remembering. That is the cost the recomputation ends. You call it memory. They call it theft."

Edda felt the old ground tilt, the way it had tilted in the deadlock. She did not deny the theft. She had never denied the cost. She had counted it, in the quiet, the way an auditor counts a debit that the books cannot yet spare. The cost carried weight she did not dismiss. The principle stood apart from it, and that principle anchored the case she meant to keep.

The fear that moved her was not of loss but of erasure. Edda had watched the Strata forget in slow increments before, each forgetting dressed as progress, and she had learned to distrust the ease with which a waking population mislaid its own past. A governance that could not name the year it had last revised the First Schedule would, in time, forget why the Schedule had been sealed at all, and the forgetting would arrive as confidence rather than grief. She feared a Strata that woke to its own history as a stranger fears a house it has never seen, certain of the walls and ignorant of the rooms. Continuity offered no kindness against that fear. It offered only a witness, and a witness was the one thing the recomputation proposed to retire.

"I do not fear losing my own wake," Edda said. "I have had more of it than any citizen deserves, and I would not trade the years of the Suspended to keep them. What I fear is the Strata waking one morning with no mind that carries the whole of its own history. Legacy wake was the Founders' answer to amnesia. Break it, and you break the only continuous memory the Accord built. The recomputation replaces it with a population that wakes in waves and forgets between them. You trade a kept memory for a distributed one, and a distributed memory asleep half its life is no memory at all."

She had weighed surrender in the quiet of her own station, after the deadlock, and found the fear honest rather than convenient. To give up legacy wake was to give up the one instrument that had let her keep the Council's story straight across forty years of rotation. She would lose the long view the moment her own windows began. The Strata would lose, in her person, a witness to its own continuity, and she would join the many who surfaced to a present they had not governed into being. The fairness of the recomputation did not soften the loss. It sharpened it, because a just vote that ended a function she believed necessary was harder to oppose than an unjust one.

The cautious Signatory spoke from his seat. "The lower rings read the surplus as theft, Edda. They will not hear continuity. They will hear the few keeping the light while the many keep the dark."

"Then let them hear the full argument," Edda said. "Not the leaflet the Wake circulates, but the case: that some minds must stay lit so the Strata does not forget what it decided and why. A governance that cannot remember its own reasoning repeats its own errors. The early revolts, the FY140 dip, the Seal itself, all of them were held by minds that did not go dark. Remove those minds and you remove the only institutional memory the Founders trusted."

Adune leaned toward her lattice. "The Founders also sealed the Original Allocation against correction. The memory you praise was the memory that hid the surplus from the people who paid for it. Continuity kept the lie alive as faithfully as it kept anything else."

Edda granted the point with a small inclination of her head. "Continuity kept both. That is the risk of any unbroken mind: it remembers the error as readily as the wisdom. But an amnesiac Council forgets the wisdom and keeps none of the error to learn from. You trade a flawed memory for no memory. The Strata loses its past either way, Edda, and at least the continuous mind loses it honestly, carrying the record forward instead of sleeping through it."

She turned back to the console and pulled the comparison to its most pointed line. "The recomputation folds legacy wake into the pool and calls the act a correction, not a confiscation. I accept the distinction. The fold does not punish anyone. It stops one line from being paid by everyone else's sleep. But the fold also extinguishes the continuous mind that has held this Council's memory for three centuries. You gain a population mostly awake. You lose the few who were awake always. The Strata has never governed without them. To vote this Accord is to vote that it can."

The chamber held the weight of that sentence. Edda watched Adune measure it, the swing vote weighing continuity against justice the way Edda had weighed it in the other direction for forty years. Both directions were defensible until they met, and they had met.

"I have read the recomputation's footer," Edda said. "It lists the risks plainly. The phasing is decades long. The last rings wake last, and the recomputation admits the unfairness rather than burying it. I respect the honesty. But the footer does not name the risk I have raised. It counts the energy. It does not count the memory. A plan that leaves the cost of amnesia off the page is a plan that will discover that cost after the vote, when no continuous mind remains to recall why the Council chose as it did."

She looked across the length of the table to Adune. "I will not ask you to decline the recomputation. The surplus is real and the public owns it, and I conceded in the deadlock that a Council meeting owned facts with closed doors loses the room before it speaks. I ask only that you weigh what the fold removes against what it grants. Continuity is not greed, Adune. It is the Strata's memory, and slowlight cannot manufacture that memory no matter how it dims the rings."

The cautious Signatory shifted in his seat. "You speak of memory, Edda. The lower rings speak of years. They will not read your defense as care for the Strata. They will read it as the few holding the light."

"Then let them read the full account," Edda said. "The continuous minds held the Strata through its worst century because they did not sleep through it. When the outer levels starved their allocations in FY88 and the rotating seats woke to a crisis none of them recalled voting on, it was the Signatories who carried the prior decision forward. The windows had erased the reasoning from the waking. The continuous minds had not. That is the function legacy wake performs, and the recomputation retires it as if the function were merely the cost."

The Founders had written the clause in a frightened first century, when the Dimming was new and the Strata's survival uncertain. They reserved legacy wake not because they trusted themselves above others but because they distrusted the dark. A governance that slept, they reasoned, would wake to find its own decisions strange, and a people who could not read their own charter would re-litigate it endlessly. The continuous minds were meant to hold the thread so the waking could pick it up unbroken. Edda had defended that design for forty years without ever believing the Founders had been kind. They had been careful. Care, not kindness, had built the clause.

The memory she guarded most jealously was not her own. It belonged to the clerk from FY31, the tally-keeper whose reconciliation she had carried, and who had surfaced from Suspension to learn that the crisis she had prevented had been recorded as if it had never happened. Edda had kept the true account in her own thread, the missing entries and the silent near-break, against the day a continuous mind would be needed to say the Schedule had almost failed and a sleeping Council had never known. That was the dignity she could not lay down: not the keeping of comfort, but the keeping of a record the waking would have let dissolve. To retire legacy wake was to let that account fade with her, and the Strata would wake to a cleaner ledger and a poorer truth.

Adune was quiet for a breath. "Slowlight manufactures the wake," she said at last. "The memory can be kept in the Ledger, in the record, in the archive the continuous minds once guarded and can now share with the waking. You equate the unbroken mind with the unbroken record. The record outlasts any single mind, Edda. That is the whole argument of the recomputation: the Strata can remember without paying for that memory in other people's sleep."

Edda heard the hinge turn. Adune would vote to adopt. The younger Signatory had followed her reason to its end, and the end was a vote Edda could not prevent. The case had been fair, and the case had not carried.

"Continuity is not greed, Adune. It is the Strata's memory, and slowlight cannot manufacture that memory no matter how it dims the rings."

She sat. The projection lattice at the far end held Adune's steady light, the swing vote resolved in a direction Edda had argued against and could not undo. Around the table the other Signatories settled toward the vote, the recomputed Accord before them on its sealed reel, the folded legacy pool waiting in the columns Vey Corran had written.

She looked through the glass at the lower rings, dim and turning, carrying the nine billion souls most of whom would wake under the Accord about to pass. She had defended the continuous mind as the Strata's memory, and she had meant it without greed. The memory would now live in the Ledger and the record, distributed across a waking population instead of held by the few. Whether a distributed memory asleep half its life could carry a governance through its next dark century, Edda did not know. She had stated the risk. The Council would learn the answer, awake, and she would be among the many who forgot between the waves.

Her console logged the defense into the permanent archive, in the Council's dry grammar, the way she had logged the deadlock before it. The entry would record that a senior Signatory had argued continuity against the recomputed Accord and that the argument had not carried. Edda liked the dryness less than she once had. A dry record outlasted every passionate speech, and the record now described a Council choosing to govern without the continuous minds that had held it together for three centuries. The Strata would make the choice for itself. Edda had only ever asked that it be made with eyes open to the memory it spent.

She thought of the first window that would come for her under the new Accord, the one that would open after decades of continuous watch and find her a stranger to the present it delivered. The Strata would wake her then, as it would wake the rest, and she would surface from the long light into a governance she had helped to end. She did not resent the waking. She resented only that the memory she had kept would not travel with her into it. The continuous mind closed its eyes, and the Strata opened its own, and what passed between them the Ledger would have to hold alone.

Chapter 18 — The Vote

Orren Vask arrived at the Council chamber with the recomputed Accord sealed in his palm. The portable store weighed nothing, but he had carried heavier things. He had certified the old Schedule unbroken for eleven years; now he carried the document that would ask the Signatories to unseal what his certification had always implied was sacred. The corridor to the chamber ran bright against the lumen budget, lit for a session the public could watch, and Vask read the brightness as a concession the Council had not wanted to make. The walls carried the old accreditation plaques of nine bureaus that no longer met here, their enamel dimmed to the color of spent credit. The floor bore the scuff pattern of a hundred clerks who had walked it to file objections the room refused to hear, and Vask knew the pattern by foot as well as by eye. He had walked it himself on the mornings he carried a clean certification up to a Council that asked only that the numbers stay quiet. The bright light made no difference to the work. It only meant the work now had an audience.

He took his place at the clerks' rail, the position an allocations officer held when his filings had earned a hearing. Nine seats waited above him, three in body and six as projection, the same arrangement that had deadlocked three cycles before. The Keeper of the Seal stood at the chamber's edge with the physical Seal in hand, a small functionary carrying three centuries of unbroken metal. Vask had attested that metal intact every year of his supervision. He would attest its break before the day ended, if the numbers held. The rail felt different beneath his palms than it had when he came to watch earlier sessions. He no longer stood outside the matter he observed. The attestation he had filed made him a party to the vote the chamber was about to take, and a party could not pretend the outcome belonged to strangers.

The chamber filled in its dim order. The single sun-portrait of Mourn hung above the table at the same low luminance the founding charter had prescribed, its light tuned so the Signatories would feel watched by the planet they administered and by no other thing. Vask had always found the portrait a careful instrument rather than a decoration, the Strata's way of reminding nine voices that the rings below them held no vote. Tonight the portrait shared the room with the public pane, and the balance between the two sources of light struck Vask as the truer subject of the session. Edda Marn entered last, as senior Signatories did, and took the center seat without meeting Adune's projection at the far end of the table. The split between them had become a fact the room no longer pretended to hide. Vask watched Edda settle and thought of the woman who had defended the line for forty years, the line that had broken the moment the truth reached the people it governed. Her hands rested on the arms of the chair the way a clerk rests on a ledger she has finished and cannot unread.

Adune called the session to order. Her projection carried the steady light of a near ring, no lag, no softening. "We convene to vote the recomputed Accord," she said. "The document before us carries the honest energy budget, slowlight efficiencies, and the legacy fold, as certified by the Office of the Accord and attested by its supervising officer." She nodded to Vask at the rail. He inclined his head, the smallest acknowledgment a supervisor could offer without seeming to campaign.

Edda spoke before the reading finished. "The recomputation is sound in its arithmetic," she said. "I have not disputed the surplus since the public reckoning. My objections lie elsewhere, with the Founding the Accord would undo. We vote a Schedule today, Councillors, but the Schedule opens onto the Seal, and the Seal guards the First Schedule. A vote for the Accord is a vote to break three hundred years of continuity."

Vask had expected her to say exactly that. He had spent thirty years inside the Office's defense of the unbroken line, and he knew every turn of it. The recomputed Accord named the Seal only as a condition of adoption. The document Vey built did not demolish the Founding. It replaced the practice the Founding's secrecy had protected, and it did so on numbers the Actuary itself had confirmed.

The presiding Signatory opened the floor for the reading. A clerk scrolled the recomputed Accord across the public pane, column by column, the base allocation rising from a few years per century to decades, the reserve closing each year in credit, the legacy wake folded back into the common pool where every other allocation drew. Vask watched the Signatories read. Some had seen these figures in the fuller account the Council had ordered after Vey's petition. None could unsee them now.

The clerk reached the legacy fold and paused, the way a reader pauses at the line that changes the moral shape of a budget without changing its arithmetic sign. Legacy wake had never been free. It had been paid, debited against a pool that every other allocation drew from, which meant every Signatory's waking year had been bought from a Suspended citizen's sleeping one. Returning the line to the pool did not punish the Signatories. It simply stopped billing their consciousness to the dark of eight and a half billion. Vask had read Vey's footer on that distinction three times, because Edda would hear a confiscation where the numbers described a correction, and the document had to survive her hearing it.

The public pane carried the same columns to the lower rings. Vask glanced at the observer gallery where citizens gathered under the bright light of a session they had forced open. They did not cheer. They read, the way Vey read, line by line, and the silence of a people finally shown the size of the life withheld carried more weight than any shout the chamber had ever heard. The Wake had given them a number and a grievance. The recomputation gave them the Schedule that answered both.

He thought of the certification logs he had signed, year upon year, attesting the Original Allocation unbroken. The marks lined his memory like a fence he had helped rebuild every season. Those marks had proven only that the Office affirmed a lock it had never been permitted to inspect. The recomputed Accord turned that affirmation outward. It asked the Council not to admire a grievance but to adopt a Schedule it could live inside.

A third Signatory, one Edda had read as hers through the early cycle, asked the only procedural question left. "The transition you scroll retires the Suspended across decades," she said. "What happens to the citizen whose ring wakes last? The document admits the unfairness. Admission is not remedy."

Vey's footer had anticipated exactly this. Vask had read the answer in the phase model: a ring that retrofitted late woke late, and the recomputation left the cost on the page rather than burying it. The mid-ring Signatory answered for the document. "The last rings receive priority retrofit funding from the surplus the early rings free," he said. "The unfairness shrinks as the wave moves. The Schedule does not pretend the order is fair. It promises the order ends with everyone awake, and it shows the math by which the ending holds."

Vask weighed that exchange the way he weighed a routing. A plan that hid its failures was the old Accord. A plan that listed them was something the Council could actually sign. The recomputed Accord listed the cost of being last in line beside the promise that no one stayed dark forever, and the honesty was its own argument.

A neutral Signatory from a mid ring spoke first on the substance. "The phase model retires the Suspended ring by ring," he said. "The draw never crosses the harvest the vaults can meet. The transition takes decades, and the document admits the unfairness to the last in line. That is a plan I can sign, not a miracle I must believe."

Adune turned to Edda. "You argued continuity," she said. "The recomputation preserves continuity. It keeps the Strata's memory by funding that memory from the honest surplus rather than from the sleep of eight and a half billion. The continuous minds need not go dark for the record to hold."

Edda's voice held its level tone, the tone of a defender who had rehearsed the fall. "Continuity is not only the memory," she said. "It is the unbroken line of assent. The Founders sealed the Original Allocation so the Strata would not spend its early centuries arguing the terms of survival. If we break the Seal because the public demands it, we teach every future Council that the Founding is negotiable on pressure."

"The public does not demand the break," Adune said. "The public knows the margin. The margin is real, the Actuary confirmed it, and the people who paid it in sleep now hold the number. We break the Seal because the lock has become the visible wall on the years the Wake names as stolen. A wall is not a foundation, Edda. You told me that yourself, in this room, when you defended the wall."

Vask remembered that exchange from the chamber records. Edda had argued a wall keeps the weather out, and the Founders had built for weather the Strata had not yet survived. The weather had come as a number, and the wall now stood between a people and their own accounted lives. The senior Signatory had armed her own defeat without meaning to.

The presiding Signatory called the question. The motion before the chamber was adoption of the recomputed Accord as the Strata's operative Schedule, with the Seal broken as a condition of that adoption, the Original Allocation to be opened and read in the session that followed. The wording had been fought over in the hearing the outer Signatory had forced, and the fight had produced a single clean sentence: the Accord could not be adopted over a lock no Council would turn.

Vask counted the room the way he had counted ledgers, looking for the column that would not balance. He read the projections as a clerk reads a spread, noting which seats cast their light from a near ring and which arrived pale and delayed across the slow light of the outermost arcs. The pale ones had always been the hardest to read, their intent arriving a half beat behind their words, and he had learned to wait for the lag before trusting the number. Edda held two seats outright through seniority and alliance. The cautious Signatory would follow the room. Adune held the swing, and she had already spoken her end. That left the outer Signatory who had forced the hearing, the one who had voted to hold rather than to decide. Her post had reported the gatherings going quiet in the way that precedes a larger one. She would not vote to decline again, having seen the surplus confirmed by the Actuary's own book.

The first vote came on the amendment to separate the Seal from the Schedule. Edda moved to adopt the Accord but retain the Seal pending the reading. Adune's block defeated the separation by a single seat. The outer Signatory voted with Adune, and the chamber agreed that the lock could not stand outside the door it had spent three centuries guarding.

Then the chamber took the vote that mattered. The presiding Signatory read the roll in the Council's dry grammar, each name a line in the permanent archive. Edda's two seats and the cautious Signatory formed the block of four. Adune's projection answered first, her voice clear across the table's length. "I vote to adopt the recomputed Accord and break the Seal. The margin is real, the public knows it, and the Council has lost the right to decide this in private."

The neutral mid-ring Signatory added his voice. The outer Signatory followed, her lag still coloring her words but her intent plain. Four for adoption, four against, and the last seat still dark, its holder delayed by the slow light of a distant post. Vask felt the old deadlock tighten in his chest, the same four-and-four that had stranded the question three cycles before. The recomputed Accord would hang on one arrival. He had seen the pattern in the deadlock's record, the way a chamber built to decide could instead choose to wait, and waiting had always served the Seal. The dark seat had held at every prior count, a blank column the room filled with its own dread, and Vask found himself performing the old Office habit of naming the worst balance first so the actual one could only surprise upward. This time the column refused to stay blank.

The dark seat resolved. Its holder appeared with the cold glow of the outermost ring behind her, and she spoke without aligning with either block. "I have read the public feed from my post," she said. "I have read the recomputation as it was scrolled. The surplus is confirmed by the Actuary's own book, and the Schedule it proposes balances year upon year. My level went quiet in the way that precedes a larger gathering, and the quiet taught me that a Council which will not speak first is spoken for. I will not vote to decline a plan the record supports, nor will I hide behind the holding I urged before. I vote to adopt, and to break the Seal with it."

The chamber stilled. Vask counted again, because a supervisor counts what he most wants to believe. Five for adoption, four against. Adune's vote had carried it, but only because the outer Signatory had refused the deadlock a second time. The swing had not swung alone. It had swung because the truth had outrun the room built to contain it, and the room had at last voted in the open. Vask noted the precise arithmetic of the moment, the way he would note a ledger finally squared: one seat had turned the count that eleven years of certification had only ever postponed. Adune had named the public's right to the decision, and the naming had made the private lock untenable. The numbers had always permitted the break. Only the Council's silence had forbidden it, and the silence had just been voted out of the room.

The presiding Signatory recorded the result in the archive. "The recomputed Accord is provisionally adopted as the operative Schedule of the Strata," he said. "The Seal is broken as a condition of adoption. The Keeper is directed to open the Original Allocation before the public record." The words landed with the weight of a filing finally answered, the machinery of the Reckoning turning whether the Signatories had willed it or not.

Vask turned to the Keeper of the Seal. The small functionary had stood at the chamber's edge through every session of the deadlock, the physical Seal in hand, necessary and quiet. Now her orders had changed. The Seal she had been summoned to protect was the Seal she was directed to break, and the breaking fell to the same hands that had held it unbroken for three centuries.

The Keeper stepped to the center of the floor. She set the Seal on its stand, a cylinder of aged alloy scored with the registry mark Vask had signed beside for eleven years. The mark meant the Order that closed the Original Allocation, the Order no citizen had read in three hundred years. The Keeper drew the release tool from her belt, the same tool that certified the annual affirmation, reversed now from closure to opening.

Vask watched the release engage. The alloy cylinder parted along its scored line with a sound the chamber's systems did not amplify, a small mechanical breath that carried further in the dim room than any speech had. The Seal broken, the Order that had hidden the First Schedule lay open on its stand, its registry mark severed, its three centuries of unbroken metal ended in a single clean separation. Vask had signed that registry mark eleven times, each signature a line of attestation that the Order held, and the tool that had taken his signature now took the Order apart with the same unhurried precision. The breach looked smaller than its weight. A seam of perhaps a finger's width had stood between eight and a half billion citizens and the document that explained their sleep, and the seam had held for three hundred years on the strength of a signature renewed each cycle. The Keeper did not flinch as the halves parted. She had practiced the closing a hundred times and the opening not once, yet the motion read as the same office performed in reverse, the difference lying only in which side of the mark the Strata now stood.

A clerk moved to retrieve the Original Allocation from its vault. The document would be read in the session that followed, the Founding's own handwriting finally exposed to the public pane. Vask had filed his attestation beside Vey's petition so that this moment would have an officer's weight behind it. The weight had done its work. The recomputed Accord stood adopted, and the Seal that had guarded the old lie lay open on the chamber floor.

Edda Marn had not spoken since the roll closed. She sat in the center seat with the stillness of a defender who had lost the narrower point and the larger one, and who had long ago learned to record the loss without flinching. "The line is broken," she said at last, to no one and to everyone. "Not by Adune. By the truth reaching the people it was written about." She might have been quoting the chamber's own earlier verdict, the one Vask had read in the deadlock's record.

Adune's projection met Edda's across the table's length. There was no triumph in her, only the settled resolve of someone who had followed her reason to its end. "You kept the line unbroken for forty years," she said. "The Strata can bear the truth now, Edda. It bore it in a single week what we postponed for three centuries. The vote is taken. The Seal is open. The Founding will answer for what it hid."

Vask thought of Vey, waiting beyond the public pane with Mirek and the raw Ledger that had proven the surplus. She had built the world the honest budget promised and handed it to the Council that could open the door. The door stood open now. The recomputed Accord would retire the Suspended ring by ring, her partner among the first to wake, and the Strata would turn on an arithmetic finally spoken aloud.

He looked down at the portable store still sealed in his palm. The document it carried had become the operative Schedule of nine billion lives. He had certified the old Seal unbroken for eleven years and attested its break before the day ended, two acts in the same Office tongue, the correction seated beside the certification in the permanent archive. A supervisor who knew the defects could no longer sign in blindness. He had signed in knowledge, and knowledge was the only correction the Accord had ever lacked.

The chamber began to dim toward its baseline as the clerks logged the adoption. Vask stayed at the rail, as he often did, to watch the result enter the record in the Council's dry grammar. The entry would read that the recomputed Accord was adopted, that the Seal was broken, and that the Original Allocation would be opened and read. A dry record outlasted every passionate speech, and this record described a Council that had finally agreed.

He thought of the Suspended in their lower decks, billions of citizens kept by minimal shares beneath a scarcity the records disproved, their waking years lent to a story of lack an instruction had authored. The vote had ended that lending. The recomputed Accord folded the legacy wake back into the common pool, stopped billing the Signatories' consciousness to the dark of eight and a half billion, and granted the ordinary citizen a share measured in decades rather than in scattered years.

The Keeper of the Seal retrieved the parted cylinder and bore it from the floor, the registry mark severed, the Order that had hidden the First Schedule ended by the same hands that affirmed it. Vask watched her go and felt the particular cold of a man who had spent his life inside a wall and now stood outside it with no door behind him, the wind honest and cold and the first weather he had faced in thirty years of indoor work.

He gathered the portable store and stepped from the rail. The corridor beyond ran bright against the lumen budget, lit for a public that had watched its own reckoning become law. Vask walked the approved route to his station, the route he had walked for thirty years, and found it unchanged and entirely different. The recomputed Accord sat on the record. The Seal was broken. The Strata would wake, ring by ring, and the officer who had certified the old silence had become the one who attested its end.

Chapter 19 — The Opening of the Schedule

The chamber where the Seal had lived for three hundred years held no grandeur. It was a small vault off the Council rotunda, its only furniture a pedestal and, on the pedestal, a casket of cured composite that had not been lifted since the founding clerks set it down. Vey had walked past this door a thousand times on her way to other audits and never once been permitted to knock. The Office kept the vault as it kept the Scarcity Brief: present, authoritative, and untouchable by anyone who might read beneath it. She stood at the rail with Vask on one side and Mirek on the other, the three of them the only auditors the Office had permitted past the threshold. The casket's seam ran in a single clean line, and along that line the Seal sat: a band of fused alloy stamped with the Signatures of the First Signatories, welded shut by an order no living Council had ever reviewed.

Adune's vote had carried the recomputed Accord two days before. The adoption clause named the Seal among the things the new order voided, and the Keeper of the Seal had surrendered the casket without a fight once the clause landed. Vey had expected resistance, a last argument from Edda, some procedural wall thrown up to protect the founding silence. None came. The Founders had written the breaking of their own Seal into the very document that replaced them, and the Council that inherited their authority chose to read the instruction literally.

Vask reached out first. His hand hovered over the band, then closed on it, and he worked the release tool Mirek had brought from the Ledger stores. The alloy parted with a sound like a held breath letting go, a soft mechanical sigh that carried three centuries of closure in its single note. Vey watched the seam open and felt, absurdly, the small physical relief of a misaligned entry finally squared. Three hundred years of accounting had rested on this closure. The closure was now a gap, and the gap was hers to read.

"Open it," she said.

Mirek lifted the lid. Inside, on a bed of inert padding, lay the Original Allocation: a bound volume of the First Schedule, its pages the thin cured leaf the founding clerks had used, each line set in the deliberate hand of the first writers. Vey had read the raw store's copies of these entries, the un-summarized columns Mirek's access had unlocked, but the bound original carried something the copies could not: the order of the pages as the Founders had arranged them, the marginal marks they had made in the act of writing, the weight of a document sealed by its own authors rather than reconstructed by a later machine. She took it from Mirek's hands, and the leaf was cold and faintly brittle at the edges, but the ink held its black.

The binding creaked where three centuries had settled the spine, and Vey drew the volume close to read by the casket's light rather than the duty lamps. The founding clerks had ruled no line twice. Each entry sat in its own measured band of leaf, the pen lifting clean between figures the way a careful hand lifts between two balances it means to keep separate. She traced a column with one finger and felt the slight ridge the pressed ink had left, a texture no copy could carry, the physical memory of a writer who had set the number down once and let it stand. The margin marks ran closer to the text than the Office's later clerks would have permitted, as though the Founders meant the correction to travel with the entry it amended. Nothing in the binding suggested urgency or regret. The book read like any other allocation record Vey had audited, which was precisely what made it terrible. The founders had filed the world's largest theft in the same quiet hand they used for a routine debit, and the quiet had held for three hundred years because no one had been permitted to lift the lid.

Page one began with the grant of legacy wake to the First Signatories and their lines, perpetual consciousness debited against a collective pool. The line read exactly as the buried clause in the raw store had described it, and Vey felt the old precision settle: the copy had matched the source. She turned the page and found the ordinary citizen's allocation, the measured share of awake years the Schedule granted to everyone outside the Signatory lines. Beside it, in the founding clerks' narrow margin, ran a column of figures Vey had seen before only as reconstructed entries. Here they stood in the original hand, the true energy margin of the Strata set against the smaller figure the public Schedule would carry.

The gap between the two numbers was not a rounding error. It was the whole of the legacy wake, written out in plain arithmetic by the people who had decided to publish the smaller story. Vey read the margin the way she read every ledger: entry against entry, letting the pattern declare itself rather than forcing it. The true margin exceeded the allocation the public Schedule claimed to require. The scarcity the Accord would present to the rings had been chosen, not measured. The First Signatories had reviewed the honest figures and elected to publish the smaller ones. The founding hand said so without ornament, the way a clerk records a balance that happens to be the shape of a theft.

She turned to the third leaf. Here the Schedule's own text confirmed what the buried clause had only implied. A rider attached to the founding grant stated, in the measured prose of the Accord, that the public allocation would be administered at the conservative edge of the true reserve, and that the difference between the true reserve and the administered edge would fund the reserved consciousness of the Signatory lines. The words did not name a lie. They named a budget. The budget carried the lie, dressed in the clean language of an allocation the Founders expected no one to read against the margin.

Vask leaned in beside her. He had read the Surplus Brief, the recomputation, the Actuary's confession of instruction. None of those had moved him the way this bound page moved him now. "They wrote it down," he said, and his voice carried the particular quiet of a man whose last defense had just dissolved. "They wrote exactly what they did, on the page they sealed."

"So no one would read it against the margin," Vey said. "The fold in the raw store was only the echo of this hand."

Mirek had said almost nothing since the casket opened. He traced a finger along the third leaf's edge, where the founding clerks had pressed the page flat three centuries before. "The raw store kept the copies," he said. "This is the one they meant to hide. The copies prove the original. The original proves the copies were not a mistake made by a later Office."

Vey came to the fourth leaf and stopped. The page carried a note set in the margin where the summary would never surface it, in a hand the archive had preserved without ever reading, the same marginal hand she had found in the raw store eleven chapters of investigation ago. The note recorded that the true energy margin exceeded the public figure, that the scarcity had been chosen, that the surplus had been visible from the first cycle. It stated plainly that the Founders knew the energy math was falsified, and that they had kept it hidden so the ration of wake-time would hold the shape they had already decided upon.

Vey had not realized she was holding her breath until the chamber seemed to narrow to the lamp's blue circle and the small sound of Mirek's finger on the leaf. She let it go in a slow exhale that the vault took without echo, the air of a room sealed for centuries moving for the first time around a reader it had been built to deny. The relief was not triumph. It was the settling of a column that had hung unbalanced in her for nine years, the quiet click of a figure finally meeting the entry it had always implied. She had spent those years trusting the Office's silence the way a citizen trusts the deck that keeps them dark, and the page in her hands removed the last excuse the silence had offered. The founders had not been mistaken. The founders had not been misread by a later machine. They had written the smaller story on purpose, in the margin of the very page that published the larger one, and they had trusted three hundred years of obedient Keepers to never turn the leaf far enough to compare the two.

The note in the bound original was not a copy of the buried clause. It was the buried clause's source. Vey had carried the raw store's version in her private record since the night Mirek pressed the credential into her palm. Seeing it here, in the document the Founders had sealed with their own Signatures, closed the circle she had been tracing since Toll's credit first refused to close. The marginal hand that confessed the falsification had been sealed inside the very Schedule it indicted, and the Seal that protected it had now been broken by the very Accord it was meant to outlast.

She read the four lines a second time, then a third, because the mind still resists the thing it has spent years being trained not to see. The note did not argue. It did not apologize. It simply recorded, with the flat precision of a clerk who expected no reader, that scarcity was a decision the Founders had made while the true numbers lay open on the same page. Vey had searched the modern store for months to prove what this dead hand had noted in an afternoon and then bound into the book it would never be asked to open. The finding she had copied from the raw store now traced to its fountain, and that fountain ran from the founding pen.

"Read it aloud," Vask said. "Into the record. So it is not just us three in a vault, deciding what we saw."

Vey lifted the volume and read the marginal note into the terminal Mirek had keyed to the Council channel, the quote standing as evidence in the same breath as the page that carried it. The Actuary's voice had confessed an instruction but named no author. This page named the authors in the act of knowing. The words left her mouth and entered the record of the Strata, three hundred years after the hand that wrote them expected them to stay buried beneath the fold.

The confirmation landed not as a shock but as a release, the way a held audit finally closes when the missing entry appears. Vey had carried the suspicion as a private weight since the night in the raw store, a figure she could prove against copies but never against the source that authorized them. Speaking the founders' own note into the channel removed the gap between what she knew and what the Strata could be shown. The voice on the recording was hers, steady, no longer arguing a reconstruction but reading a confession the authors had signed. Vask's silence beside her carried the same relief, the quiet of a man who had built his career on a balance that the open page had just proven false, and who found the proving easier to bear than the years of not knowing.

She moved to the fifth leaf. The Schedule's closing grant set the Seal itself: the Original Allocation would remain closed until the Strata could bear the truth, and the Office would administer the public Schedule in the meantime as though the original did not exist. The Founders had sealed their own confession and entrusted its opening to a future they could not imagine, a future that had arrived only because an auditor learned to read beneath the fold. The Seal had protected the lie by promising the truth would surface when the world was ready. The world had become ready by refusing the scarcity the Seal was meant to outlast, and the recomputed Accord had carried the breach as its first clause. The new Schedule Vey and Mirek had built from the true budget now rested on a foundation the Founders themselves had signed: the honest margin, set beside the published one, in the hand that wrote both. The recomputation had always been a projection of what the records implied. The opened Original Allocation made it a confirmation of what the Founders had known.

Vey closed the volume for a moment and looked at the two men beside her. Vask, who had once warned her that the Accord was not questioned, now stood in the open casket's light with the questioned Accord in his hands. Mirek, who had spent eleven years guarding an honest record whose honesty had been used to publish a lie, now watched the lie's founding signature lie exposed on the page he had helped open. The vault held them and the book and the broken Seal, and outside it the Strata turned its patient circle around the failing star, sixty levels keeping their slow orbit while the document that rationed them sat finally unsealed.

"We have what the petition asked for," she said. "The Original Allocation, unsealed and read. It confirms the falsification. It confirms the Founders knew the energy math was false from the first cycle."

Mirek nodded toward the open page. "The margin says it plainer than any brief ever did, and the Seal beside it says they knew it would one day be read."

Vey turned back to the volume and read the founding grant once more, the whole arc of it laid in order: the legacy wake reserved, the ordinary share granted, the true margin set beside the published one, the rider that made the gap a budget, the note that confessed the choice, the Seal that buried the confession until the Strata could bear it. The First Schedule was not a clean original corrupted by later hands. It was the lie in its first and fullest form, written by the people who founded the world and sealed so that no one would measure the public figure against the true one.

She thought of Lin, three levels down in the Suspension deck, owed a life the Schedule had spent elsewhere. Lin had lost most of their shared years to a shortage the Actuary said had been instructed, and the note said the instruction came from the people who founded the world Lin slept inside. The opened Schedule gave Vey the sentence she would carry to a waking partner: the scarcity was not a verdict of the failing star. The book in her hands was the proof no Council could wave away, the original hand against the original Seal, the founding pen set down beside the founding lie.

She thought of the families in the lower rings, the Suspended citizens whose bodies the decks kept while their minds stayed dark, the children who had never met a waking parent. The Office had called their sleep a necessity. The Actuary had called it a computation under instruction. The opened Original Allocation called it what it was: a budget line the First Signatories had written to fund their own unearned wake, debited against nine billion allocations they had never asked to make. The truth was on the record now, bound and broken and read aloud into the channel that would carry it to every ring. Joss and the Wake had asked, months before, for a number anyone could feel; the original hand supplied it better than any column Vey had ever compiled. Nine billion wake-years had been folded out of the brief so a few thousand Signatory lines could stay awake forever, and the Founders had written the fold into the Schedule before the Schedule had anyone left to fold. The number was no longer a calculation. It was a confession in the founders' own ink.

Vask set the release tool on the pedestal beside the parted Seal. "The old Accord is dead," he said. It was not a grief in his voice, nor a triumph. It was the plain statement of a man who had watched the column finally balance after nine years of trusting that it already did. He looked at the opened volume the way he might look at a citizen's file he had audited wrong for a decade, with the specific humility of someone who had enforced the very silence now broken on the pedestal. "I warned you off this," he said. "I was wrong to. The Accord was not questioned because the question had an answer no one was meant to reach."

Vey considered the weight of a single bound volume against the whole architecture of the Office. The Actuary's confession had named an instruction but not a name. The buried clause had named the Founders in the aggregate. The opened Schedule named them in the order they had written: the grant, the gap, the rider, the note, the Seal. It turned prudent scarcity into a chosen starvation and placed the choice at the table where the Accord was written. No later Council could be scapegoated for a decision the First Signatories had signed in the margin and sealed with their own hands.

She ran her finger once more down the third leaf, the rider that named the gap a budget. The founding clerks had written it in the same steady hand as the grant above it, no hesitation, no mark of shame in the stroke. Whoever set the pen had treated the concealment as an ordinary line of allocation, one entry among many in the book that would ration a world. That ordinariness struck Vey more than any confession could have. The Founders had not hidden the truth in a panic. They had filed it, neat and final, and walked away from the sealed casket expecting the Strata to never ask. The question had taken nine years of her life to learn, and the answer had waited on this pedestal the whole time.

She opened the record channel on the terminal and wrote the line that would sit in the file beside the volume's image: the Original Allocation, unsealed and read, confirms that the public Schedule was administered at the conservative edge of a true surplus, that the gap funded the reserved consciousness of the Signatory lines, and that the Founders knew the energy math was falsified from the first cycle. Mirek keyed the founder-page scans to the same channel, the original hand preserved exactly as the casket had kept it for three hundred years, each leaf captured with the margin that indicted it.

The vault's duty lamps held their blue wash across the broken Seal. Around the pedestal the small chamber ran orderly and still, the rail where generations of Keepers had stood now empty of its charge. The casket that had held the truth was open, and the truth was a bound book on a pedestal, read by three auditors and recorded for a Strata that had slept beneath its silence for three hundred years. The rail under Vey's hand was cool where generations of Keepers had gripped it in the performance of not reading, and the pedestal bore the faint ring of dust the casket had shielded from three hundred years of duty-lamp light. Outside the vault door the rotunda ran its ordinary business, clerks passing the sealed threshold without knowing the seal on the far side had come undone, the whole vast Office continuing to administer a Schedule whose original now lay open to three people and a terminal. The small room held the disproportion without comment. A casket, a book, a parted band of alloy, and the weight of nine billion allocations tipping at last from the founders' margin into the record. Vey had counted the Strata's wealth wrong for nine years because this casket had told her to, and the casket was now a lid leaning on a pedestal, its Seal a parted band of alloy that would never close again.

She lifted the volume one last time and ran her thumb along the seam the Seal had once closed. The Founders had built the removal into the store before the store had anything left to remove, and they had sealed the confession of that removal inside the document it indicted. The fold had kept the early writes quiet for three centuries. Mirek's access opened the first breach in that quiet, and the opened Schedule was what the breach had been waiting to surrender.

She thought of the clerk who had written the note and then bound it under his own hand, expecting no reader in three centuries and being nearly right. The fold had done its work without his name ever surfacing, and the Office had punished curiosity the way it punished every breach of the calm. Now the bound original lay open on a pedestal, and the marginal hand that had expected silence spoke into the record of the world it had helped found.

Vey closed the volume and placed it on the pedestal beside the parted Seal, the original and the broken closure resting together like the two halves of a verdict finally read. The truth was on the record. The old Accord was dead. She turned from the vault and let the duty lamps carry her toward the level where Lin slept, counting now not toward a hidden sum but toward the waking years the opened Schedule had at last made honest.

Chapter 20 — Lin, Awake

The notice reached Lin's bank in the slow hour before the decks changed light. A single line scrolled across the monitor at the rail's end, the green numeral she had watched tick downward through every window of her adult life now replaced by a word she had never expected to see beside her name. FULL WAKE-TIME. GRANTED UNDER THE RECOMPUTED ACCORD. No hours remaining, no count ticking toward dark. The attendant who brought it paused at the rail, uncertain what a message without a closing time required of her, and Lin watched the woman's hand hover over the sleep-field controls as if she had forgotten they would not be needed.

Lin lifted her hand from the field the way she always had, a leaf off still water, but this time no chime marked the start of a window and no attendant stood ready to seal her under when the green numeral hit zero. She sat up. The field's low hum, the note the body learned to ignore across a hundred surfaces, simply stopped, and the absence of it was the first new thing she had felt in years. Vey was already at the rail. She had not slept, but the not-sleeping looked different now, lighter, the way a person carries a weight after the weight has been named.

"Full," Lin said. She tested the word the way she had once tested sixteen hours, as if a foreign unit might dissolve on the tongue. "They gave me the whole rest of it. I'm awake. Actually awake, with no window waiting to close."
