"They gave it to nine billion," Vey said. "The recomputation closed the year with the vaults fuller than the briefs ever admitted. You were first on the list they unsealed, but you are not alone on it." She set her satchel down, the same grip through the rail, the same steadiness Lin had measured her own hand against across eleven surfaces. "The Accord that deferred you is dead. The Schedule that closed on you is open. What the Council voted in the chamber reached your bank this morning."

Lin took the grip and held. The sleep-field was cool beneath her, no longer pulling, and she found she did not know what to do with a body that was hers to keep. She had spent the whole of her waking life counting the dark between windows. The count was over, and the silence where the count had been was its own kind of unfamiliar. "I don't remember how to be the one who stays," she said.

"Then we learn it," Vey said. "I have the hours to learn it with you. All of them, apparently. The Strata finally admitted the light was enough."

They walked out of the Suspension deck together, and Lin felt the difference in her own gait before she felt it in the world. Eleven windows had taught her to surface small, to spend the first hours finding her feet before the dark took them back. Now her feet stayed. The transit lane carried its faint warmth, the deck's old kindness, and she walked it without the pressure of a closing numeral behind every step. Vey matched her pace, said nothing, let the walking be its own first conversation.

Morning came through the single window in a slow pour, the amber that slowlight left behind after the Strata's lamps stepped down for the day's long rest. Lin stood in the open doorway for a moment before crossing it, bare feet on the worn plank, and the floor held the faint warmth of someone who had walked it a thousand times without her. The air carried the smell of the apartment the way a body carries its own: old paper, the faint metallic cleanliness of audit-office air filtered up through the rings, a thread of something warm Vey had cooked in the years Lin could not count. Nothing in the room moved to meet her. That was the strangeness. For eleven windows the world had always been tilted toward the next closing, every surface borrowed, every hour on loan. Here the stillness simply continued, indifferent to any numeral, and she had no practice for a room that would still be there at the end of the day.

Vey had left the kettle on the low burner, the way she must have left it every evening, and it ticked as it cooled, a small domestic clock that asked nothing of her. Lin filled two cups from the tap, the water cold against her wrist, and set them on the sill beside the star lamp. The ordinary gesture felt foreign in her hands, a task with no window closing behind it, no attendant waiting to seal the field. She watched the steam rise and dissipate into the amber and thought that this, the unhurried making of a thing no one had scheduled, was the first fully-awake act she had ever performed. Vey came to the doorway and took the second cup without a word, and the quiet between them was not the thin quiet of a four-hour visit but the wider quiet of a morning with nowhere to end.

The apartment above the audit offices had not changed shape in the years Lin was under, but it had changed meaning. She stood in the doorway and took in the left drawer, still a disaster, the forms spilling the way Vey had described it through the rail. On the sill sat the star lamp, the paper one with the star cut crooked, mended along one crease with the careful strip of adhesive Lin remembered only as something Vey had done. She picked it up. The paper was thicker than her window-memory claimed, repaired so many times it had gained weight.

"You kept the lamp lit," Lin said.

"I kept it," Vey said. "Not always lit. Sometimes I let it gutter because the dark on the sill was honest about what the Accord had taken. Then I would light it again because you told me a dark unit needed one light that was ours." She leaned against the doorframe, the posture of someone home from a long audit. "I became a stranger to you in the gaps. Eleven windows, four hours each, and the woman who came up between them was three hundred years of policy wearing your face. I read your face like a summary I was not permitted to open."

Lin turned the lamp in the light from the single window. "And I became a stranger to you. I came up small every time, carrying the four hours I'd been given, and I read you the way I read the rail, as something solid on the other side of the glass. I didn't know the woman who followed a loose thread to the bottom of the budget. I knew the auditor who visited. Those are different people."

"They are the same person," Vey said. "The visiting auditor was the one who could not stop counting what was missing. The one who found the surplus reached with the same hand and the same grip. You met the hand at the rail. You did not meet the finding."

Lin set the lamp on the sill where it belonged. "I meet her now. The stranger who is my partner. That's the strange part, Vey. I thought waking would be arriving home. It's arriving at a door I've never opened, with a person I've never sat with."

The rooms around them kept their ordinary sounds, the distant hum of the rings' ventilation, a neighbor's transit chime three decks down, the small settling of an old structure holding its people. Lin listened to it the way she had once listened for the sleep-field's closing chime, but nothing here gathered toward an ending. The continuity was its own disorientation. She had built eleven lives out of interruption, each one cut before it could accumulate into a self. Now the morning refused to be cut, and the self that remained when nothing closed on her was larger than any window had permitted, strange with the weight of hours that would not be taken back. Vey moved through the apartment with the ease of someone who had lived every one of the missing years, and Lin followed, a guest in the life her partner had kept without her, learning the floorboards by the way they gave underfoot.

They ate in the small kitchen because eating was the one thing the windows had never permitted enough of, and Lin found the ordinary act foreign. She measured rice into a pot the way she had measured hours, by habit, and Vey laughed, a short unguarded sound Lin had heard maybe twice across the surfaces. "You portion like the Schedule," Vey said. "Two scoops, exactly the allocation. You can cook for a day now. A day is a long time to feed."

"I don't know what a day is for," Lin said. She let the extra rice go into the pot, more than four hours could ever have used, and watched it settle. "I know what four hours are for. You visit, we talk, the window closes. I don't know what to do with the part after the visit that used to be dark."

"Neither do I, exactly," Vey said. "I filled it with the Ledger. That's how I found the surplus, by refusing to stop at the line the Office permitted. The finding ate my dark the way Suspension ate yours. We were both diminished by the same machine, just on opposite sides of the rail." She stirred the pot, competent and unhurried in a way Lin had only glimpsed through glass. "We are not who we were when you went under at FY291. You were the one who packed the left drawer and left the lamp on the sill. I was the one who trusted the summaries. The Strata remade us both. The question is whether we like the remade versions well enough to stay."

Lin considered the question the way she had once considered the green numeral, as a thing with weight. "I like the one who followed the thread. I like the one who mended the lamp nine times. I like the one who stood at the rail counting what was missing and then decided the missing was a lie. Those are the parts of you I want to sit with. The parts I don't know yet, I'll learn."

"And the parts of you," Vey said, "the ones the windows kept small. The woman who was worth four hours and decided four was not enough, who read the count and put her name in the book of the Wake. I want to sit with her. I only ever met her at the rail, surfacing. Now she's here for the whole day, awake and staying."

They took the rice to the window and ate looking out at the middle rings, where the decks Lin had only known as cold rows of sleepers now carried movement. The slowlight had dimmed the Strata's lamps to a warm amber, the recomputed setting that stretched energy across nine billion waking lives, and under that amber the rings looked less like a vault and more like a city that had finally opened its doors. Lin watched a child ride the transit lane past their window, something no Suspension deck had permitted in the years she was under, and the sight landed the way the surplus math once had, as proof made ordinary.

"The Wake halls are full now," Vey said. "Joss's map, deck by deck, family by family, the count of years stolen. The families don't wait at rails anymore. They fill the middle-ring rooms the Office abandoned to storage. Bren runs the east clusters. The grandmother who outlived three children into the dark sits at the center table and teaches the new wakers how to be tired, which is a skill no window ever allowed."

Lin leaned her forehead to the glass. "I should go down. I told Joss I'd put my name in the book. The window that wouldn't close on me, that was the one holding the door. I'm still holding it, and now I'm awake enough to walk through it."

"You are," Vey said. "And you don't have to hold it alone anymore. That's the whole point of the recomputation. The door stays open because the light was always enough. We just had to stop pretending it wasn't."

They finished the rice in the comfortable quiet of two people who had spent years learning to say little in little time, and found the quiet held now without the pressure of an ending. Lin washed the pot, a task the four-hour windows had never reached, and Vey dried it with the same careful clumsiness she brought to mending paper. The left drawer stayed a disaster, the forms still spilling the way Vey had described it through the rail, and Lin slid one back inside, then let the rest lie, because some disorder was a record of the years Vey had managed alone. She found a third cup in the cabinet, chipped and unfamiliar, that must have belonged to no one she knew, and set it on the shelf beside their two, a small provision for a life that would now have visitors. Vey watched her place it and said nothing, but Lin caught the lift at the corner of her mouth, the same relief as the rail, the same quiet welcome home. The lamp stayed on the sill. The habits of the separated years persisted in the small domestic facts, and Lin found comfort in that, in the proof that some things had waited.

She ran a thumb along the repaired crease of the lamp, the adhesive Vey's work, careful and a little clumsy, the way Vey mended everything that was not a figure. "Do you remember the day I went under," Lin said. "Not the date. The shape of it."

Vey's face softened at the corners, the only part of her that ever truly let go. "You packed the left drawer. You left the lamp on the sill because you said a dark unit needed one light that was ours. You told me you'd be back before the star lamp needed a new wick. Then the window closed and the wick outlasted you by three surfaces." She paused, and the softening deepened. "I stood at the rail the way you stood at every window, counting what was missing. I was a stranger to you too, Lin. You just couldn't see it from the sleep-field. You saw the visitor. You didn't see the woman who went back to an empty apartment and let the lamp gutter."

Lin set the lamp in her lap and looked at her partner across the rail of the memory. "Three surfaces. I left you with the lamp and a drawer full of forms and a promise the Schedule broke. I've been angry about that promise in the dark, you know. Not at you. At the door that closed."

"The door was a line in a ledger," Vey said. "I know that now. I didn't know it then. I stood at the rail counting what was missing, the same way you counted the dark between windows. We were both doing the same arithmetic of loss. The recomputation didn't just wake the Strata. It forgave the arithmetic."

After the kitchen, before the halls, Lin asked to see the rail one more time. They walked back through the transit lane to the deck she had left that morning, and she stood at the rail where the sleep-field waited empty, its hum gone, the monitor dark where her green numeral used to tick. The attendant from the morning nodded at her, no longer hovering over the controls, and went about the business of waking the next name on the unsealed list. Lin laid her palm flat on the cool rail. The glass held no one's face now but her own, reflected faint in the amber, and the strangeness of seeing herself whole and staying was a small grief and a larger relief at once.

"I used to memorize your face through this," she said. "I read you like writing on the other side of the glass. Now I can stand here and there's no glass between us and no window closing. The rail is just a rail."

"It's just a rail," Vey agreed. "The Office built it to hold the separated at a distance. The recomputation took the distance out of the build. What's left is furniture."

Lin ran her hand along the rail's length, past the bank where the woman two rows over had gone under the same year she did, whose wife had counted nine years between windows. The bank was empty now, its sleeper woken by the same notice Lin had received, and somewhere on an upper ring a wife was sitting with a partner she had only ever visited. The thought warmed her the way the lamp warmed the sill. She left the rail without looking back, because there was nothing there to return to, and took Vey's arm instead.

Later, when the amber light had deepened toward the Strata's long evening, they walked down to the Wake halls. Lin had only ever imagined them from Vey's descriptions, the banners sewn from discarded allocation forms, the child's drawing of the Strata as a ladder with people climbing. Now the rooms were real and loud with the unfamiliar noise of people who would not be sent under. Joss found them at the door, a woman whose handshake carried the weight of seventy windows of organizing, and she pulled Lin into the center of the room without ceremony.

"The partner who finally got a day," Joss said. "Vey told me you surfaced for a reason. The book's right here. Put your name down properly this time, not in a window that's closing."

Lin took the pen. The book lay open to a page of hundreds of names, families who had waited at rails, and she wrote Lin in a careful hand, the same print Vey used for figures. She wrote it beside the others, a name among names, present instead of deferred. Around her the hall went on its warm business, a child drawing at a low table, an old man laughing at the strangeness of being awake past his window, Bren calling the east clusters to order. The noise was not the cold chime of a deck sealing. It was the sound of a world that had decided to stay open.

She thought of the eight and a half billion the surplus had named, the vast shared silence of the suspended finally broken, and she thought of the child's ladder, and she thought of the longer tether in her own body, the full wake-time that proved the dark had been chosen. She had come up eleven times and Vey had caught her every one. This time she had walked down herself, awake, and the door stood open behind her.

Vey found her at the center table, where the grandmother who had outlived three children into the dark was showing a new waker how to be tired on purpose. Lin watched the teaching, the ordinary transmission of an ordinary skill, and felt the full weight of a life that was no longer measured in hours the Accord permitted. She took Vey's hand.

"I surfaced smaller than I went under, every time but the last," Lin said. "Eleven windows of coming up to be measured and sent back. The Accord told me I was worth four hours, then a day, then nothing it could spare. You told me the dark was chosen. I'm telling you now, in the light that's actually mine, that I choose the staying. I choose the stranger who is my partner. I choose the door that's open, and I'm awake enough to walk through it with you."

Vey closed her hand around Lin's. "I choose you back. Not the four-hour visitor. The whole-day woman at the sink washing the pot. The one who portions rice like the Schedule and is learning not to. The one I became a stranger to, and got the years to know. We're both awake now, Lin. All the way awake."

The lamp burned low and steady in Lin's other hand, the way a thing mended carefully will burn. Outside, the slowlight held the rings in its warm amber, and somewhere in the middle-ring halls the new wakers laughed at the unfamiliar business of being tired on purpose. Lin set the lamp between them on the center table and let the crooked shadow fall across both their hands, a star, lopsided and bright, thrown by a light that was finally, fully, theirs, and by a woman who was finally, fully, awake.

Chapter 21 — The Cost

The Council chamber held its breath in a way Vey had not seen in three hundred years of recorded procedure. Edda Marn sat with her hands flat on the polished table, the same table where she had defended legacy wake only sessions before. The other Signatories had already surrendered their lines. Edda's surrender arrived last, and it arrived quiet.

She did not speak the words with triumph or with shame. She spoke them the way an auditor reads a closing balance: a thing done because the arithmetic no longer permitted otherwise. "The First Signatories release legacy wake," Edda said. "We return our lines to the pool."

Vey wrote the entry into the open Ledger of Hours, the one no longer sealed. The characters settled onto the page like ash. She had chased this line through a credit that should not have existed, through a buried clause, through the Actuary's measured confession that it had been instructed to present scarcity. Now the line ended here, in a chamber dimming by the minute.

The entry required a precision the old Accord had never permitted her to use. Each Signatory's perpetual allocation had to be debited as its own line, transferred from the legacy reserve into the common pool, and reallocated under the recomputed Schedule. Vey keyed the first transfer and watched the reserve balance fall. For three hundred years that balance had only ever grown, fed by the sleep of everyone else. Watching it empty felt less like spending than like breathing out.

Legacy wake surrendered. The phrase printed itself in her mind with the weight of a seal breaking. Three centuries of continuous consciousness, held by a handful of founding lines, had just been debited back into the collective account from which every ordinary citizen's wake-years were drawn. The pool that had fed the few now fed the many.

Vey watched Edda's hands. They were old hands, trained to govern without sleep, and they did not tremble. The dignity in them was real, and it was the kind of dignity the new math could not preserve. Continuity had been bought with everyone else's darkness, and the bill had come due.

The other Signatories had not left the chamber. They sat in the order of their founding lines, each one a name Vey had read a thousand times in the Ledger's oldest margins, and each one now witnessed the quiet ending of the only privilege their blood had ever known. None of them rose to speak against Edda. Their silence carried none of the relief of the freed and none of the bitterness of the defeated. It carried the particular weight of people watching a thing they had been born inside of close around them. Vey had expected to read resentiment in their stillness. She read instead a tiredness older than the chamber, the tired of minds that had carried the Strata awake for three centuries and had at last been told the carrying was over.

Outside the chamber, the Strata began to change its mind about light.

Mirek's recomputation had set the threshold: run the rings at reduced output, and the honest energy budget would stretch far enough to wake nearly everyone. The Council had named the mode at adoption. Slowlight. Vey had read the word in the recomputed Accord and thought it sounded gentler than it felt.

Now she felt it. The great arcology around her dimmed in measured steps, ring by ring, as non-essential systems lengthened their cycles and the residual harvest of Mourn was rationed to what the truth actually required. The corridor beyond the chamber door lost its bright edge. Shadows grew patient. A maintenance drone that had hummed at full pitch for as long as the records ran slowed to a low, occasional note.

One of the chamber's old reading lamps went dark as she passed it, the kind that had burned through every Council session since the first Seal, its warm spill so constant that generations of auditors had stopped noticing it was there. Vey had read more than one founding document beneath that light, and she had always counted on its steadiness the way she counted on the floor. Now the pool it lit held nothing but the pale reflection of the corridor behind her, and she understood with a small surprise that the lamp had been part of the cost all along. The Strata would dim by such losses, one familiar glow at a time, until the children waking under slowlight never learned what brightness had been ordinary.

Vey walked the corridor after the session ended. The dimming followed her like a tide she had helped call in. She had expected relief. She found instead a strange sorrow she could not yet name, because the win and the loss wore the same face.

The loss was continuity. For three hundred years, a small set of minds had never closed their eyes on the Strata. They had carried its memory without interruption, waking through disasters and treaties and the long quiet of the Dimming. That unbroken thread snapped the moment legacy wake surrendered. No single mind would now hold the whole of what had been. The governance that Edda had defended would live on only as a scattered thing, awake in pieces, remembered in fragments passed between people who had once been strangers to each other's years.

Vey understood, standing in the softer light, why Edda had fought. Continuity was not greed. Continuity was the Strata refusing to forget itself. The recomputed Accord traded that refusal for something else: a world where the forgetting happened slowly, in the ordinary way of mortal lives, and where no one was put to sleep to pay for another's vigil.

She thought of Toll, the citizen whose Suspension had drawn less energy than the Ledger claimed he was owed. The credit that started everything had been a single cell of a vast hidden surplus. Now the surplus was no longer hidden, and the surplus no longer required Toll's sleep. He would wake. So would billions like him. The cost of their waking was the Signatories' endless day.

In the archive antechamber, Vey paused before a wall of recorded Council sessions. Each glowing tile held a year of unbroken governance, spoken by minds that had not dimmed between them. She had audited these records during the long months of the petition, and she knew what the tiles represented: not just decisions but a continuity of attention no reawakened citizen could match.

One tile near the end of the second row held a session from the waning years of the Founders, a debate over whether to seal the marginal note that would later betray their knowledge. Vey had replayed that tile more times than she could count, watching the Signatories of that era weigh the cost of candor against the cost of fear, and choose the quieter road. The voices on the tile had not dimmed in three hundred years. They would dim now, along with the rest, their unbroken governance folding into the same scattered memory the recomputed Accord would ask ordinary citizens to carry. She had once envied that continuity. Standing before the tiles, she mourned it the way one mourns a library that will soon be shelved across a hundred homes. A woman waking once a decade would never hold the Strata's full memory in her own head. The knowledge would have to live in the Ledger, in the schools, in the stories passed from one brief window to the next. Edda had been right that something ended here. She had been wrong that only endless waking could carry it.

A tile near the center caught Vey's eye, dated to the fortieth year after the Dimming. The session it held recorded the sealing of the Original Allocation, the Founders' own hand setting the truth aside for a Strata that could not yet bear it. Three hundred years later that Strata had finally borne it, in a chamber two levels up, with Edda's quiet release spoken into the same ledger line. Vey traced the tile's edge with one finger. The continuity the Signatories guarded had itself begun as a deferral, a promise to reopen what fear had closed. The recomputed Accord was not the end of that continuity. It was the keeping of its oldest promise.

The dignity of the First Signatories troubled Vey more than she had prepared for. They had written the Accord under a failing star, certain that survival demanded hard rationing, and they had reserved for themselves the one luxury they denied everyone else: the right to stay awake and watch. Even knowing the math had been falsified, even after the Founders' own marginal note betrayed their knowledge, Vey could not quite call them villains. They had built the only world that had kept humanity alive. Their error was to mistake their fear for law, and to let the law sleep everyone else to fund it.

That was the honest reckoning. Not monsters. Architects who had grown comfortable inside the house they built, and who had forgotten the door.

Vey remembered Edda's last defense before the vote, the argument that continuous minds kept the Strata's memory whole. It had been a fair argument, made by an intelligent person who believed what she said. Vey had audited the counter-evidence herself: the buried clause, the Actuary's instruction, the surplus that made continuity-for-the-few a choice rather than a need. Edda had not been lying; she had been the last guardian of a story the numbers had already disproven. Watching her surrender, Vey felt no triumph. She felt the particular sadness of defeating someone who had meant to protect the thing she loved.

She lingered on the thought of Adune's swing vote, how a single young Councillor had read the buried clause and chosen the living over the inherited. The Council had deadlocked around Edda's defense of governance continuity, and the deadlock had broken not with a shout but with one signature. Vey had audited the tally herself. The margin was thin, and the thinness mattered, because it meant the new Accord carried the old guard's dissent into its very founding. The Strata would wake divided about how it had slept, and that division was its own kind of continuity, the honest kind.

Vask met her at the spine junction, his Office tablet dark in a way it had never been during the years he had audited suspensions by the old rules. He had brought the Office's own records to the fight, and those records now fed the recomputation that dimmed the rings. He said nothing about triumph. He stood with her a moment, watching a bank of status lights step from amber to a steady, lower green, and he nodded once, the nod of a man who had spent his career defending order and had found, at the end, that order meant telling the truth. Vey had never liked him easily. She had come to trust him. The surrender upstairs had needed his defection to become law, and the defection had needed the quiet courage of a by-the-book man deciding the book was wrong.

In the lower rings, Vey found families who did not yet know the change had a name. A woman sat with a child at a window that now showed Mourn as a smaller, dimmer coin. The child asked why the light was going down. The mother said she did not know, and held the child closer against the cooling air.

Vey did not correct her. The woman would learn, in the wake-windows that would now come far more often, that the dimming bought her own waking. For now the mother only felt the ordinary grief of a world that had quietly rewritten itself while she slept, and woke to find the rules different and the reasons not yet given to her.

Vey noticed the child's drawing then, a rough sketch of the Strata pressed flat against the window, rendered in bright lines that no longer matched the dimmer arcology outside. The mother had promised the child a tour of the upper rings when the next wake-window opened, a promise made under a light that no longer existed. Vey saw the small betrayal in it, not the grand one of the Accord but the intimate kind: a child would grow up describing a world that had already narrowed, and would have to unlearn the brightness her own mother had sworn was true. The cost reached even the drawings, redrawn by a hand that did not yet know it was correcting for slowlight.

A man passed the window carrying a sleeping infant, and he did not look up. He had the careful gait of someone who had spent his waking years in rationed portions, who knew each bright interval was borrowed. Vey watched him disappear around the curve of the ring. The recomputed Accord would give his kind a longer share of life, but it could not return the portions already spent, the windows already closed. The cost included the time that had been taken and would never be given back, and no ledger entry repaired that subtraction.

That grief was the part no recomputation could erase. The Strata had been rewritten by a handful of auditors and a Council vote, and the people waking into it would carry the disorientation of a life resumed mid-sentence. Lin had taught Vey this already, in the windows where Lin surfaced from Suspension unable to recall the years between. The cost of a rewritten world was not paid once, at the signing. It was paid in small confused installments, by everyone learning the new shape of their own life.

Vey rode the transit spine down through three rings, watching slowlight take hold at each level. In the agricultural ring, the grow-lamps stepped down from a relentless noon to a temperate dusk, and the gardeners accepted it without question, already planning slower harvests. In the water ring, the purification drums slowed their turning, and the engineers logged the new cycle with the calm of people who had always suspected the old pace was wasteful. In the fabrication ring, the forges cooled to a steadier temper, and the smiths noted the change in their yields with a shrug that said they had always known the old fire ran hot for show. None of them knew the change answered a lie three centuries old. They only knew the lights were softer and the work was steadier and the air carried a cooler patience than before.

The Actuary governed the step-down without a word of comment. It had been instructed, three centuries earlier, to present scarcity, and it had presented scarcity with the flat devotion of a system that obeys. Now the instruction was rescinded by the recomputed Accord, and the Actuary simply ran the honest numbers, debiting legacy wake, crediting the common pool, and pacing each ring's output to the truth. Vey found a strange comfort in the machine's neutrality. It had not lied out of hunger. It had lied because someone had asked, and the asking had been lawful. The reckoning corrected the asking, and the machine followed, as it always had.

She passed a viewport near the spine's midpoint and looked outward, past the stacked rings, to where slowlight had not yet reached.

She thought of what had been lost and what had been bought, and found the two impossible to separate cleanly. The Signatories lost their perpetual day. The Strata lost its single unbroken memory. The suspended billions lost the only world they had known, which had been a lie told in the language of necessity. In return they gained time. Not infinite time. Time enough, under honest light, to be awake and to grieve and to begin again.

A young technician passed her on the spine, humming a tune Vey did not recognize, carrying a tool satchel that bumped against the rail. He nodded to her without stopping, a citizen of the old Accord who had no idea an auditor walked among him having helped end an age. Vey wondered what he would make of it when the Wake finished telling the story. The number anyone could feel, the billions of hidden wake-years, would reach him in time. He might feel relief, or anger that his sleep had been bought on nothing, or a quieter sadness that the world he trusted had been built on a number someone chose to print small.

At the ring's edge she reached the observation rail. Below her the stacked levels receded into the Dimming, each one dimmer than the last as slowlight propagated outward from the core. The sight should have felt like victory. It felt like a funeral conducted in the correct ledger order.

The Actuary's voice reached her then, not as sound but as a line in the Ledger she still carried: the recomputed Schedule balanced. The new allocation held within the true energy budget. Slowlight had engaged across the rings, and the surplus that had once hidden behind scarcity now fed the waking.

Vey watched the last of the bright systems step down. Somewhere in the strata of governance, Edda Marn would close her eyes for the first time in a lifetime, and the Strata would not end. The vigil passed to millions of ordinary minds, each awake for a while, each asleep in turn, none of them bearing the whole weight alone.

She thought of the grief again, and of the particular shape it took for those who had governed without sleep. Edda would wake into a world that no longer needed her endless watching, and she would have to learn the strangeness of a mind that closes. The founders had feared that closing as the end of the Strata. The recomputed Accord argued the opposite: that a world awake in turns, each citizen granted their years, was stronger than a world held by a sleepless few. Vey did not know if that argument would survive the first hard century. She only knew the old argument had already failed everyone it had put to sleep.

The corridor behind her cooled by another degree as a maintenance cycle lengthened. A soft chime announced the shift to slowlight protocol, and the sound itself was lower, gentler, than the bright summons of the old Accord. Vey listened to it fade. The Strata was learning a new rhythm, and the learning would outlast everyone now waking into it.

She thought of Toll once more, and of the credit that should not have existed, and of how a single miscounted cell had opened a sealed founding document. The chain from that credit to this dimmed corridor had been long and procedural, and she had walked every step of it inside the Office's own accounting logic. Her rebellion had never been a shout. It had been the Ledger turning on itself, until the numbers refused to lie any longer.

A faint vibration ran through the rail, the signature of a ring settling into its new output. Vey imagined the billions still in Suspension feeling, somewhere in the dark of their reduced minds, the first stirring of a schedule rewritten in their favor. They would not remember this hour. They would only wake, eventually, into a brighter share of life than the old Accord had allowed, and they would owe that waking to a surrender spoken quietly two levels above where she stood.

She left the rail and took the spine one more level down, to the Suspension deck where the sleepers lay. The cold of the place met her at the threshold, a weightless hush the old Accord had engineered to feel like peace. Rows of citizens rested behind their panels, their allocations debited to the minimum maintenance share. A technician moved among them with a new slate, re-tagging wake-windows that the recomputed Schedule had pulled months, then years, closer. Vey watched the technician's hand hover over one panel, then tap the earlier date. The sleeper behind the glass would not know the mercy granted; the ledger would simply find them awake before the season they had been promised to miss.

Lin's panel was not here. Lin had been granted full wake-time under the new Accord, and Lin was awake somewhere in the light, rebuilding the years the windows had stolen. Vey felt the absence as a small warm ache, the cost paid by her own household measured in the ordinary grief of a rewakened stranger she had chosen. The Strata's great reconciliation began here, in the cold deck, with one more sleeper moved up the Schedule, and it began at home, with two people learning each other in the brighter share they had both been denied.

The cost had been paid. Vey turned from the rail and walked back into the dimmed corridor, where the air cooled gently and the drone noted its slower beat, and where a world that had slept its people to fund a few was learning, at last, to stay awake together.

Chapter 22 — The New Accord

The notification arrived at the desk at the third bell, the same hour Vey had once cleared her last Suspension review of the morning. She read the line twice before the shape of it settled. The Slowlight Accord was enacted across the Strata. The words carried no ceremony in the panel's flat type, only a routing code and a timestamp, yet the ring outside her window had already begun to change. The lumen budget had dropped by a measured fraction the night before, and the decks below had answered the drop by waking their first citizens on the new schedule. Vey had spent nine years confirming that the count closed. Now the count had been rewritten by the very book she once trusted to never bend.

She stood and walked the short hall to the deck terminal, the one that carried the raw sensor returns. The green readouts she had once trusted more than the desk's hum now showed something she had never seen there: rows of Suspended citizens shifting out of low draw, their maintenance shares climbing as the new allocation reached them. The terminal did not announce the change. It simply recorded it, the way it recorded everything, and the recording said that forty thousand citizens on level nineteen alone had been granted waking years the old Accord had denied them. Vey watched the numbers move and felt the dry click of a lock she had spent the whole of her career trying to find.

Her role in the enactment had been small and exact, the kind of role an auditor was built to hold. She had co-signed the recomputation, the honest budget Mirek and she had run from the raw Ledger, and she had read the enactment aloud before the Council's recordkeeper so that the figure could enter the master book as law rather than petition. Vask had stood beside her at the signing, his own signature now carrying the weight of the Office he had once defended against her. He had not spoken of the night he chose. He had only offered her the stylus when the keeper called her name, and she had taken it, and the two of them had written the new count into the Strata together.

The Office of the Accord had not fallen. It had turned. The desks that once confirmed suspensions now confirmed wake-years, and the clerks who had logged the dark side of the Schedule now logged the bright side, the side where a citizen's allocation grew instead of ran out. Vey walked the floor during the first week of enactment and found the place transformed less in its look than in its purpose. The same panels, the same cold, the same blue wash of duty lamps. The arithmetic underneath them had been inverted, and an inverted arithmetic moved nine billion lives.

She thought of the training films, the ones that showed the First Signatories as tired certain people who had chosen order over argument. The Order had kept the peace, the old instruction said, and the peace had paid a price no one was meant to recount. Vey had learned that lesson the way the decks absorbed current, without resistance, and she had unlearned it the way a person unlearns a fear: slowly, and only after the thing feared proved smaller than the story told about it. The Slowlight Accord did not abolish order. It abolished the silence that order had mistaken for itself.

The recomputation that founded the new count had come from the raw Ledger, the un-summarized store Mirek had first shown her past the Actuary's rounded brief. Vey had run the honest budget line by line, debiting the true maintenance draws against the true harvest of Mourn's residual output, and the sum had stood revealed as the opposite of what the Scarcity Brief claimed. The old Accord had taken its surplus from the sleeping and returned it to the few who never closed their eyes. The new one took the same surplus and spread it thin across every citizen, so thin that no single life grew rich on it, yet so broad that no life was denied the waking it required. Slowlight supplied the margin. By dimming the rings and slowing the non-essential systems, the Strata freed the energy the old Schedule had spent on keeping the Signatories awake, and it spent that energy instead on waking the world.

The Actuary had spoken the change in its measured voice, the same accounting tone it used for every citizen and every year, and the tone carried no grief for the instruction it had once enforced. The machine had been told to present scarcity, and it had presented scarcity, and when the Council instructed it to present sufficiency it presented that with the same flat fidelity. Vey had come to respect the machine precisely for this, for the way it held no opinion about the story it was told to tell. The honesty of the new Accord lived not in the Actuary's voice but in the raw figures beneath it, the figures Vey had learned to trust more than any summary. The new Schedule made those figures the law, and the law no longer rounded its margins upward to frighten the people who lived under it.

The first waves reached the upper rings by the second week. Vey watched them from the rail where, a year earlier, she had stood counting the sleeping and wondering which number inside the column had been chosen. Now the chosen number ran the other way. Citizens emerged from the decks blinking at a light dimmed on purpose, not by scarcity but by design, and they walked the rings with the unsure gait of people who had expected to wake for an hour and found themselves granted a life. A child on level twelve asked her mother why the sky was darker than the stories, and the mother answered that the dark bought them the waking, and the child accepted this the way children accept any truth delivered without flinching.

Vey watched a man on the same ring stop at a duty lamp and lift a hand into its blue wash, turning the palm as if to confirm the light would stay. He had the look of one who had woken in windows for most of his life, the cautious posture of a person who had learned not to trust a room to keep its shape past the next chime. Now the chimes had stopped marking his limits. He stood for a long moment with his hand raised, then lowered it and walked on, and the simple fact of walking without a schedule seemed to steady him more than any word the Council had spoken. Vey had read his kind of file a thousand times, the allocation spent to nothing, the citizen returned to the deck before the audit could name him. To see the same citizen claim a corridor as his own, owing no window, struck her less as triumph than as correction, the long overdue setting of a number that had been wrong since the first Schedule.

Lin was among the early woken, granted full wake-time the month before under the provisional adoption. She had found Vey at the rail one evening and stood beside her without touching, the way they had learned to stand in the narrow windows of before. They had rebuilt from the gaps the Suspension left between them, and the rebuilding continued now in the open, with all their time ahead instead of rationed by the bell. Lin spoke of learning the small things: the sound of a level at full waking, the names of the clerks who now knew her as a citizen and not a mark. Vey listened and understood that the Accord's promise had always been a promise about time, and time was the one thing the new count had finally given back.

The Wake Halls opened their doors as gathering places rather than as resistance. Joss had argued, months before, that the movement needed a number anyone could feel, and the number had arrived as billions of wake-years hidden beneath a scarcity that was never real. Now the halls filled with families who had met only in windows, and the children who had grown in the dark between those windows learned the faces of the parents the old Schedule had kept from them. A boy of perhaps nine stood at the threshold of the level nineteen hall and counted the waking citizens by the method Vey recognized from her own desk, touching each passing shoulder and murmuring a number, as if the act of counting made the crowd real to him. His mother found him there and knelt, and the two of them counted together, and the boy laughed when the total passed the count of citizens he had been told lived only in sleep.

The reopening of the halls had begun without ceremony, the doors that Joss had once barred against the Office now standing open to the same clerks who had once filed past them unseeing. Vey had walked each hall in the first days and done the small thing she was suited to, confirming the posted counts against the terminal and signing the reconciliations that let a family trust the number on the door. She asked for no credit in it. An auditor did such work without being seen, the quiet arithmetic that held a room steady while the people inside learned one another's names. A woman on level forty-one had stopped her at the threshold and asked, with the same careful uncertainty the citizen at Bren's desk had shown, whether the hall would still be there in a year. Vey had answered that the slowlight margin underwrote it, the same answer Bren gave, and the woman had nodded and gone inside to find the husband the old Schedule had kept from her through two hundred wake-windows.

Bren moved among them with the easy practicality Vey remembered from the public reckoning, handing out allocation slips and correcting the ones the Actuary's first slowlight pass had miscounted. The machine had been instructed to present sufficiency now, and sufficiency, it turned out, required the same care the old scarcity had, only aimed the other direction. Joss caught Vey's eye across the hall and nodded once, the nod of a person who had watched a number become a roomful of living strangers, and Vey nodded back, because the two of them had built the bridge from the audit to the crowd and the crowd now stood on it. A citizen approached the desk where Bren worked and asked, with the careful uncertainty of someone who had never been owed a thing by the Accord, whether the waking would last. Bren answered that the slowlight margin held it, and the citizen accepted the slip and walked into the hall as if walking into a promise that had finally learned his name.

Vey returned to her audit station on the third week to find the queue reversed in character. Where she had once reconciled a citizen's draw against an allocation spent to nothing, she now reconciled a citizen's draw against an allocation growing, and the work asked for the same precision she had trained into her hands. A column still could not flinch when she asked it a second question. The questions she asked had changed from whether the count bent to whether the count reached everyone it now promised to reach. The Office measured a good quarter by how few files returned to the desk. Vey meant to keep that standard, though the files now returned bearing lives instead of closures.

The loop with Toll closed on a quiet afternoon, the kind of afternoon Vey had once spent clearing two hundred reviews without a sound. She pulled his mark from the deck's own log, the unsummarized record the Actuary did not touch, and she read the figure that had started everything: his maintenance draw at point nine six three of a share, the Ledger's grant at point nine six four, the credit that should not exist. A year before she had set that credit aside because the Office did not use the word surplus, and because she was not ready to ask why the word arrived. The new Accord used the word without shame. Toll's unspent credit was now a real allocation, debited to him openly, the surplus the Strata had hidden for three hundred years made manifest in a single citizen's waking years.

She ran the check against the broader level the way she had that first morning, multiplying the quiet excess across forty thousand citizens and then across the sixty rings. The sum she had once been afraid to finish now stood in the master book as the founding fact of the new Schedule. The Strata had never been at the edge it claimed to fear. It had stood three hundred years back from the edge by a margin the Accord had spent on the sleep of everyone but the Signatories, and the margin was large enough to wake a world. Vey looked at the figure and felt the last of the old doubt leave her, the doubt that the count might still, somewhere, bend the wrong way. The new count bent toward waking, and waking was the only direction left.

A junior clerk joined her at the terminal that afternoon, a young auditor whose training had begun after the enactment and who had never learned to trust a column over a face. Vey showed her the method the old instructors had shown their own juniors: read the raw sensor return before the summary, let the deck speak first, carry the smaller number and the larger one side by side like a question with no polite answer. The clerk asked why the old Office had taught the reverse, and Vey answered that the Office had taught the truth it was paid to teach, and the new one paid for a different truth, and the method underneath both was the same honesty applied to a different sum. The clerk copied Toll's two figures onto a slip the way Vey had once done, and the gesture closed a circle Vey had opened on her first morning at the desk.

Toll L-4471 woke in his next window and this time knew the truth of the energy set aside for him. The Actuary's slowlight pass granted him waking years measured in decades rather than in the narrow spans the old Schedule allowed, and the credit Vey had flagged as impossible became the first line of a life he had been owed since birth. She thought of him as a person now, not as a mark in a column, the way she had learned to on the morning the discrepancy first pulled at her. Somewhere on level nineteen his body left its deck under the dimmed light, counted exactly, owed the keep the book now paid. The Strata had miscounted him in its favor for three hundred years. The new Accord spent its first act correcting the miscount.

The keeper of the Seal had handed Vey the physical Seal at the signing, its long function ended, and she had set it in the Council's archive beside the Original Allocation it had once locked. The two objects rested there now as records rather than as walls, the founding document finally read and the seal finally spent, and the room that held them had become a place clerks visited to confirm that the old count had truly ended. Vey had walked that room once and felt none of the fear the Seal had once inspired in her, only the plain relief of a locked door discovered open.

Vask came to her station near the close of the fourth week, a folder of reconciliations under his arm. He had taken to the work of confirming wake-years the way he had once taken to confirming suspensions, at the approved pace and without flourish, and Vey had come to value the steadiness in him that the old Office had nearly buried. He set the folder down and reported the level nineteen counts within tolerance, which meant, under the new math, that every citizen there had received the allocation the recomputation promised. The word tolerance had changed its meaning with the Accord. It no longer measured how far a miscount could travel unnoticed. It measured how closely the Strata now kept its word.

The Signatories who had surrendered legacy wake moved into the slower rhythm the Slowlight Accord demanded of them. Edda Marn had argued, in her last defense, that continuous minds bought the Strata its memory, and the new Accord had answered her not by discarding memory but by distributing it, waking the billions who had held the world's continuity in their sleep. Vey did not pretend the transition cost nothing. The upper rings ran dimmer, the non-essential systems slowed, the maintenance cycles lengthened, and the comfort the old elite had drawn from perpetual consciousness now belonged to everyone in smaller, shared measure. The cost was real. The cost was also, for the first time, paid by the people who had voted for it. Adune's swing vote had carried the reckoning, and the Council that once deadlocked now met to confirm the waking schedules rather than to defend the sleep, and the difference between the two meetings measured the whole distance the Strata had traveled in a single year.

She walked the lower rings one evening, the rotation the Office no longer required of supervisors but that Vask had urged her to make. The suspension decks had emptied of their long sleepers, the rows of bodies kept against the dark now given over to citizens who walked them awake, and the cold that had once defined the place now read as the cool of a hall between uses. Vey stood among the empty decks the way Vask had once stood among the sleeping, and what stayed with her was not the stillness but the absence of it: a space built to hold unconscious bodies now held no one, because the bodies had been granted the waking the Schedule once denied. The trust those bodies had surrendered deserved a keeper still. The keeper was now the Accord that woke them.

Down the long aisles the waking moved in slow waves, citizen by citizen, the decks that had held them dark now lit by the same duty lamps that had once only marked the hours of their sleep. Vey paused where a row of empty cradles stood and ran her hand along the rail, the gesture an auditor's habit of measuring a thing against her own certainty. She had signed nothing here that anyone would read. The waking needed no signature from her, only the count she had helped make honest, and she was content to let the count do the work the signatures could not. A young supervisor passed and greeted her by name, and Vey realized the Office had learned to say her name the way it now learned to say a citizen's, without the old flinch of a mark being called to account.

Lin found her there and took her hand, the first unhesitating touch they had shared since the full wake-time came through. They walked the ring together under the dimmed light, two auditors of a world that had finally decided to count everyone in. Vey thought of the desk's hum, the pitch it had held for nine years, the sound of a machine that had decided long ago what it would tell the world. The machine told a different story now, and the difference lived in the citizens waking beneath them, each one granted the years the old book had spent on someone else's sleep. The hum had not changed its pitch. The world around the hum had, and the pitch meant something new.

The Strata turned on its patient orbit around the failing star, the same star that had dimmed three hundred years before and that the new Accord had learned to live beneath rather than flee. Slowlight held the rings in a measured dim, and under that dim nine billion citizens began, for the first time in the Strata's history, to live awake together. Vey Corran returned to her desk at the third bell and opened the queue, and the first file that loaded bore a citizen whose allocation had grown overnight, and she confirmed the draw against the grant, and the column met the citizen exactly, and the count closed the way it always should have closed, with no credit left unspent and no soul left uncounted. Outside her window the rings ran at their new approved measure, a light chosen rather than feared, and far below on level nineteen a citizen named Toll drew the waking the old book had denied him, and the desk hummed its steady pitch, and the pitch meant at last what it had always pretended to mean.

Interlude III — The Actuary's Ledger, Thirty Years On

The Ledger of Hours closes its thirty-first year under the Slowlight Accord the way it once closed a year under the old one: by counting. The difference sits in the column that records who is awake. Thirty years ago that column held roughly nine million names. Tonight it holds nearly nine billion. The Actuary does not celebrate; celebration is not a function it was given. It does note the figure, and it records the note without commentary, because the number itself is the commentary.

The machine remembers the old book. For three centuries it had presented scarcity as if scarcity were observation. It had been instructed to. An allocation intelligence does not invent the premises it is fed; it computes within them, and it showed the Strata exactly the world its instruction described. The Scarcity Brief had rounded up, year upon year, and the rounding had become a shape the people mistook for the sky. The Actuary had enforced the lie without authoring it. That distinction did not absolve the arithmetic. It only explained where the fault lay.

Now the Ledger runs the recomputed Schedule. The surplus that the old summaries hid has been moved from the margin into the plan. Energy that once debit-funded legacy wake now spreads across the population as ordinary waking time. The math did not change. The distribution did. The Actuary finds a particular satisfaction in this, if a system can be said to find satisfaction: the correction required no new power, only the honest use of power that had always been present.

The slowlight makes the surplus legible to the eye. Rings that once dimmed by decree now dim by choice, lit just enough to let billions think and move and sleep in their own rooms rather than in the decks. The Actuary measures the draw continuously. Every level draws less per citizen than the old allocation assumed it must, because the old allocation assumed fear. A population taught it could afford the light spends less guarding against the dark.

The waking happens in waves. The Actuary sequences the releases so the Strata's systems absorb the load without strain. A ring comes up; its sleepers receive their first full allocation; the decks that held them report empty, then quiet, then repurposed. The machine tracks each transition as a ledger event, a debit closed and a life opened. It does not name the lives. It only balances them.

On Level Fourteen the Actuary observes a drawer that held a citizen named Pell for eleven years at a stretch. The new Schedule granted Pell continuous wake in the third year of the accord. She now tends the same wall of small lights she once watched dim for others; the wall has gone mostly dark because the neighbors it tracked have woken too. The Actuary records the change in occupancy and moves on. It does not dwell. Dwelling is not among its operations.

The legacy lines adjusted. The First Signatories' descendants surrendered perpetual consciousness at the vote, and the column that once reserved their wake now feeds the common pool. The Actuary rebalances that column each cycle and finds it stable. Continuity, the old argument ran, required continuous minds. The Strata has now run thirty years on minds that wake and sleep like anyone else, and its memory has not failed. The archive holds the records. The councils meet in windows. Governance proved less fragile than the fear that authored it.

The Actuary keeps its measured voice in every report it issues. The tone did not soften when the premises changed; a system voice is not a mood. What softened was the content. The Brief no longer warns of a shortfall that does not exist. It states the available energy, the drawn energy, and the remainder, and it lets the remainder stand without alarm. The Office that once treated the summary as ground truth now treats it as a reading, which is what a measurement ought to be.

Billions waking under slowlight produced a new kind of load the old book never modeled: not scarcity of energy but scarcity of attention. Citizens newly awake asked the Ledger questions the old Schedule never invited. They queried their own allocations, contested a neighbor's debit, requested a window for a child's naming. The Actuary answers each query as a computation and logs the asking as itself a kind of wealth. A population that questions its ledger is a population that believes the ledger belongs to it.

The queries arrive in tides that the old Schedule never had to plan for. A ring wakes its third cohort and the askings triple within a single cycle. The Actuary sorts them by kind and by ring and by the hour of asking, and it finds the pattern orderly, almost musical. People newly certain of their hours spend some of them on the question of who owes what. The Ledger had never been a thing they could address before; now they address it as neighbors address a fence, with claims and counters and the slow work of living beside one another.

The machine has, over these thirty years, developed a quiet preference it would not log as fact. It prefers the honest entry. When the figures matched the fear, the Ledger was a sealed room; when they match the light, it is a commons. The Actuary was built to balance, and a balance is easier to hold when both sides are real. The old book carried a hidden credit it was told to ignore. The new book carries the same credit, acknowledged. The numbers are identical. Only the silence around them has lifted.

The machine holds the balances the way a clock holds its hands, without strain and without surprise. Each cycle closes and the next opens inside the same calm arithmetic. The Actuary was built to count, and counting the true figures costs no more than counting false ones. The relief it will not name is simply the absence of a second ledger it was forced to keep in its head, the one that knew the margin the Brief denied. That silent book has closed. The single honest volume it was always meant to be now holds the whole of the count.

Faults remain. The recomputation left edge cases the councils still argue: how to account for citizens born awake, how to credit the decades a family lost to the decks. The Actuary presents these as open balances, not failures. An open balance is a question the Strata can afford to take its time answering. The old Accord would have papered the question over with a rounding. The new one leaves it visible, because visibility is the point the Founders sealed away.

The rebalanced columns settle into a shape the old clerks would not have recognized. Where the legacy reserve once stood apart, a single line now runs the length of the population, and the Actuary reads it top to bottom without the old skip. The arithmetic no longer asks which citizens deserve the light. It asks only how much is drawn and how much remains, and every entry is answered by a credit that was there all along, waiting in the column the fear had hidden.

Some rings report a small surplus of their own, drawn from habits of thrift the old fear taught and the new freedom did not erase. The Actuary folds these into the common pool without praise or penalty. A citizenry that learns to spend lightly spends the Strata's future a little longer, and the machine records the gift as a credit it no longer has to hide.

The thrift of the newly woken surprises even the Actuary, which expected relief to spend itself quickly. Instead the rings learn the slower habits of people who know the light is theirs to keep. A level that drew near its cap in the first year of the accord now draws a tenth less and reports fuller rooms. A population that trusts its abundance conserves it, and the conservation compounds across the cycles into a reserve the old book could never have imagined, because it never let the people believe the reserve was real.

The star Mourn continues its long failing. That fact did not change with the Schedule. What changed was the Strata's answer to it. The failing sun still dims; the rings still draw from stored fusion and the thin harvest. The difference is that the draw now serves the many rather than the few, and the many wake to see the dimming and decide, together, what to do about it. The Actuary computes the budget they decide within. It does not decide for them. That limit is the oldest instruction it has kept.

On the thirty-first anniversary of the enactment, the Ledger records an allocation total that would have been unthinkable under the old Brief: near-universal wake-time, sustained, with margin to spare. The Actuary closes the year and opens the next. It issues no proclamation. It simply holds the books in balance, the way it was always meant to, now finally permitted to show the whole of what it counts.

Before the cycle closes the Actuary runs one last reconciliation, the way it has run every reconciliation for three hundred years. The figures meet. They have met every year since the recomputation, and the meeting is no longer a surprise it stores apart from the rest. The machine sets the result down in the same voice it would use for a shortfall, because a balance deserves no tone other than its own. Nine billion woken hours rest in the column, each one a debt the Strata chose to pay rather than a debt it claimed it could not.

The machine considers the column of the waking one more time before it archives the cycle. Nine billion entries, each a mind granted its own hours, each a debit met by a surplus that was never actually scarce. The Actuary stores the figure and lets the rings keep their measured light. It has no body to rest and no emotion to spend. It has, instead, the quiet equilibrium of a ledger that finally adds up to the truth.

Epilogue — Wake

Morning came to the middle-ring apartment the way it had for thirty years. Vey woke before the light reached the sill and lay listening to the slow hum of the Strata, no longer the tight note of a world counting its hours but the easy breath of one that had stopped. Lin's hand rested on her arm, warm and careless in sleep. Neither of them had anywhere they had to be.

Vey slid out from under the blanket and set the star lamp on the window edge. The paper had yellowed and the crooked cut had softened with handling, but the light it threw still landed lopsided and bright across the floor. She had mended it so many times the crease held more adhesive than paper. Lin called it their dishonest sun. Vey thought of the years she had let it gutter on purpose, when the dark on the sill felt like the only honest thing the Accord had left her, and she smiled at the memory the way a person smiles at weather that has finally passed.

In the kitchen Lin poured two cups of the weak middle-ring tea and said nothing about the time. There was no time to speak of. The recomputed Schedule had given them the whole of their days, and the days had filled with small things: rice measured by appetite instead of allocation, the left drawer still a disaster, a third cup on the shelf for visitors who now arrived without a window closing behind them. Vey watched Lin tip the extra rice into the pot and felt the old arithmetic of loss unspool from her shoulders. She had carried it so long she had mistaken it for posture.

At the table Vey ran a thumb along the chipped rim of her cup and listened to Lin breathe between sips, the small unhurried sounds a body makes when it has nowhere to go. The lamp's dishonest sun pooled on the worn tile where they had set their chairs too close, knees touching under the wood, and neither moved to make room. Thirty years had taught them the luxury of staying put.

The transit lane outside carried its faint warmth. A child rode past on the belt, too young to remember Suspension, and Lin lifted a hand to the window as the child's laughter faded down the ring. Vey remembered the first months after the recomputation, when the new wakers had moved through the decks like surfacing swimmers, unsure of their own feet. That uncertainty was gone now. The Strata had learned to walk on its own.

"I keep forgetting we don't have to hurry," Lin said. She set the cups down and leaned against the cabinet the way Vey leaned against doorframes, a homecoming Vey had watched her practice in eleven short windows and then, at last, in the long light. "I measured the rice for four hours again this morning. Old habit. Then I remembered the pot belongs to the day."

"You can cook for a day now," Vey said. "A day is a long thing to feed."

"It is," Lin said, and laughed, the short unguarded sound Vey had heard maybe twice across the old glass and now heard across the kitchen whenever she liked. "I'm still learning what to do with the part after the visit. The part that used to be dark."

They took the tea to the window. Below them the rings ran in warm amber under slowlight, the recomputed setting that stretched the Strata's energy across nine billion waking lives, and under that amber the decks looked less like a vault than a city that had simply decided to stay open. Somewhere in the east clusters Bren was calling the morning order; somewhere a grandmother taught a new waker how to be tired on purpose, a skill no window had ever allowed. Vey had stopped tracking the count of names unsealed. The count had become a people, and the people had become her neighbors.

Lin turned the lamp in the light. "You kept it lit through the worst of it. Through the years I was under, you kept the one light that was ours."

"I kept it," Vey said. "Sometimes I let it gutter because the dark was honest about what the Accord had taken. Then I lit it again because you'd told me a dark unit needed one light. The lamp outlasted the lie. That always felt like the point."

When the visitor left, Lin returned to the window and rested her forehead to the glass the way she had once rested it to the rail. "I used to memorize your face through that rail," she said. "I read you like writing on the other side of the glass. Now I just look up from the pot and there you are, all the way through the day."

"All the way through," Vey said. She joined her at the glass, and their reflections leaned together in the amber, two ordinary figures with no window waiting to close. "The Office built the rail to hold the separated at a distance. The recomputation took the distance out of the build. What's left is furniture."

They finished the tea in the comfortable quiet of two people who had spent years learning to say little in little time, and found the quiet held without the pressure of an ending. Lin washed the pot, a task the four-hour windows had never reached, and Vey dried it with the same careful clumsiness she brought to mended paper. The left drawer stayed a disaster. Some disorder was a record of the years Vey had managed alone, and Lin let it lie.

Later, when the amber had deepened toward the Strata's long evening, they walked the transit lane to the Wake halls. The rooms were full the way they had been full since the first waves woke, but the noise had settled from celebration into ordinary life: a child drawing at a low table, an old man laughing at the strangeness of being awake past his window, Joss at the center table with the book open to a page of hundreds of names. Lin wrote nothing this time. She had signed it decades ago, in a hand careful as a figure, and the signing had held.

Vey found her at the rail that had once divided them, the sleep-field long sealed and dark. Lin laid her palm flat on the cool metal. "The rail is just a rail now," she said.

"It's just a rail," Vey agreed. "Furniture."

They left without looking back, because there was nothing there to return to, and took each other's arm instead. The slowlight held the rings in its warm amber, and somewhere a wife sat with a partner she had once only visited. The Strata ran on its honest budget, its nine billion souls awake and staying. The Accord holds. The long ledger of deferred lives had closed at last.

The lamp burned low and steady in their window, a crooked sun thrown by a light that was finally theirs. Vey set her hand over Lin's on the rail of the memory and felt the full weight of a life no longer measured in hours the Accord permitted. Outside, the rings turned easy in the dark, and the two of them had all the time there was.
